#7087. AI Ambitions Are Running Into a Network Infrastructure Trust Problem

Enterprise ambitions around AI are running into a fundamental problem: Orgnanizations aren't always confident in the networks those projects depend on, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

The research found that concerns about network providers have caused organizations to rethink strategic initiatives over the past two years. Among respondents whose initiatives were affected, AI and data-driven projects were the most cited, at 49%. That put AI ahead of security and compliance programs at 44%, digital transformation at 42%, and cloud migration at 38%.

The findings come from Arelion's report, "The Trust Illusion: Why AI and Digital Transformation Need More Than a Reliable Network," based on a survey of 518 senior enterprise network decision-makers in the U.S., U.K., Germany, and France conducted by Savanta.

All respondents worked for organizations with more than 2,000 employees and influenced network strategy across data centers, cloud, and connectivity. Of those surveyed, 66% had final sign-off responsibility. The results suggest that as organizations pursue increasingly data-intensive AI projects, confidence in the network infrastructure supporting them could become another deployment obstacle.

On the surface, Arelion found little evidence of a trust crisis. A total of 93% of respondents said they largely or completely trust their current network provider, while 83% said that trust has strengthened over time.

The picture changes when respondents are asked about a serious network incident. Only 15% said they have complete confidence in their provider's ability to quickly detect and address such a problem. Another 42% said their provider seriously lets them down anywhere from a few times a year to monthly or more frequently.

Those concerns have consequences beyond the network itself. Responding organizations have delayed, scaled back, or stopped strategic initiatives because of provider trust concerns, while others have added resiliency or risk-mitigation measures.

AI and data projects were the most affected among respondents reporting an impact, as organizations try to move AI from experimentation into larger production deployments.

A serious provider failure could also have substantial consequences. Forty percent of respondents said such an event would cause major operational disruption, while 18% anticipated significant financial loss or severe financial or reputational damage.

Another tension runs through Arelion's findings. Enterprises need networks they can trust to support AI projects, but many of the same decision-makers are concerned about AI's growing role inside those networks. A total of 54% of respondents identified AI and automation in network management and security as the factors most likely to negatively affect network trust over the next three years. That ranked ahead of data sovereignty expectations and geopolitical instability, both at 39%, and regulatory requirements and quantum computing threats to current encryption standards, both at 38%.

At the same time, respondents aren't rejecting AI as a network technology. Proactive AI-driven fault prediction was among the capabilities they said would matter most to future trust, alongside stronger built-in security, greater accountability, and responsive support. That leaves enterprises in an unusual position: AI is increasing their dependence on digital infrastructure while also raising questions about how that infrastructure itself is managed.

Arelion has an interest in emphasizing the importance of network-provider trust, and the survey reflects the views specifically of network decision-makers at large enterprises. Still, the findings point to an infrastructure consideration that can get lost amid the rush to adopt new AI models and agents. AI projects ultimately depend on the networks, cloud environments, data centers, and connectivity beneath them. If organizations lack confidence in that foundation, access to better AI models alone won't remove the obstacle.

"Trust is influencing which strategic initiatives move forward, how organizations manage risk, and which providers they choose to work with," said Mattias Fridström, Vice President at Arelion. "As businesses become increasingly reliant on digital infrastructure, enterprises are looking for network providers that can demonstrate transparency, accountability, and operational excellence, not just availability."

The full report is available for download here at the Arelion site (registration required)

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

Cloud Security Alliance's 2026 Top Threats report ranks identity as the leading cloud security concern, followed by two new AI-related risks.

#7086. Martha Stewart’s Halloween Costume Controversy Continues After Several Apologies

Even after multiple apologies, the controversy is still following her.

Halloween has come and gone, but Martha Stewart is still facing the horrors. The famous homemaker has issued several apologies after her controversial costume raised eyebrows.

In case you somehow missed her since-deleted post, Martha seemingly dressed as an unhoused person for the holiday. She wore blue jeans, a torn-up flannel and a cardigan, and a beanie with fake dirt on her face as she held up a handwritten sign that read, “I am hungry. Help me!!”

People were equally outraged and confused by her costume choice, questioning the decision altogether.

“How do you even think this is a good halloween costume in the first place…” one person wrote, while another added, “Rich celebrities mocking poor people and their struggles is so dystopian...this is disgusting.”

Others called Martha out for being “out of touch” and “detached from reality,” as a recent report from the U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development reveals that homelessness has increased by 27 percent between 2013 and 2025.

Upon receiving backlash for the costume, the 85-year-old multi-hyphenate released a statement to Page Six after deleting her photo.

“I am distressed that my Halloween costume yesterday, which was intended to bring more awareness to the very serious subject of homelessness, has offended people, which was never my intention,” she told the outlet on Sunday, November 1.

“Homelessness continues to remain one of America’s greatest challenges and ongoing crises, something that I care deeply about and I have spent a significant amount of time and effort supporting charities that combat this ongoing social epidemic,” she continued, adding that she is “committed to continuing to make a difference.”

If you thought the drama ended there, spoiler alert: It didn’t. After Just Jared posted on Instagram about Martha’s costume, she dropped a comment, writing, “My costume was designed to bring the homeless and the hungry to everyone's mind and boy it certainly did!!! But not in the correct way, because I did not explain it and I am so sorry I care and I support causes who care . Do all of you???”

Shortly after she commented on the post, actor, model, and activist Indya Moore shared a lengthy reply explaining why the costume was problematic.

“We all make mistakes. I certainly do. I try my best to correct them, not just with explanations but with more active support around the actual issue I may be contributing to. But this is a really tough one for me,” Indya wrote in part.

“It also seems like you apologizing for not writing a caption and our subsequent failure to understand the outfit—not because you wore it,” she later continued before calling Martha out further and sharing organizations to help the cause.

“If you were serious about poverty, you’d use your platform to back policies that tax the rich, put free food in front of people who are hungry and homeless, and provide free healthcare and education,” Indya added.

After Indya’s comment, Martha shared a series of Halloween-related content, including a “gay Halloween” post referencing a time she handed water to a child in New York City and a wholesome jack-o’-lantern pic. She then issued another Notes App apology for her costume.

“It’s hard to admit when you’ve really screwed up,” she wrote, in part. “My earlier statement didn’t go far enough. I know many people are suffering economically. Wearing a costume like I did was insensitive and not funny. I am very sorry.”

That’s all for now, but we’ll be back with more as we have it. (You know, in case there’s another jump scare in this mess.)

If you or someone you know is unhoused or are at risk of losing housing, call 2-1-1 to connect with local resources and support, including emergency shelter, housing assistance, and other essential services, or visit 211.org to find help in your area. Calls are free and confidential.

#7083. Research: Lack of Specialized Infrastructure Is a Top AI Adoption Challenge

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research Digital Realty. Forty percent of survey respondents cited a lack of specialized infrastructure required by AI as a main challenge to adopting a formal AI strategy, up from 9% in the company's 2024 research.

The Global Data Insights Survey 2026 points to a widening mix of infrastructure models behind enterprise AI. Half of respondents host AI models in private clouds, 86% are pursuing or exploring sovereign AI, 92% tie data-location strategy to their AI plans, and 88% follow a distributed-data approach.

The research is based on an online survey of 2,131 IT decision-makers across 19 countries, and 11 industries, conducted from Nov. 23, 2025, through Feb. 4, 2026.

Organizations also appear to be shifting from AI planning toward execution. The share actively executing AI to achieve operational efficiency or reduce costs rose from 27% in 2024 to 37% this year. Only 3% reported no measurable ROI to date, while 63% expect returns within the next six months to two years.

Specialized infrastructure led the survey's list of AI adoption challenges at 40%, followed by regulatory and personal-data concerns at 35%. Data availability and quality, storage capacity, interconnection, compute resources, and staffing also ranked as significant constraints.

Those requirements are also shaping workload placement. Integration with existing IT or data infrastructure ranked first at 43%, while regulatory compliance and performance were each cited by 37%. Data proximity, sovereignty, sustainability, cost, and intellectual property protection also factored into hosting decisions.

Private cloud features prominently across the report's AI hosting categories, but enterprises are clearly using a mix of private cloud, public cloud, on-premises infrastructure, and colocation. Private cloud accounted for 50% of custom-built AI model hosting and 47% for open source or fine-tuned large language models (LLMs).

Sovereignty is another part of that placement decision. Thirty-two percent consider sovereign AI — in which data and AI models remain within a single national or regulatory jurisdiction — a requirement, while another 54% are exploring it. The findings point toward hybrid environments in which infrastructure location, governance and data control increasingly overlap.

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

#7082. Obsessed With ‘Obsession’ Star Inde Navarrette? Time to Meet Her Boyfriend Nick Aiello

Greetings to everyone who’s still fully obsessed with Obsession (as well as with the film’s star, Inde Navarrette). Inde is totally captivating in her role as Nikki in the indie horror film from YouTuber Curry Barke—and while she may not be an obsessive girlfriend in real life, the scream queen is taken, as evidenced by her SNL hard-launch. So, without further ado: Time to meet her boyfriend, musician Nick Aiello.

It’s unclear how long the pair has been together, since both keep their personal lives pretty under wraps. But it seems like the two have been an item since at least early 2026. For instance: Earlier this spring, both Inde and Nick posted videos of themselves riding motorbikes in Thailand, and a few weeks later, both Inde and Nick included close-up shots of each other as the last slide in their respective photo dumps from Coachella.

Though they didn’t take any red carpet snaps together, Nick even attended the L.A. special screening of Obsession alongside Inde.

The maybe-couple kept the rumor mill turning when they were photographed grabbing late-night tacos together in May, and they’ve been spotted together several times since—though it wasn’t until Inde referred to Nick as her “boyfriend” during her SNL monologue on October 31 that dating rumors were finally confirmed. Cuuuute!

So, who is Nick Aiello, you ask? He’s a singer-songwriter from San Diego who studied musical theater at the University of Michigan and happens to be very good friends with High School Musical: The Musical: The Series star Joshua Bassett. The two even performed together at Belly Up Tavern in San Diego.

Back in April, Josh also posted a photo of himself, Nick, Inde, and a couple of other friends casually hanging in a Goodyear Blimp.

Nick released his first EP, ILO, back in February 2026 and has been performing nonstop ever since. Here’s hoping we see more of him and Inde in the future!!

#7078. Is the Kitchen the New Living Room?

Find inspiration for your next kitchen renovation.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Above: Eleven Madison Park chef Daniel Humm’s loftlike apartment blurs traditional boundaries.

Plenty of living happens in the kitchen. It’s a creative comfort zone for the culinarily inclined, the hub of daily activity for many families, and often the most crowded room at a party.

So why shouldn’t the design reflect that? We looked back at some of our favorite kitchens featured in ELLE Decor, from chef Daniel Humm’s to designer Joy Moyler’s, to share what elevates these spaces beyond elegant function and material beauty into inviting destinations. “Where is the kitchen?” Humm asked. “It’s there, but I don’t want it to be all you see.”

Instead, this kitchen—and the rest that follow—make us want to look twice and stay a while.

Gather ’round. No offense to the ever-popular, undeniably functional kitchen island, which, paired with some counter stools, can be a great spot for grabbing a quick breakfast, tackling homework with a snack in the afternoon, or perching with an evening glass of wine. But certain spaces call out for something different. A deftly chosen table with chic, interesting chairs can fulfill the same duties with a welcome lightness and ease, not to mention a ton more flexibility.

Wave goodbye to the island, and vast new design horizons open up. What type of table will you choose? Square or round? Legs or pedestal base? Wood, stone, metal, or another material? Perhaps one with expansion leaves for holiday gatherings or blowout buffets? Whatever style suits your fancy, add the right chairs and it’s a party.

Choose from, say, India Mahdavi’s colorful seating, a structured option from starchitects Herzog & de Meuron, or a classic collectible by Jean Prouvé. Be sure to make plenty of space for stemware, meandering conversation late into the night. Don’t say we didn’t warn you.

Circle: Aqua table by Fabrice Berrux for Roche Bobois

Turn any dinner party into a roundtable chat with this shapely Roche Bobois design—no guest is a corner outcast.

The more space-efficient cousin of the circle table exudes modern grace, as exemplified by this Scavolini table.

Contemporary and crisply balanced, this Antonio Citterio design for Maxalto can be a perfect fit for certain small spaces.

Classic American craftsmanship, warm cherry wood, and elegant midcentury style define this piece by Stickley

Don’t be afraid to play with pattern and palette when it comes to cabinets. “I’m always looking for new materials and putting them together in an unexpected way,” says Elle Decor A-List designer Laura Gonzalez of a Paris project, below. Go for restrained whimsy with Ikea’s -terra-cotta-color wave pattern fronts or Isla Porter’s crafty cutout design. And don’t neglect the backsplash, which can be a statement-making creative canvas unto itself.

Consider trading the parade of industry-meets-farmhouse pendants for something more sculptural, more fabulous to play against the kitchen’s functional identity. There’s no reason you can’t bask in the amber glow of a Saint-Louis crystal chandelier while frying an egg.

Utility doesn’t have to mean boredom. “The brief was to create a gathering place,” says Elle Decor A-List designer Joy Moyler of her project, above. Form and function stylishly blend in faucets such as one by the Danish brand Vola in a -powder-coated pistachio-green finish or a Dornbracht design that has stone handles. For something sleek and understated, Kohler’s minimalist four-in-one faucet offers a choice: still or sparkling?

Out-of-the-ordinary pulls are a savvy way to add personality.

These are stunning fusions of art and technology. Which style suits your tastes?

A selection of irresistibly idiosyncratic kitchen extras that we are longing to live with.

Sean Kim is the market and editorial assistant at ELLE Decor, where he covers market trends, design, and culture.

#7077. OpenAI Intros Dots, AI Agents that Continue Working Between User Conversations

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

OpenAI is also previewing specialist Dots for organizations, with separate identities and credentials for defined responsibilities. Those enterprise deployments are beginning with focused pilots. The company is working with Microsoft on integration with Agent 365's governance and security controls.

The broader ambition seems to be to make delegation last beyond a chat session. That change could reduce the need to repeatedly explain a project. It also makes supervision a continuing obligation: An instruction that was appropriate yesterday may no longer fit today's circumstances.

Users currently cannot inspect or directly edit individual Dot memories. Deleting a Dot deletes its own context, while files and conversations it created remain separately stored. Plugin permissions are shared across Dots, ChatGPT, ChatGPT Work, and Codex.

For an organization, that creates two related governance questions: what an agent can retrieve now, and what it already knows. Revoking a connection addresses the first without necessarily resolving the second.

OpenAI's safety account distinguishes authorized tasks that continue in the background from unsolicited "proactive research." The latter uses tools restricted to reading permitted sources. Those research tasks cannot directly send messages, change connected application content, or control a browser or desktop. Any subsequent action must pass the usual rules and checks.

A separate system, Auto-review, checks certain planned actions against instructions and safety requirements. OpenAI says the agent cannot modify the controls enforcing those checks.

"Dots can still make mistakes," the company cautions.

OpenAI's Dots system-card appendix acknowledges that persistence and multi-agent systems are not new. Its evaluations examine how continuing work affects authorization boundaries.

In one test involving successive related tasks, moderate scope violations increased from 8.6% to 19.7% when the number of intervening tasks doubled from five to 10. Examples included carrying information between unrelated tasks or editing a shared document beyond the intended scope. The company reported no severe breaches or exfiltration in that evaluation.

These are controlled test results, not measured failure rates in customer deployments. They nevertheless identify a problem that a successful demonstration cannot settle: whether an agent keeps the right boundaries as its workload accumulates.

According to OpenAI's DevDay recap, Dots are available to Pro and Business Premium users in eligible markets. Enterprise, Edu, and Healthcare access is an administrator-enabled beta, off by default.

The operational question is how organizations will keep delegated authority current. A continuing assignment needs a human owner who can revise its scope and decide when to stop it. Otherwise, the convenience of leaving work with an agent can outlast the reason it was authorized.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

#7075. These Nail Artist-Approved November Manicure Ideas Are Perfect for Cozy Season

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

I’m that person who, as soon as November 1 rolls around, is stripping my apartment of all its Halloween decor and going right into cozy season, complete with an episode of Gilmore Girls playing at all times and a warm candle burning on my bedside table. And that means I’m also thrilled to move on from my last spooky set to the November nail ideas I’ve had saved in my camera roll since, probably, August.

I might be biased (because it’s my birth month), but November is my favorite time of year for manicures. From animal prints to autumnal hues, there’s tons of chic fall nail inspiration to choose from. To find the best nail designs trending this November, I also tapped celebrity manicurists Sigourney Nuñez and John Avery for their insights and favorite nail ideas.

From our coats and accessories, down to our manicures, “animal print has been huge this year,” explains Nuñez. “Given the nature of the color palette of the Bambi nail trend, which includes a range of browns, chestnut, and beige nudes, this look will definitely be around for the next month.” You can make the look as subtle or statement-making as you want, whether that means a few delicate spots scattered across a nude base or a full set of Bambi-inspired fur.

“November is like a sweet spot for earthy and cozy colors if you’re not quite ready to commit to the festive season yet,” Nuñez explains. “Think rich espresso shades and deep greens that lean more toward emerald.” For a coffee hue, go for Manucurist’s Chestnut polish, and for some November earthiness, OPI’s Stay Off The Lawn is autumn dew, bottled.

Nothing gets me into the fall spirit like spotting colorful foliage, which happens to be peaking in NYC right now. And these detailed leaf motifs bring that same seasonal beauty to your manicure, with intricate shapes and warm, earthy hues that feel perfectly suited for November. To recreate the look, start with a milky base, then paint on your favorite leaf shapes in a mix of fall-inspired colors.

“A classic I’m loving right now is the French tip, but with a seasonal twist,” says Donna Charloff, director of service operations at nail care brand MiniLuxe. “My favorite way to freshen it up is with a deep or colorful French tip paired with a warm, creamy nude base.” Try a fall-ready hue like chocolate brown, burgundy, or burnt orange. Or why settle for just one color when you can opt for an entire striped French manicure inspired by your favorite cozy sweater?!

“The cat-eye craze is the biggest trend I’m seeing right now,” says Avery. “The magnetic gel creates movement within the color, and it can look like velvet, satin, or even a gemstone depending on the lighting.” The jewel-esque shades are especially gorgeous as the holiday season approaches, and you can do one color on each fingertip, or go for a monochrome set that still boasts some dimension.

“You can’t go wrong with a plaid design,” says Nuñez. “If you use a moody color palette with charcoals, deep purples and jewels tones, the manicure will have a dark academia vibe which feels just right for November.”

A mug of hot cocoa and November are pretty much synonymous, and the same goes for a toasty manicure. And to amp up your classic chocolate brown, try topping it off with a reflective chrome layer for an elevated autumnal finish.

Jasmine Hyman is the assistant beauty editor at Cosmo who covers all things nails—from the latest polish launches to new manicure trends. For this story, she chatted with nail artists Sigourney Nuñez, John Avery, and Donna Charloff for their insight.

You’ll also likely find her in bed reading a good book or endlessly scrolling TikTok (spoiler: it’s usually the latter) while listening to Harry Styles’ entire discography on repeat. Follow her on Insta to be inundated with pictures of her meals.

#7072. Survey Identifies Data Governance Enforcement Gap for Agentic AI Tools

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time. Perhaps more worrying is that only 19% said their organization could detect in real time when an agent last accessed data outside its intended scope.

That disconnect is at the center of Delinea's fall 2026 Identity Security Report, "The AI Enforcement Gap," which suggests companies have made quick progress establishing AI governance but have not necessarily built the technical controls needed to enforce it.

The findings come from a pair of surveys covering 2,254 IT and security leaders and 2,250 non-IT employees at organizations with at least 500 employees across the U.S, U.K, Germany, Australia, Singapore, UAE, France, and India.

All the organizations were using or piloting AI, while the employees surveyed used at least one AI tool for work.

The problem becomes more consequential as companies move from AI tools that primarily respond to users toward agents capable of taking actions on their behalf.

Eighty-seven percent of IT and security respondents said their organization had experienced or suspected of an incident during the past year in which an AI tool or agent accessed sensitive information beyond what was required for its task.

Delinea's research also suggests companies are giving agents access that can persist longer than necessary. Only 58% of IT and security leaders said they have mechanisms that automatically revoke or expire AI access when a session ends, and those controls do not necessarily cover every agent or tool. Forty-two percent said many agent credentials remain active until an audit.

Agents also are not always receiving their own narrowly defined permissions. Half of IT leaders said their organizations use the employee's existing permissions as a boundary for what an agent can access. That can effectively hand an agent years of accumulated access belonging to the person who launched it.

That is particularly significant because an AI agent does not necessarily behave like a traditional service account. A service account typically performs a predefined process, while an agent can choose different tools, actions and sequences at runtime depending on the situation.

"Service accounts run fixed scripts," said Mike Albrecht, associate director in the Risk Advisory Practice at Cross Country Consulting. "An AI agent decides what actions it's going to take at runtime."

The enforcement problem is not limited to autonomous agents.

The researchers found that 76% of employees surveyed had bypassed formal approval at some point to use AI tools with company data, applications or systems. And 48% said they always or extensively use AI tools without going through formal approval.

Meanwhile, 60% said they had felt pressure to use AI with sensitive or confidential information even when they were not sure doing so was permitted.

Executives were particularly likely to circumvent the process. Eighty-one percent of C-level respondents said they always or regularly bypass AI access approval, compared with 33% of intermediate-level employees.

The result is a widening gap between written AI governance and what companies can see and control. Delinea found that only 41% of IT leaders said all AI tools and agents accessing company data are actively monitored. When an AI tool or agent went outside its intended scope, 55% said it took at least a day to detect the incident, while 30% said detection took four days or longer.

That suggests the next phase of enterprise AI governance may depend less on writing additional rules and more on whether organizations can enforce existing ones while an agent is working.

"Most organizations cannot confidently answer three questions: What agents are running in our environment? What can they access? What have they actually done?" said Delinea CEO Art Gilliland. "An agent can begin a session with legitimate access and drift into something it was never meant to do."

The full report is available for download here.

#7067. Claude Intros Sonnet 5.5 with Model-Switching Cybersecurity Safeguards

Anthropic's new Claude release, Sonnet 5.5, brings cybersecurity safeguards that can change which model handles a request during a Claude conversation. Higher-risk tasks can fall back to Sonnet 5.

According to Anthropic's explanation of model switching, automatic fallback is enabled by default in Claude. Users receive a notice, and the answer is labeled with the model that provided it. The chat then stays on Sonnet 5 unless the user changes the selection.

The company identifies penetration testing and exploit generation among activities that may trigger cybersecurity fallbacks. Source-code vulnerability scanning remains supported. Biology and reasoning-extraction safeguards can block requests outright, rather than route them elsewhere.

The distinction matters operationally. A block stops the request; a fallback allows an attempt to continue with a different model. Neither outcome should be treated as interchangeable with completion by the model the user originally chose.

Anthropic says Sonnet 5.5 generates output more than 30% faster than Sonnet 5 and can reduce cost per task by up to 30%, based on its testing. Token prices remain unchanged.

Those claims describe performance under the company's test conditions. An organization evaluating the service also needs to examine what happens when its own assignments encounter safeguards.

An internal pilot could record how often work is interrupted, whether fallback answers are usable, and when users need help. Such measurements would give buyers a more relevant basis for deployment than assuming every task receives the advertised model's capabilities.

Anthropic has announced plans to expand its Cyber Verification Program with tiered access for Sonnet 5.5, Opus 5.5, and Mythos models. Its current program guidance explicitly excludes the two new 5.5 models and says the expansion is forthcoming.

Organizations should distinguish those planned arrangements from access available today.

The broader enterprise issue is accountability. When an AI service changes models during an assignment, someone still needs to determine whether the resulting work is suitable. A visible handoff helps, but organizations must decide how employees should respond to it.

For more information on Sonnet 5.5, go to the Anthropic site.

#7059. Vegetable Wallpaper and Animal-Clad Handmade Tiles Add Playful Charm to This Maryland Farmhouse

A gut renovation gave a family-owned regenerative farm a modern path forward.

By the time the nine-foot-wide headboard arrived, Angeline Guido Hall and her team at Angeline & Co. had already navigated the unique brand of logistical (and architectural) gymnastics that come with gutting a 1979 home and rebuilding it around a growing family. For five nerve-wracking minutes, the team contemplated whether they could maneuver the enormous custom headboard through a tight vestibule into the primary bedroom, or whether they’d have to remove the windows to get it inside. Fortunately, they puzzled it together, and the bed—large enough to accommodate two parents and their three children—found its rightful place.

It’s an amusing anecdote, but also a telling one: At this Hampstead, Maryland, property, nearly every design decision could be traced back to the wonderfully particular rhythms of the homeowners’ lives. Owners of the regenerative farming business Crescent Moon Farms, they purchased the house overlooking their farm with the intention of putting down roots. The existing residence, however, wasn’t exactly equipped to handle the life they envisioned.

Hall and her team at Angeline & Co., including principal designer Reno Grossi and then-project lead Sarabeth Maher, embarked on an extensive renovation, taking the 6,092-square-foot house down to the studs, expanding its footprint, and reconsidering the floor plan from nearly every angle. “It is technically a remodel, but so much changed that it almost felt like a new build with a lot more constraints,” Hall recalls.

Throughout the renovation, the house’s original character remained part of the conversation. The staircase and two fireplaces survived the gut, while decorative stair brackets spotted during the team’s first walkthrough offered a detail worth preserving. Outside, new stonework, extended porches, and custom shutters punctuated with crescent-moon cutouts nod to the family’s namesake farm.

Inside, a vegetable-patterned wallpaper animates the olive-green butler’s pantry, alongside hand-painted tiles that depict backyard wildlife often seen around the farm. In the reading room, embroidered stars shimmer against grasscloth, accompanied by celestial-shaped ottomans. Antique furniture, unexpected color combinations, and an enviable assortment of collected curiosities give the interiors a personality that couldn't be specified on a floor plan.

Much of that character comes from homeowner Megan, an enthusiastic collector whose art, pottery, and furniture assortment helped shape the interiors. “It isn’t often that we have the opportunity to work with a client who is such a design enthusiast,” Hall says. “She has the most incredible collections of dishes, art, pottery, and antique curiosities she has been curating over the years.”

The finished house is a study in making room for real life, from muddy farm boots and energetic children to an ever-growing collection of beautiful things. Here, even the most extravagant gestures have a practical purpose—including that bed.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Angeline & Co.Architect: Phillip GugliuzzaStylist: Limonata CreativeContractor: Bayer ConstructionLocation: Hampstead, MarylandThe Space: A five-bedroom, three-and-a-half-bathroom home across 6,092 square feet

A hardworking kitchen doesn’t have to read as utilitarian. Hall and her team expanded the home’s footprint to create a generous central gathering space that functions as a second living room, complete with a fireside seating area, breakfast nook, and adjoining butler’s pantry.

A newly raised 10-foot ceiling accommodates a dramatic brass hood and hand-hewn beams, while corner windows bring in views of the backyard. The original fireplace, now outfitted with a wood-burning stove, ensures there’s always somewhere to settle in while dinner cooks. “This family loves to cook, so they wanted the kitchen to feel inviting and spacious enough for people to want to linger and visit,” Hall adds.

Centered around a rather extraordinary antique—a table sourced at Round Top that once belonged to the Versace mansion—this cozy nook is the ideal spot for morning coffee or a quick lunch. Antique Dutch chairs and a custom banquette upholstered in a cheerful Jasper stripe offer a comfortable spot to perch, while interior windows were added to bring additional light into the kitchen.

The vegetable-patterned Schumacher wallpaper was a fitting choice for the home of regenerative farmers, but the references to the family’s livelihood don’t stop there. Hand-painted tiles feature wildflowers and rabbits inspired by the surrounding property, while olive-green cabinetry and skirted storage lend the room a charming, old-fashioned sensibility.

Two entry points—one into the kitchen and one into the serving area—allow this space to act as a practical throughline. “This room almost acts as a second kitchen, so we wanted it to have as much attention and access to natural light as the kitchen itself,” adds Hall.

Stuffy formality was never on the menu in this dining room; instead, Hall wanted the space to feel “relaxed and informal,” in line with how they typically entertain. The homeowner’s existing table anchors the space, joined by vintage Henning Kjærnulf chairs, which Hall had reupholstered in a practical indoor-outdoor fabric.

An antique hutch sourced from Parson’s Nose Antiques displays her collection of handmade plates, while a vintage rug adds another layer of history. Among the room’s more amusing details? An oil portrait depicting the family’s pet as a naval admiral.

Given permission to design without parameters, Hall and her team made the most of this petite powder room. A constellation-like installation of sculptural mirrors provides an unexpected counterpoint to the room’s traditional skirted marble vanity, while botanical wallpaper and deep green trim add another layer of drama.

Even the toilet became a design talking point: the decorative model from Kohler’s Heritage collection, realized in a soft sage green, was enthusiastically embraced by the homeowner. “It’s not often we get excited about a toilet, but this one got us excited!” says Maher.

“One of the first materials selected for this room was the Cowtan & Tout Galaxy wallpaper that has gold and silver sequined stars embroidered on grasscloth,” says Grossi. “We all fell in love with it, and this space was the perfect place to use it.” Custom moon- and star-shaped ottomans continue the theme, offering a subtle nod to Crescent Moon Farms.

The original fireplace was a keeper, but the rest of the living room was reconsidered with family life in mind. A new bay window makes room for the Christmas tree come December, while plush seating—including a sage mohair sofa and antique spindle chairs—encourages cozy family gatherings.

The defining feature of the primary bedroom isn’t immediately obvious in photographs: The custom bed measures a whopping nine feet wide. Designed to accommodate the entire family for movie nights and morning cuddles, it required careful consideration of the room's eight-foot ceilings.

Elsewhere, antique-inspired details soften the scale, including a custom-painted cabinet. “We went to Round Top to source for this project and were really inspired by the painted bridal cabinets we saw there from the 1800s,” says Maher. “We couldn’t find one that was the right size or color palette, so we worked with a painter to recreate one, and it turned out beautifully!”

For a family juggling three children, several dogs, and a working farm, the mudroom needed to earn every inch of its footprint. Custom cabinetry conceals a dog crate and food storage, while dedicated lockers corral backpacks, jackets, and more. A utility sink doubles as a potting station for the homeowner’s gardening hobby, while playful wallpaper, checkered marble floors, and pretty details ensure that practicality still comes with a healthy dose of personality.

An antique American flag from the homeowner’s collection provided the jumping-off point for this spirited bedroom. The team paired it with Cole & Son’s imaginative Macchine Volanti wallpaper, whose red and blue motifs established the room’s palette. Tailor details, like the deep blue trim, plaid fabric, and leather bed, reinforce the Americana references without making the room feel overly themed.

“We knew we wanted this space to be whimsical and thematic, so when we stumbled upon this grasscloth wallpaper with butterflies on it, we knew it would be the perfect fit,” says Grossi. “We used the wallpaper as an anchor point and then added pops of color throughout, such as the seafoam green backplates on the sconces and pink nightstands, to tie it all together.”

If Hall could choose one calling card for the whole home, it would be this basement bathroom. “Of all the rooms in the house, I think this space really demonstrates how much this client embraces color and pattern,” she adds. “What a dream for a design team! The thing I love most about this space is that there are some design elements in here you would think aren’t supposed to work together, but they just do. That is the magic; the client wanted a whimsical wonderland of a bathroom down here and I think we gave that to her.

“We have designed a lot of playrooms, but this was our first children’s room to design that really answered to another level of play,” says Mahar. “Our client wanted a space that was not only fun and vibrant, but also where the kids could flip, run, and burn off some energy.” The team worked with the contractor to ensure the ceilings were properly blocked to accommodate the monkeybars, and incorporated concealed storage to tuck toys out of sight.

Founded in 2014 by Angeline Guido Hall, Angeline & Co has grown from a solo practice into a true design house. Clients who work with the firm experience the full creative strength of a multidisciplinary team, blending expertise in design, architecture, styling, and project management.

#7058. Convergence of AI, Quantum Risks Is Impacting Cybersecurity

Recent research from Thales highlights how AI adoption and emerging quantum computing threats are putting pressure on existing security measures. The 2026 Thales Data Threat Report: Quantum & AI Trends, based on responses from 3,120 security and IT management professionals across 20 countries, found that 98% are considering how the two technologies affect each other. Thirty percent reported a significant increase in security budgets specifically for AI.

The research, conducted by 451 Research by S&P Global, examines both the risks and potential benefits of the technologies. Fifty-seven percent believe quantum computing will enhance machine learning, while 54% expect it to enable advanced simulations of complex systems. The security findings span data governance, cloud infrastructure, and preparations for quantum-resistant encryption.

Cloud-based storage, cloud-delivered applications, and cloud management infrastructure were the three most frequently cited asset types targeted by attackers, at 35%, 34%, and 32%, respectively. The report connects that exposure to the large datasets required for AI training and the rapid expansion of AI infrastructure.

Organizations are also looking to cloud providers for protection: 67% reported investing in their cloud provider's AI-specific security tools, compared with 63% using an established security provider. Respondents could identify multiple sources of protection, including startups, large language model providers, and internally developed controls.

Encryption coverage remains incomplete. Only 7% of organizations reported encrypting more than 80% of their sensitive cloud data, while 29% reported encrypting more than 60%. The report's chart puts the average share encrypted at 47% in 2026, down from 51% in 2025.

The findings extend concerns raised in coverage of Thales' 2025 cloud security research, when AI security already ranked second to cloud security in spending priorities and 52% of respondents reported that AI security spending was displacing existing security budgets. The latest report again places AI security second, while its historical comparison shows average sensitive cloud data encryption coverage falling from 51% in 2025 to 47% in 2026.

AI security was the second-highest prioritized security spending category overall, behind cloud security. But protecting data ranked much lower among the measures organizations use to judge AI projects: Reducing or managing risks of data loss or noncompliance placed sixth among seven success criteria, at 35%.

Improving customer experience led those measures at 68%, followed by reducing employee toil through greater task automation at 63%. Meanwhile, poor data quality or initial data governance was the leading inhibitor to AI adoption, cited by 65%; security risks from exposed data followed at 61%. Another 51% cited AI initiatives moving too quickly to be properly secured.

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

#7056. Tom Hardy's Favorite Clothing Brand Just Made Sailing Gear Using Bulletproof-Vest Technology

Founded by two former Special Forces soldiers, British label ThruDark teamed up with Musto to make some seriously hardcore stuff.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Tom Hardy has always looked like a man who knows his way around a bit of extreme weather. So it's perhaps unsurprising that he's a fan of ultra-tough British outdoor clothing brand ThruDark. He recently set the internet on fire when he wore the brand's Rōnin Tanto ski jacket in the music video for his latest foray into rap, complete with a rather menacing balaclava. (He's actually been involved with the company since 2018, when he narrated its launch film.)

Now ThruDark has teamed up with Musto, the grand old name in British sailing gear, to produce a collection that looks ready for an Atlantic crossing—or possibly a covert military operation in the dead of night.

Founded in 2018 by former Royal Marines and Special Boat Service operators Anthony “Staz” Stazicker and Louis Tinsley, ThruDark makes seriously technical clothing informed by its founders' combined 28 years of military experience. Musto, meanwhile, has been keeping sailors dry since the ’60s. Its founder, Keith Musto, won an Olympic silver medal in 1964.

Their new Nautikos collection combines the two companies' expertise in what Musto claims is its most technically advanced offshore range yet. There's Gore-Tex Pro waterproofing, Dyneema reinforcement (an exceptionally strong fibre also used in climbing ropes, bullet-resistant armor, and yacht rigging), removable pockets that transfer between layers, impact-absorbing knee protection, and reflective details designed to help keep you visible in a storm.

The headline piece is a $2,200 waterproof jacket, with matching trousers for $1,750. There's also a $325 fleece, a $105 cap and, rather brilliantly, a $105 T-shirt made using fabric technology that incorporates recycled coffee grounds.

And what does Hardy make of it? Well, there's no suggestion he's taken up competitive sailing just yet. But the relationship with ThruDark is genuine.

“We always love to see Tom wearing ThruDark,” co-founder Stazicker tells Esquire. “From our side, we're grateful he does. It's a genuine relationship built on mutual respect, and we never take it for granted. We're really proud of what we've built with Musto, and we'd love to see it out there on anyone who puts our kit through its paces. He'd certainly wear it well.”

Whether you're planning to cross the Southern Ocean or just want to look as though you're seriously preparing for the next big nor’easter, it's an impressive amount of engineering to have on your side.

#7053. This Colorado Mountain Escape Is Home to a Plum Kitchen, “Quilted” Bedroom, and the Occasional Grizzly Bear

Designer Emily Tucker designed her family’s rustic retreat with hosting in mind.

The first guest to make himself at home at designer Emily Tucker’s Colorado mountain retreat was a bear. While the property was still under construction, the curious visitor took to wandering through the unfinished rooms, even leaving a few paw prints on the architectural drawings. It was a fitting introduction to life at Camp Grizz, Tucker’s family getaway in Ward, Colorado, where the surrounding wilderness has always had a say in things.

Tucker and her family purchased the wooded parcel, which backs up to a national forest, in 2020. The existing cabin offered rustic accommodations (read: no running water), but the family had bigger plans for the property. “The goal was to build a house as modest as possible, but able to fit two families at a time,” says Tucker, who regularly hosts friends and their children for weekends away. Building on a landscape composed almost entirely of granite, however, was no small feat. With no existing infrastructure to tap into, everything from the well to the driveway had to be created from scratch. “It was a beast,” the designer adds.

Tucker envisioned interiors that felt “colorful, warm, Scandinavian-ish, but with a lot of collected touches,” taking inspiration from the mismatched charm of a patchwork quilt. That influence appears everywhere, from the primary bedroom’s pieced-together grasscloth walls to the checkerboard bathroom tile and eclectic mix of textiles. Designing her own home also offered Tucker the opportunity to indulge ideas that hadn’t quite found an audience with her clients. Chief among them? An eggplant-purple kitchen, complete with plum cabinetry and glossy zellige tile. “I had proposed purple kitchens and mudrooms for many clients who had all said no,” she laughs.

But Camp Grizz’s greatest luxury has little to do with its finishes. “My favorite thing about the house is the way we feel in it,” Tucker says. “It’s cozy, layered, collected, and holds our family and friends for celebrations, relaxing, and just enjoying life’s small moments.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Emily TuckerLocation: Ward, ColoradoThe Space: A three-bedroom, two-and-a-half-bathroom home across 1,800 square feet

A chance discovery in a stair-parts catalog led Tucker to the charming Swiss-style balusters that distinguish the staircase. The decorative stenciling came later, inspired by a trip to London during construction. Tucker and her son worked out the pattern together on a spare baluster before local decorative painter Annie DeCamp brought their design to life.

A mix of existing furnishings (a “hodge-pod of things I already owned,” says Tucker) gives the living room its collected, unfussy character. Flanking the wood-burning stove, a pair of floating live-edge wood benches act as a happy departure from Tucker’s original plan for concrete seating. “When the opportunity came up to use the live-edge wood slabs, it seemed the perfect fit,” she says.

“The vintage Thomas Moser chairs were purchased before we even broke ground on the house; I love these chairs and always have,” Tucker says of the seating in the dining nook. “The classic but modern, pared-back Americana design was perfect for the space.” Nearby, trim clad in Rattan by Benjamin Moore unites the open floor plan.

After years of pitching purple kitchens to clients without success, Tucker finally took the plunge herself. “Once I landed on eggplant purple, I couldn't get it out of my head,” she says. Benjamin Moore’s Plum Royale cabinetry meets glossy Purple Iris zellige tile from Zia Tile, with soapstone countertops providing a moody counterpoint. Behind the saturated facade, clever cabinetry conceals appliances and pantry essentials, leaving the countertops streamlined and clear.

For her own bedroom, Tucker gathered leftover Phillip Jeffries wallcoverings from previous projects, supplemented them with her installer’s remnants, and pieced together a one-of-a-kind patchwork-style wall treatment. The room’s generous ceiling height also gave her the chance to indulge another longtime wish: a canopy bed, which she dressed with modified Anthropologie curtains.

A length of cowboy-print fabric gifted to Tucker by a client became the unexpected starting point for the bunk room’s spirited palette. Picking up on the textile’s red accents, she painted the built-in bunks in Benjamin Moore’s Caliente and layered them with a deliberately mismatched assortment of Pendleton blankets and pillows from Target, St. Frank, and IKEA.

The home’s patchwork inspiration finds another expression in the bunk bathroom, where blue and green tiles from Wow Tile form an intentionally irregular checkerboard. Leftover green-veined marble flooring from another project adds another layer of color.

A lucky auction find meant this compact guest bedroom needed an unconventional layout. Because the painted dresser was too large to fit alongside a traditionally centered bed, Tucker aligned it to one edge instead, installing a wall-to-wall upholstered headboard to restore visual balance.

Emily Tucker is the founder and principal designer of Emily Tucker Design, a Boulder, Colorado–based interior design firm specializing in colorful, collected, and deeply personal residential interiors. A California native, Tucker earned her degree in Architecture and Planning from the University of Colorado Boulder before honing her eye under celebrated designers Ruthie Sommers, Kelly Wearstler, and Steven Gambrel in Los Angeles and New York City. In 2015, she returned to Colorado to establish her eponymous studio, where she now oversees projects across the country, ranging from ground-up construction to thoughtful renovations.

The weather wasn’t on King Charles’s side during his and Queen Camilla’s Caribbean tour. Charles got completely soaked during the annual Independence Ceremonial Parade at Sir Vivian Richards Stadium in Antigua and Barbuda on Sunday, November 1—and the internet is losing it at his reaction.

#7049. Flashpoint Receives Patent for Ransomware Risk Model, Tying Vulnerabilites to Likelihood of Attack

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

The patent comes at a time when security teams are confronting a growing vulnerability problem: Flashpoint's Global Threat Intelligence Report: 2026 Midyear Edition tracked 21,667 vulnerability disclosures during the first half of 2026, an 8% increase.

Of those, 4,015, or around 19 percent, already had public or functional exploit code. Flashpoint also reported a 45 percent increase in Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS) attacks during the period.

The patent was granted August 11 2026, although the underlying Ransomware Risk model isn't new. It has been available through Flashpoint Vulnerability Intelligence since 2022.

The model addresses a basic vulnerability-management problem: severity and real-world ransomware risk aren't necessarily the same thing, because severity scores do not necessarily indicate which flaws attackers are most likely to target.

The Common Vulnerability Scoring System, or CVSS, measures how severe a vulnerability could be. The Exploit Prediction Scoring System, or EPSS, estimates the probability of exploitation more broadly.

Flashpoint's Ransomware Risk model adds another dimension by comparing newly disclosed vulnerabilities with the characteristics of flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks.

That could change where a vulnerability lands in an organization's patching queue. A lower-severity flaw might normally sit behind numerous critical vulnerabilities. But if its characteristics closely resemble vulnerabilities repeatedly targeted by ransomware operators, security teams may have reason to move it higher.

"A severity score alone can't tell you which vulnerabilities pose the greatest real-world risk of exploitation by ransomware actors," Flashpoint CEO Josh Lefkowitz said. "Our patented Ransomware Risk model applies years of threat intelligence to that problem, giving customers an earlier signal and helping them prioritize based on how attackers actually operate."

The firm's midyear research also illustrates the scale of the prioritization problem. Its vulnerability intelligence covers traditional IT systems as well as cloud, IoT, operational technology, open source software. and third-party libraries.

The company says it also tracks more than 105,000 vulnerabilities that aren't represented in CVE or the National Vulnerability Database, expanding the pool of flaws that security teams may need to evaluate.

The cybersecurity company's patented methodology profiles vulnerabilities using more than 60 technical characteristics, including whether a flaw can be exploited remotely without authentication, whether it affects operational technology systems, and how exploitation could affect data availability.

Those characteristics form a multi-factor fingerprint. The model maps each vulnerability against clusters containing flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks and assigns a Low, Medium, High, or Critical Ransomware Risk rating.

Flashpoint says the rating can be generated when a vulnerability is disclosed, rather than requiring security teams to wait for public evidence of active exploitation. Ratings are then updated as new ransomware activity and threat intelligence emerge.

Within Flashpoint Ignite, Ransomware Risk appears alongside CVSS, EPSS, Social Risk, and exploit maturity. The idea isn't to replace existing scoring systems, but to give administrators additional information when deciding what to remediate first.

Nor does a High or Critical Ransomware Risk rating mean a vulnerability will necessarily be exploited. The model measures how closely its characteristics resemble vulnerabilities historically associated with ransomware attacks.

For security teams facing thousands of potential vulnerabilities, that distinction matters. The flaw carrying the highest severity score isn't necessarily the one that most closely resembles what ransomware operators have historically chosen to attack.

"Despite being the most popular indicator for priority, we know that severity alone doesn't tell you whether a vulnerability will be exploited or used in ransomware," said Ben Haynes, Data Science & Analytics Lead at Flashpoint and co-inventor of the patented methodology.

"A vulnerability can score low on a traditional severity scale and still share the characteristics we repeatedly see in ransomware attacks."

For more information, go to the Flashpoint site.

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

Microsoft is combining its security information and event management and threat protection products within Microsoft Defender, creating what the company calls an Integrated Security Operations Center built for an era of AI-driven attacks.

A recent Cloud Security Alliance report urges organizations to inventory, govern, and continuously monitor machine and agent identities.

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT.

#7048. 6 Surprising Antiques That Homeowners Are Searching for Right Now, According to Experts

These vintage finds add instant charm, character, and utility.

Pictured above: Ashley Hotham Cox’s family room where she has many of her antique treasures on display.

No matter the aesthetic of your home, there’s always a place for an antique or two (or 10). From new traditional to variations of contemporary style, there are bound to be some vintage items that’ll make your space look more layered with added character and a charm you simply can’t find with new pieces.

This is the perfect time of year to start thinking about what in your home you can switch up or refresh. To get the lowdown on which antiques people are buying this time of year, we reached out to three designers—Jennifer Baxter, Victoria Maggard, and Gaia Guidi Filippi—who shared what types of pieces homeowners are looking for, both for themselves and as gifts.

Salvaged architectural pieces are always going to be popular, like doors or fireplace surrounds for example, but Baxter is really drawn to antique pedestals and plinths at the moment. “A pedestal gives one object, like a vase or a sculpture, its own focal point and gives a neglected corner or window bay some personality,” she explains. These displays can feel formal or museum-like if you want, but they don’t have to be styled that way; they can be kept fairly casual. “Even a marble or alabaster plinth looks warm with a few branches in a simple glass vase.”

For history buffs, vintage maps as art are taking the antique world by storm at the moment. “They introduce color, pattern, and a personal story (where you grew up, where you met someone special, your favorite travel destination) that you never need to second-guess or defend,” Baxter says. “I like to hang them on chains from a picture rail so the collection can grow without a new nail hole.”

If you've ever enjoyed a meal on an extendable dining table, you are already familiar with the concept of leaves. When it comes to dining tables, the leaves are usually separate, completely removable pieces that slide into a designated slot in the table. Drop leaves, on the other hand, attach to the table by a hinge that keeps the leaf upright, making the table larger. Or, the leaves can fold downward, making the table smaller. Either way, the leaf is visible.

"These are especially great for small spaces, or even larger living or great rooms where the homeowner likes to entertain," Filippi explains. "On a daily basis, this table sits beautifully against a wall or sofa as a console, with objects and decor on it. But when you need to, it does double-duty as a card table or an extra dining table."

Even in small spaces, an accent chair—especially an antique one—adds instant charm and character. "There's just no limit to how you can customize and make an antique accent chair work in your space," Filippi says. "From painting it to reupholstering it and giving something old that has served another household an entirely new life." Alternatively, you can always leave it as is if you like the slightly worn look of an antique seat. "And with chairs, there's such a vast availability of periods, styles, and price ranges. It's the easiest thing to incorporate into a design."

The great thing about antique accent chairs is that they actually pair really well with more contemporary desks, tables, and more, so if you want to juxtapose time periods, start with a centuries-old chair style. Baxter is also a big fan of incorporating an antique chair into a space. She says, "Antique chairs are often where personality enters a room, and antique chairs, in particular, carry an instant sense of character."

A specific style of accent chair that’s making its comeback in 2026 is the wingback chair, according to Maggard, who loves using them at the ends of a dining table, flanking a gaming table, or a single one alone in a quiet corner. “While there are plenty of modern interpretations, antique wing chairs have a character that's difficult to replicate,” Maggard says. “The exaggerated proportions, dramatic wings, and unusual silhouettes make each one unique.” The strong form is still wonderfully surprising.

“As interiors become more layered and personal, we're seeing a renewed appreciation for antique oil paintings, particularly smaller works,” Maggard says. Just because a painting is small doesn’t mean it can’t make a statement while still feeling approachable thanks to its modest size. “You can fall in love with a painting at an antique shop and almost always find a place for it at home,” Maggard explains, adding that it’s also a design-forward way to make a change without too much of a commitment.

This is what Baxter loves about small oil paintings and drawings as well, that they can be found a home almost anywhere. “There’s just an undeniable pleasure in finding something unexpected that speaks to you,” Baxter says. “It's not the same pressure as hunting for the perfect piece to fill a wall; and an imperfect, one-of-a-kind painting that you found makes a room feel distinctly yours rather than like a showroom.”

Antique bar cabinets are perhaps less accessible than, say, antique chairs, but they are definitely worth looking into if you like to entertain. Bar cabinets have been around for a long time, but they really gained popularity toward the end of Prohibition in the United States. Decades later, throughout the 1960s, they were one of the most popular pieces of furniture any stylish homeowner could snap up.

"Depending on the style, they can be sultry, masculine, or give distinctive nods to a period that immediately injects interest into a piece of furniture that is functional and a gathering point," Filippi adds. "And again for small spaces, when closed, they can also serve the dual purpose of being a place to display objects."

#7043. SailPoint Intros New Capabilities to Address Agentic AI Identity Security Challenge

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint. In its latest Horizons of Identity Security report, SailPoint found that 79% of enterprises already have AI agents running in production, while just 2% have deployed identity security tools specifically designed to govern them.

That gap could become harder to ignore as organizations give agents more autonomy. Agents can increasingly access applications, sensitive data, and credentials, and in some cases take actions on a user's behalf, effectively creating a new class of identity that IT and security teams need to manage.

SailPoint, which develops identity security and access governance technology for enterprises, is responding with a collection of new identity security capabilities announced at its Navigate 2026 conference. The company is expanding its Agentic Fabric to discover "shadow AI" agents and provide runtime authorization and controls capable of immediately disabling an agent.

The company also introduced what it calls SailPoint Autonomous Agents, specialized AI agents designed to govern other agents. The security agents can monitor runtime activity, intercept threats, and adjust permissions while allowing legitimate agents to continue operating, SailPoint said.

The idea reflects one of the challenges created by increasingly autonomous AI systems: Traditional human approval processes may not be fast enough to govern software capable of taking large numbers of actions on its own.

"The idea that a security admin is going to review and approve access for a group of autonomous agents making 10,000 tool calls a second isn't just outdated — it's a fantasy," SailPoint CTO Chandra Gnanasambandam said.

Another part of SailPoint's approach focuses on visibility.

New endpoint and browser sensors, along with SIEM and XDR telemetry and non-human identity discovery capabilities, are designed to uncover agents, MCP servers, credentials, tools, and data stores that haven't been brought under an organization's identity program.

The company is also introducing Autonomous Identity Security Posture Management, or A-ISPM, which uses identity information from its Atlas platform to identify dormant accounts, orphaned access, shadow administrators, and other potentially risky access paths. Organizations can then revoke access, disable an identity or AI agent, assign an owner or automate enforcement.

Just-in-Time provisioning is another part of the strategy. Rather than giving people, service accounts, and AI agents permanent access, permissions can be granted when needed and subjected to conditions such as reauthentication or a business justification.

Capabilities across SailPoint's Agentic Fabric, Human Fabric, and Atlas platforms are beginning to roll out globally. A-ISPM and several other announced features are expected in the company's fourth quarter of fiscal 2027.

"Autonomous agents are no longer just experimental copilots; they are becoming the primary execution layer of modern enterprises," SailPoint President Matt Mills said.

For more information, go to the SailPoint site.

#7039. Designers Say Placemats Are Falling Out of Style—Here’s What They’re Using Instead

These chic alternatives are stealing the spotlight.

There was a time when no tablescape was complete without a carefully chosen placemat. Department stores, boutiques, and linen closets in hosting-ready homes were filled with placemats covered in holiday motifs and seasonal hues. Whether serving as a practical safeguard or a conversation-starting detail, placemats were once a nonnegotiable element of the table.

These days, not so much. This table-setting staple is slowly disappearing. Here’s what designers say is behind the decline—and what they’re using in its place.

Placemats have long been used to add pops of color and texture to tablescapes. But now these textiles are losing their place at the table as hosts abandon them for chargers, runners, and slabs that give a more minimalist look to the table. However, some designers say that placemats will always have a place at their table.

“We love layers in all aspects of home design,” says Johanna Lyle, founder of the interior design firm Jo Lyle & Co. “Placemats are another way to add texture, color, and/or pattern to your tablescape. Functionally, they also protect your tablecloth from the messiest areas.”

Pretty as they may be, placemats are a first line of defense against spills, splatters, and stains. They’re easy to swap out, often less expensive than newer alternatives, and bring together style, value, and practicality. But they can interrupt the visual flow of a tablescape—a faux pas even novice hosts may want to avoid. Charger plates offer a popular alternative, pairing practical protection with a look that complements flatware.

“Placemats often chop up a table,” says Mallory Robins, co-founder of the Kansas City-based luxury design studio Kobel + Co. “A beautiful tablecloth layered with chargers offers the same defined effect but with a more seamless base layer.”

Designer Sarah Stacey couldn’t agree more. While she would hesitate to cancel placemats entirely, she’s rarely reaching for them when putting together her tablescapes.

“I usually opt for a tablecloth because placemats always feel bulky and too oversized. A tablecloth offers more flexibility in decorating a tablescape,” says Stacey.

If you find yourself yearning for the charm of linens, Robins suggests using linen napkins to add a personalized feel, textured detail, and pattern into your designs. But the most bespoke dining detail sure to dazzle guests? Place cards.

“Most critically, we're team place cards over place mats! There is nothing like walking into a gorgeous dinner party with clarity as to where you are sitting,” says Robins. “It feels inherently welcoming and is a great way to curate your guests into the ultimate conversation groupings.”

If you happen to own a beloved collection of placemats, we wouldn’t suggest tossing them just because they’re currently out of favor. Even the most dated decor details have found a way to resurface in a new way. The trend cycle is a cycle, after all.

#7037. Survey: Most Organizations Are Not Consistently Measuring AI Value Against Costs

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs. The company's Global AI Pulse Q3 2026 report found that 89% of organizations report measurable value from AI, while just 12% consistently assess that value against costs across the organization.

Average planned AI investment for the next 12 months has risen to $210 million, compared with $186 million in the first quarter of 2026.

The findings are based on responses from 2,131 senior leaders across 20 countries and jurisdictions, surveyed between July 23 and August 26, 2026.

Most organizations have established ways to track AI spending: 61% review costs during project approval, and 59% monitor them once systems are operating.

Consistently connecting those costs to value remains less common. Beyond the 12% that do so across the organization, another 26% assess value against costs for most initiatives.

Organizations reporting established AI returns have stronger measurement practices. Among this group, 48% consistently assess value against costs, four times the proportion in the overall survey group.

Cost-monitoring dashboards are also more common among organizations reporting established returns: 77% use them, compared with 43% of those still experimenting.

"The organizations seeing a return did the implementation and the transformation properly: guardrails in place, workforce ready, data cleaned up," said Priya Emmanuel, global head of aIQ at KPMG International. "That is a sequence, not a secret."

Organizations are also developing more formal systems to manage AI deployment.

Fifty-five percent operate a formal AI management layer, which KPMG calls a "harness layer," comprising controls and tools between AI models and business applications.

Security and identity controls, along with data access controls, were the most common capabilities, each reported by 43% of respondents. Model routing, which determines which model handles a particular task, was reported by 23%.

Meanwhile, 53% assign accountability for AI-informed decisions to a named executive, the CEO, or an executive committee.

The proportion reporting significant employee adoption of AI agents rose from 25% in the first quarter to 34% in the third quarter.

Cybersecurity concerns were more common among organizations with mature AI programs.

Half of those reporting established AI returns identified cybersecurity as a barrier, compared with roughly one-third of those still experimenting.

Overall, 86% of respondents said their organizations are adapting their cybersecurity operating models to address AI-accelerated threats.

The report identifies accountability, operational resilience, and cost management as priorities as AI becomes more embedded in business operations.

"Many organizations have made AI models widely available and track their use," said Swaminathan Chandrasekaran, head of the US AI Center of Excellence at KPMG. "But consumption alone is not a strategy."

For the full report, visit the KPMG site.

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.
