#7032. Watch 9-1-1 for the Volcano Eruptions and Bee-nadoes. Stay for the Heart.

The first responder drama understands grief and the power of found families better than any other series on TV.

But 9-1-1 is also a tough show to stay up to date with—there are over 140 episodes across 10 seasons, not including the spin-offs. Critics call it trashy but, really, it’s absurd excellence. Me and the 9 million average viewers of the most recent season can’t be wrong. 9-1-1 is epic, must watch television. So, after a brief time away, I returned to 9-1-1 because I heard that something crazy occurred, even by 9-1-1’s standards. And no, I’m not talking about the volcano eruption.

Midway through season 8, 9-1-1 killed off a major character. For seven years on TV, Peter Krause played the soul of the fire station, Captain Bobby Nash. Then, in season 8, episode 15, he contracts a deadly fever during a chemical lab outbreak and decides to quarantine himself inside. Before he dies, he hands the sole antidote to his appointed successor, Howard “Chimney” Han (Kenneth Choi). Major character deaths are not new to TV. Game of Thrones famously dispatched characters like the series was pulling petals off daisies while playing, “He loves me, he loves me not.” But after eight seasons spent with Krause’s character basically serving as the paternal guiding hand of the series, it was an emotional blow.

Around the same time, I received news that my grandfather had terminal late-stage cancer. Due to distance—I live in New York City, while he lives in China—I realized that I would be unable to travel to say goodbye at his side. It was devastating, and I didn’t know how I would manage roughly 7,000 miles away from my family. We watch TV to be entertained and be comforted from the stress of our daily lives. We also tend to create parasocial relationships with our favorite TV characters. So, following my grandfather’s diagnosis, mourning the loss of someone so central to your family like the fictional Captain Nash helped me to grieve.

I originally avoided the show the best I could once images leaked of the cast carrying a casket through the streets of Los Angeles. Usually, 9-1-1’s promotion is enough to draw you back in. You’ve most likely seen the viral ads for 9-1-1 containing bee-nadoes, tsunamis on Santa Monica, or giant inflatable runaway pumpkins. But despite the wacky premises, 9-1-1 was something that I was relegated to watching alone—mostly because my boyfriend would groan and exit the room every time I had it on our TV. Plus, when I bring it up amongst friends and family, I’m usually met with raised brows and derision. But I’m tired of pretending that 9-1-1 isn’t amazing TV. It isn’t solely the ridiculous emergencies that keeps me watching: It’s the genuine portrayal of the first responders and fire station’s found family.

Following Captain Nash’s death, the most recent season focused on the aftermath among those he left behind. The tremendous loss affected every character on the show, including the tough but vulnerable lead, police officer Athena Grant (Angela Bassett). As Grant, Bassett faces an array of life-or-death situations with compassion and ferocity every episode. Together with Krause’s Captain Nash, who played her husband, they faced a lot of fires and even one cruise ship invaded by pirates. They got through it by sticking together.

Aside from the leads, 9-1-1 also features the strong and smart Henrietta Wilson (Aisha Hinds). Jennifer Love Hewitt plays Maddie Buckley, a 9-1-1 operator and domestic abuse survivor. Her husband, Han, steps up as the new captain of the firehouse. But the character I find most compelling is Evan “Buck” Buckley (Oliver Stark), the impulsive, adrenaline chasing firefighter.

Buck is the beating heart of the show, starting out as a brash and immature member of the firehouse before growing into a more level-headed and responsible emergency responder. His close friendship with the single-father firefighter, Edmundo “Eddie” Diaz (Ryan Guzman), is constantly shipped by fans of the show in a fervor that would make Spock and Kirk stans blush. (The passion from fans rooting for the two to get together has only increased since Buck came out as bisexual in season seven.) But the show wants you to root for them, often spotlighting how the two characters would literally die in a blaze of glory just to save the other.

Throughout my years watching 9-1-1, these characters became something of my found family as well. Seeing every character struggle with the loss of Captain Nash in their own ways—like reconnecting with religion, numbing themselves with substance abuse, or facing their fears—helped me accept that my grief might manifest differently than I expected. It was cathartic to see all the ways that the pain would be survivable with the help of my loved ones.

The tenth season of 9-1-1, which premiered on ABC on October 15, teases an erupting volcano in the middle of Los Angeles. That’s just good TV, folks. But as the season carries on, the show spreads a message of optimism for humanity—proving every episode that moving with compassion and relying on community helps us build resiliency no matter what absurd tragedies we may face. I don’t care if the volcano takes Los Angeles back to the prehistoric era, I know that firehouse 118 will get through it with the power of their friendship and their boundless determination to do good. Amidst our constant news cycle dominated by bad actors, we could all do with a little dose of hope.

#7030. Report: Cyber Attacks Increasingly Exploit Trusted Workplace Tools

In its latest threat intelligence report, Ontinue found that attackers are increasingly using trusted workplace tools to trick users into giving them access to enterprise networks. Approximately 30% of organizations monitored by the cybersecurity company experienced at least one attempt to impersonate IT support through Microsoft Teams during the first half of 2026. About one in every 990 external Teams contacts showed signs of someone pretending to be an internal IT worker.

Ontinue's report says the trouble is that these attacks blend in with ordinary workday communication, which makes them harder to catch.

"The majority of effective attacks today don't rely on sophisticated exploits. They rely on trust," Balazs Greksza, director of Advanced Threat Operations at Ontinue, said in the company's announcement.

Ontinue broke down how a typical attack works: An employee gets an unexpected Teams message or call from someone claiming to work in the organization's IT department. The person warns of a security issue, a software problem or an update that can't wait. Then they walk the employee through opening Windows Quick Assist, installing remote-access software, or running a PowerShell command. Following those steps can hand the attacker control of the employee's computer, often without the employee realizing it.

The approach is very similar to the Black Basta ransomware group, which helped popularize similar attacks in 2024. Unfortunately, Ontinue found the technique has since been adopted by other cybercriminal groups, including some with no known link to Black Basta.

Some threat agents are also combining tactics. They might first overwhelm an employee's inbox with unwanted e-mails, then reach out through Teams pretending to be IT support offering to fix the problem.

"Phishing no longer resides solely in your inbox. It has spread across the entire corporate communications environment," said Mika Aalto, co-founder and CEO of Hoxhunt.

Teams impersonation is only part of the problem. Ontinue also found attackers taking advantage of legitimate Microsoft sign-in processes. Between May and June, researchers identified 10 confirmed device-code phishing compromises across five customer tenants.

In these attacks, criminals trick employees into completing what appears to be a normal Microsoft authentication process. But doing so can give the attacker access to the employee's account and system without actually breaking their password.

The report also found attackers using blockchain-based infrastructure to hide malicious activity, accounting for more than 25% of confirmed malicious ClickFix findings.

"Attackers are no longer looking for gaps in your perimeter. They are looking for the systems your organization already trusts and finding ways to move through them undetected," said Shane Barney, chief information security officer at Keeper Security.

The security firm says that IT needs to keep a closer eye on unexpected Teams contacts, remote-support requests, suspicious PowerShell activity, and changes to authentication settings. Employees should also confirm sensitive requests through established internal channels, rather than relying on the name or account that appears on their screen.

Aviv Nahum, co-founder and CEO of Above Security, noted that AI is making these attacks more convincing by helping criminals imitate familiar voices, writing styles, and conversations.

"The security model therefore must shift from 'does this look real?' to 'can I independently verify that this person is who they claim to be?'" Nahum said.

The full Ontinue report can be found here.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

Cloud Security Alliance's 2026 Top Threats report ranks identity as the leading cloud security concern, followed by two new AI-related risks.

#7027. The 8 Best High-End Coffee Makers of Every Variety

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Whether you're looking for drip, espresso, or cold brew—this is how to get your caffein fix.

Every true coffee drinker knows that a bad cup can ruin an otherwise good morning. At home, good coffee is consistency, it’s comfort, it is often what gets you out of bed in the morning. If you get a coffee machine that starts to deliver substandard cups, you start to question your purchase... and your sanity.

It’s not just about a perfect cup of Joe; espresso drinkers deserve the luxury of an easy at-home solution that allows them to make the perfect latte or Americano, and repeat that process daily. It doesn’t matter who you are, you deserve not to stress about good coffee. Your order might not be simple, but these machines keep it easy breezy.

I've rounded up eight of my fellow Esquire editor's favorite high-end coffee machines (including a few of my personal picks) that go above-and-beyond. From elevated drip offerings to single-serve delights to automated espresso heaven— there’s even one machine entirely dedicated to cold brewing—I’ve included everything a coffee drinker could ask for. Built-in grinders? They have it. Milk foam wand? Of course. Or just want a plain old drip option that’s elevated? I’ve got you covered there too.

These are the cafe quality coffee machines that do all the work for you (or don’t, if that’s what you’re after) and look great doing it. With any one of them, you'll regularly be making top-notch coffee at home in no time.

For your first high-end coffee maker, there's no machine more universally loved than the Moccamaster. It's relatively small, counter-friendly, and made simply so that it lasts for decades. It also just looks better than anything else on the market.

Here's its brew deal: Functionally, it is like a drip machine where you set it and forget it. But in practice, it is actually an automated pour-over machine. There is a nine-hole outlet that drips water in stages, at strategically placed times and at specific temperatures, so that you get a proper bloom and extraction phase. It takes a pretty quick six minutes, but the drawback is you can only brew a full or half carafe—no single cup. But if you are looking for the best non-espresso coffee maker in the business, the list could pretty much start and end right here.

Once you get past (or if you can get past) the sticker shock, Balmuda's coffee machine is bringing something special to the easy-cup-of-coffee table. The small-capacity machine makes just about a cup and a half of coffee, but it is as good of a cup and a half of coffee as you'll ever find. There are a few controls—three sizes and regular, strong, and ice brew options—perhaps a few more than you would expect or want.

However, the brew process is kind of extraordinary to watch. The water comes out in small, steamy drips at specific intervals and temperatures to get the most out of the grounds. The execution is just about perfect, though I find the water doesn't hit the grounds equally. The middle can end up over-extracted and the sides under. I find it brews better when I push some of the grounds toward the center so they get equally saturated, but for a coffee machine that costs $700, I would prefer the water head was a little bigger so that it would do this job for me.

“High-end” can mean different things to different people. If you want to move on from a Nespresso pod machine, this is would be our first port of call in figuring out if you will enjoy the life of “espresso guy.” Don’t go onto the expensive stuff until you have at least tried one of these.

What sets the Breville Bambino apart from other value-focused espresso machines is that it has the chops to be an end-game machine. On the build side, that is because the body of the machine is stainless steel, and the accents have the weight you get in the higher-end machines. On the brew side, it’s because it does a true low-pressure pre-infusion—a crucial part of the espresso process—and has incredible temperature control.

The main thing we think really sets it up as an in-between machine is the portafilter. It’s a 54-mm basket, the standard commercial size, and it has the option to go non-pressurized like commercial machines, or pressurized like more budget machines. What does that mean? If you have a good espresso-level grinder—since that is often a more important step—this machine can be as good as the eye-wateringly expensive ones you’re about to see. But, if you aren’t at that level, a pressurized basket can pull a decent shot with even Café Bustelo (no hate, we still drink it).

If you don’t need another difficult learning curve in your life (trust me, I relate), you might want something a tad easier than the De’Longhi. You don’t need to go fully automatic to turn espresso brewing into an intuitive three-step process. According to coffee snobs, the grind selection and tamping processes are the important and fun part. This machine keeps that intact, while boiling it down (hehe) to just selecting your grind size, dose, and then you can take the mathematically correct number of grounds for your drink of choice and tamper it and then let the machine pull your perfect shot.

It unfortunately doesn’t have some of the more handy automatic cleaning functions of the more full-auto machines. This means weekly upkeep, especially if you make frequent use of the milk wand, which for some people is an absolute no go.

This is the espresso machine to get if you’re dedicated to learning. It will make your coffee and be your teacher all in one. First off, the thing just looks great. I’m a big fan of the cherry red and walnut color scheme myself. Inside, it’s basically a big computer dedicated to brewing the best espresso possible, and to make sure you improve it will even give suggestions over time—pro tip, if something’s off it’s usually your grind size. You still get all the fun of precision espresso making, with a wand for you to steam your own milk to boot, only you will actually learn how to pull a better shot; you won’t just stumble into it or, conversely, have a machine do your thinking for you.

Now, this is the Series 1 of a technology-based product, which means it may come with its fair share of potential issues. You may get one that works flawlessly. Or... a random system error can cause the whole thing to brick or the screen to be completely unusable. If it breaks, Fellow customer-service is genuinely interested in helping you out, so don’t be afraid to be a first-gen adopter. That said, I’m hoping a Series 2 will roll out a touch more seamlessly.

This is without a doubt the most high-end espresso machine on the market right now. The rotary pump, dual boiler, and three-tier setup combine to make for a foolproof semi-auto machine that still leaves room for experimenting. Seriously, the shots we’ve been pulling from this have been consistent to an almost scary degree. Through all the bean switches, recipe tweaking, and shot dialing, the coffee was always passable. The dedicated tier for the grinder keeps your beans away from the hot water. In most coffee makers, they sit on top and over time this exposure to heat can lead to burnt beans and worse coffee.

What you end up with is the difference between needing to dial in the right settings, and the freedom to experiment. The Meraki machine is always the latter, giving you room to grow your hobby without making bad coffee along the way.

DiFor as much as I love my KitchenAid automatic coffeemaker, the Magnifica Duo is a much better value. It’s one of the rare consumer machines where you can sit back and genuinely let it do all the work. It gives more options too, which is exactly what people want from an automatic espresso machine. Absolutely no fuss and the ability to choose any drink from the screen on a whim. It’s as easy as selecting what you want and waiting a couple of minutes. That’s the magic of fully automatic (or superautomatic) espresso.

If you’re really into the milk and foam art side of being a barista, this is the high-end machine to get. The dual milk frothing tank can make hot or cold foam from any kind of milk or milk alternative. From the touch-screen menu, you can adjust foam texture and tell it which dairy alternative you’re using too. But, really, at the end of the day you can still just put in whatever you have and tell it to make you a latte. The result will be tasty enough. If you care more than that, you can start making adjustments; just don’t try to dive too deep, it is fully automatic for a reason.

Remember when I said that if you wanted cold brew, you should get a dedicated machine for that? Well, here we are. This is the Cumulus and it’s overwhelmingly the easiest and quickest option for at home cold brew there is. It has three options that let you brew cold brew, nitro, and cold espresso. After just a 15 minute warm up (or cool down, I supposed) you’ll be able to brew any of the three options in just five minutes.

There is, however, a catch. The Cumulus makes really good cold brew because it brews from pods filled with coffee concentrate; not grounds. To do this you need to buy Cumulus’s proprietary pods because, right now, it is the only one in the game. That means you’re really at the whims of the company in terms of pricing. And if the company ever goes under, despite you loving your Cumulus, you won’t be able to keep using it without access to more pods.

Manual or Lever Machines (none featured): We could add a lever machine to this list. But, having tried and owned models, they are simply too fussy for the purposes of this story. If you’re on that level, we assume you don’t need this particular list.

Semi-automatic Espresso Machines: This is the bulk of the category. Everything from the La Marzocco your barista uses, the Lelit your boss raves about, and the entry-level Breville we mention. You are responsible for the entire process, so there is plenty of room to mess up, but they are not “manual” because of the existence of truly manual lever machines. If you have dreams about getting “serious” about your at-home barista hobby, this is the category you want to aim at.

Super Automatic Espresso Machines: This is the category where everything is done for you. Typically, you interact with a touch screen. You fill up the milk and water tanks, press cortado, and your drink is made for you. That’s it. You get to choose your beans and save money on expensive cafe drinks, but you’re not really tasked with much work. Get this if you don’t want to faff about with becoming a coffee guy.

What to Look for in High-End Coffee Makers

The size on some coffee machines can feel way different in person from how they appear online, particularly if you need to fill a reservoir or a burr grinder. You might need clearance, not just for the machine but for around the machine. When you're sizing your space, take into account the space on any side of the machine.

Size can be, but isn't necessarily, indicative of capacity. And when you get into these pricier high-end coffee machines, you'll notice capacity generally goes down. That's because the focus is on making a single great cup of coffee, often with methods that are more involved than a drip. When we are talking drip, we mean brew capacity, aka how much it can make at once. When talking espresso, it’s all about tank capacity; how much water can it hold at once before you need to refill?

For some people, automation is the entire point of getting a high-end coffee maker, as opposed to what we would call an espresso machine that's manual. And for that, we understand. Most of these are semi-automated, but we tried to include something for everyone, including one superautomatic machine.

#7025. This Is the Right Way to Do a Gallery Wall, According to a Framing Expert

It’s the artful update your space has been waiting for.

Framed art never goes out of style, and gallery walls are one of the go-to ways to display cherished photos and prints. For Tara Gavin, the founder of On the Wall Framing in Toronto, the secret to doing a gallery wall right is to make sure it has a “design direction.”

“My team and I love playing with scale, unexpected mat colors, different frame finishes, and the way photographs are positioned within a frame in order to keep them fresh,” Gavin says.

Gavin’s approach proves that gallery walls can look unexpectedly artful instead of off-kilter. Here’s how she intentionally designs gallery walls to make them a standout feature, plus, her three rules to follow so that you can do the same.

The easiest solution for any gallery wall is to stick to white backdrops and wood frames. And while a classic approach will always look great, Gavin wanted to give the above space an edge. She kept the familiar oak and black frames for some pieces, and then distinguished others with burgundy-hued mats and frames.

“The burgundy introduces warmth and richness, while the oak keeps everything from feeling too formal,” she says. “The black frames provide a little contrast and help tie the composition together.”

You can follow suit with the same palette, or you can use this pop of color to coordinate with a prominent shade in the surrounding furnishings—the results will be eye-catching, while still allowing the gallery wall to blend in with the rest of the room. “We tend to think of white as the default, but a rich burgundy, soft blue, olive green, or even a warm neutral can completely transform the look of a photograph,” Gavin adds.

One of the most interesting aspects of Gavin’s gallery wall approach is the use of negative space: the size of the mat around the image. She notes that it’s one of her team’s favorite tools for making a photo feel special, since it inadvertently makes you want to lean in for a better look at the shot. And given that the smaller size of the images naturally creates more room, Gavin also recommends using this as an opportunity to stick more than one photo into a frame. Then she’d play around with a composition that feels right.

“Rather than always centering a single photo, we often use multiple openings within one frame or place photos slightly off-center,” she says. “It's a small detail, but it makes the whole gallery wall feel more interesting and a little less predictable.”

Although it isn’t always necessary, Gavin recommends printing images in black and white for a uniform look, as was the case with the above project.

“One of the biggest challenges with family photographs is that they're often taken at different times, in different lighting conditions, and with completely different color palettes,” she says. “Converting them to black and white can bring a sense of consistency to the collection.”

Since you’ll already be planning out the positioning of your gallery wall, devote the same consideration to the photos that will go in it. The black-and-white composition will make the pictures look cohesive as a display, but the memories they stir up will be much more dynamic.

“People sometimes forget that the photographs sitting on their phones or tucked away in boxes can become some of the most meaningful art in their homes,” Gavin says. “You don't need an expensive art collection to create a beautiful wall. Sometimes the most compelling gallery walls are built around the memories and moments that matter most to you.”

#7021. Sophos Launches Tool for Shadow AI Defense

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos. In a recent product announcement, the company highlighted the growing use of shadow AI, referring to AI tools and agents operating without formal organizational approval or oversight. The security firm cited research indicating that 69% of organizations suspect or have evidence of unauthorized AI use.

Separate research from Gartner and Cloud Security Alliance found that 82% of organizations discovered shadow AI agents in their environments during the previous year.

According to the security firm, organizations have struggled to identify which AI tools employees use, what information those tools access, and whether their use complies with security policies.

"Organizations are adopting AI faster than they can secure it, and most security teams can't answer three basic questions: which AI tools are running, what data is going into them, and whether that activity lines up with policy," said Kyle Falkenhagen, senior vice president of product management at Sophos.

The company also cited an IDC projection that the number of AI agents could reach 1.3 billion by 2028.

Sophos researchers reported observing attackers use AI agents within a customer's network to build and test nearly 80 malware modules.

The cybersecurity firm announced the general availability of Sophos AI Defense, a security offering designed to identify AI tools, monitor their activity, and enforce organizational security policies.

The product operates through Sophos Fusion, the company's cybersecurity platform, and uses information from devices, networks, identity systems, and browsers.

According to the company, AI Defense can inspect prompts, uploads, and other AI interactions to identify potentially risky activity and help prevent sensitive data exposure.

For more information, go to the Sophos site.

#7018. The 14 Best Leather Colognes That Are Perfect for the Cold Weather Ahead

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Just as versatile as your favorite leather jacket.

You pass a shoe store, a real one with old-school styles, and catch that scent of new leather. You slip on an old beat up leather jacket and get that deep, squeaky earthy hit. You slide into a leather banquette at your town’s most popular restaurant. Ever wanted that scent, in any variety, bottled up? That’s what a leather fragrance gives you. Warm, rich, and earthy, and often with a smoky edge, they’re especially great for fall and winter, though the right one can work year-round.

Even vegetarians like myself can get behind a sexy leather cologne; they’re not distilling real animal leather, if you were wondering. Since leather has no extractable essential oil that can be used for perfume, perfumers recreate its scent using synthetic or botanical accords, often blended with smoky, resinous notes like birch tar, cade, and labdanum. It’s creating the idea of leather. The result can range from rugged and intense to soft and smooth like suede. There are plenty of different takes on leather fragrance out there, but these are our favorites.

This fragrance from Cremo mostly features white moss and amber, but the base notes of smooth suede come through at the end to create a warm, elevated aroma. Don’t be fooled by the low price point—you’ll still get a sophisticated scent of suede, though it’s less aggressive than its spicier and woodier alternatives.

This Vintage cologne from John Varvatos is about as manly as it gets. Its warm, woody overall scent with hints of patchouli and tobacco leaf underneath leave you with a long-lasting air of sophistication and refinement. The warm spice notes perfectly complement crisp autumn air while the patchouli and suede offer warmth during the winter like a fireplace in a cozy cabin.

There’s no mistaking the leather in this cologne—it permeates the entire package—but instead of being aggressive, it comes across as sophisticated. It’s a gentlemanly scent that’s surprisingly wearable for both day and night, thanks to the amber and patchouli at the base that give it a warm, herbal vibe. Think of it as less “cowboy” and more “oil baron.”

If Ombré Leather is Tom Ford’s easy, toned-down version of leather, Tuscan Leather is the aggressive, literal version. The leather almost overpowers everything else—almost—but the sweeter top notes allow for a delicious opening that adds smoothness to the leather and the warm, woody dry down lends a layered elegance. The leather here is top quality. Not new car smell, but perfectly worn-in bespoke shoes or the seats of a vintage Ferrari.

At first spray, the fruity plum and peach top notes take over, but pretty soon they give way to a deeper, more layered fragrance that really does call to mind the stacks of an old library. The sweetness doesn’t entirely go away, but as the florals and leather appear, like pages turning in a book, more depth is revealed.

Designed as an enhancer—a lighter fragrance to layer with others to change the vibe—this interpretation of leather smells almost exactly like the real thing. It’s slick, smooth, and warm, with a sharp edge that’s masculine in a ’70s Camaro kind of way. Sure, you can wear it as it was meant (that is, with another cologne), but we say try it by itself to really drive that leather home.

Tru Western’s Leather Vintage Label cologne collection is a great place to start when it comes to leather fragrances. Each of the five scents highlights a different aspect of leather, but No. 1 brings out woody notes—and a bit of fresh clementine—to give the leather accord a classic, straightforward backdrop that lets the leather accord be the star player. For something a bit richer with amber notes, try No. 4 (Deep Leather).

We’re big fans of Ranger Station, the Nashville-based niche fragrance brand known for its nature-inspired scents—and it all started with the Leather + Pine candle. After the candle took off, Ranger Station of course had to turn the scent into a wearable fragrance. Slightly different from the candle’s scent, the EDP is a blend of tanned leather, pine sap, amber, and sandalwood, which creates an alluring blend of sweet and savory.

We can’t talk about leather colognes without mentioning Malin + Goetz’s famous take on the scent. Simply called “leather,” it’s a rugged blend of florals, spices, and woods that emphasizes pepper and lily of the valley along with leather accord. And while it features a handful of floral notes, it never leans into powdery territory; it remains a solidly masculine scent, though it suits pretty much everyone.

For a bit of a more intense, spicy take on leather, Desert Nights is the cologne to go with. It’s inspired by Marrakech after sunset, layering luxurious oud and sandalwood with earthy patchouli and moss, and the spices up top carry through. It’s seductive and perfect for a date night or winter outing.

Arquiste is known for complex fragrances inspired by historical events and specific landscapes, and Nocturnality is one of our favorites—we even included it in our 2025 Cologne Awards. The inspiration for this one comes from mid-’90s New York City nightlife; picture an underground club with humid air, booze, cigarette smoke, and lots of people dancing. In a less abstract sense, that means latex, leather, and oud, which combine for a dark, sexy scent.

Most leather colognes lean on warmth, sweetness, and spice to complement the leather notes (which is partly why you often see a shift toward leather colognes in the winter), but this one does things a bit differently. Yes, there’s spice in the opening, but there’s also citrus that moves on to florals in the middle before drying down to earthy, musky notes. The leather is apparent all the way through, but it feels lighter and fresher than many others on this list.

There’s something very nostalgic about this fragrance but it doesn’t smell old-fashioned. Instead, it calls to mind dark rooms, sexy rendezvous, sweaty nights, and skin tight jeans. It’s smoky and spicy, but layered with musk and pungent black leather for an über masculine vibe that’s impossible to ignore. When you wear it, expect to be noticed—and for the compliments to come nonstop.

Perfumehead’s scents, like all the best fragrances, tell stories and paint pictures. This boozy, smoky juice is inspired by the cowboy-filled honky-tonks of the 1970s Sunset Strip, and one whiff is more than enough to take you there. The final effect of the fragrance isn’t especially Western or aggressive, but more cozy and sensual, like finding a vintage leather jacket that miraculously fits you like a glove.

#7016. Unraveling All the Details Behind Wicker’s Wicker

We asked the artists behind Alexander Skarsgård’s woven-to-order suit how they built it—and kept his appeal intact.

“It will take her another lifetime to understand wicker,” Marella Agnelli reportedly said of fellow socialite Mercedes Bass’s apartment. With Alexander Skarsgård’s help, some of us are getting there a little faster.

In Wicker, the romantic comedy based on Ursula Wills’s short story “The Wicker Husband,” Olivia Colman plays a fisherwoman who commissions a husband from the local basketmaker (Peter Dinklage). Skarsgård plays her woven-to-order spouse, whose resemblance to patio furniture does surprisingly little to diminish his sex appeal. Directed by Eleanor Wilson and Alex Huston Fischer, the film comes out in theaters today.

Behind the wicker transformation is Wētā Workshop, the New Zealand design and effects company whose credits include The Lord of the Rings and Avatar. Its artists make the costumes, props, and creatures that give imaginary worlds something tangible—and, in this case, marriageable.

For prosthetic makeup designer Joe Dunckley and lead makeup effects artist Sarah Rubano, the assignment was to turn Skarsgård into a convincing wicker husband without losing the expressions that make him recognizable. The team spent 18 months developing and manufacturing his suit and facial prosthetics, translating his features into a weave, right down to his furrowed brows.

Naturally, we had questions. How do you make wicker sexy? What happens to the eyelashes at lunch? And could anyone involved look at a household basket the same way again? Below, Dunckley and Rubano explain the work behind the wicker.

Before we get to the cheekbones, how did this assignment arrive?

Dunckley: We were approached by Eleanor Wilson and Alex Huston Fischer about 18 months out from filming. Initially, they were after a piece of concept art—a beautiful piece that they could take to potential cast members. This was part of their development process, so they had a tool for helping show their vision for the project.

Pretty quickly after that came the question: How are you going to make this wicker husband character? So much of the film’s success hinges on the success of that character. They always wanted to make the wicker husband a physical prosthetic character. That led us into a process of research and development. It was an 18-month period of R&D and manufacture to get to the final product.

A husband made to order. What, exactly, was in the order?

Dunckley: About 95% of the prosthetic and fabricated suit is made out of a 3D-printed material. Most prosthetic materials are manufactured to imitate human skin, but the wicker husband is built of wicker, not flesh and bones, so we knew we needed something different.

Sometimes we found the limits of existing programs and had to write our own code for weaving. Weaving is such complex geometry that it was very challenging for sculptors to weave in 3D.

The prosthetic appliances were then printed directly, without needing to go through the mold-making process.

Rubano: On [Skarsgård’s] body, we had about 50 individual pieces stitched and anchored onto thin spandex or mesh. The face, including the wig, was about 10 individual pieces. We also used foam latex for the lips, eye bags, and underlip and chin piece.

Did anyone get to weave an actual basket, or was this strictly a digital operation?

Dunckley: Absolutely. We had a team of weavers who had just come off Avatar, where they’d been weaving costumes for the Na’vi. Our design team would block out the shapes that we liked, and our lead weaver would do a physical weave sample using flax, giving us those shapes.

We’d print, do a makeup test, and learn what we learned: This one’s too loose. This one’s too tight and it binds. We’ll need more movement here. Slowly, we developed what you ended up seeing in the film.

I had a crush on the wicker husband. How do you make wicker handsome?

Dunckley: One of our main objectives was to make sure Alexander shines through the makeup. He’s a handsome man, and he has an expressive quality that’s really unique and recognizable.

We wanted to get those qualities into the weave. It was a matter of deconstructing what it is about those expressions that are endearing, that are heart-melting, and then reconstructing them in a weave pattern and enabling that movement for him through the prosthetic.

How long did it take for Alexander Skarsgård to become the wicker husband every day of filming?

Rubano: We got the whole morning process down to an hour and a half. That was the brief at the beginning, because he was working every day. It was an ambitious shooting schedule, so all the build techniques supported being able to expedite it.

He would come in, and we would put on a partial fabricated undersuit. He would come to the makeup chair, then go back and get more of the body suit on. Then we got into gluing the appliances to his face, the eyelashes, the wig, the styling, the makeup around the eyes—all the finishing touches.

And at lunch? Surely even a wicker husband gets a break.

Rubano: About halfway through, once we were really confident with our processes, we started giving him a bit of relief at lunchtime. I would take off the eyelashes because they’re quite robust, and he’s not used to wearing fake eyelashes. We would wrap up his beard and put a little bib over it so he could eat and be comfortable. We’d take off the gloves—just all the things we could do to give him some reprieve. After lunch, we’d build him up 100% again. The cleanup at the end of the day was about half an hour. The real challenge was keeping him cool. He runs hot, so we had fans and ice packs.

How do you feel about all the online chatter about “sexy wicker”?

Dunckley: We never would have expected this kind of level, to be honest. It’s been quite a ride to watch the enthusiasm, all the excitement, all the memes. We sat around watching memes for hours and laughing our heads off.

Rubano: We’ve built so many different types of characters for so many different types of films over 30-plus years. Sometimes they strike a chord as hoped, sometimes they don’t. Sometimes the chord has been struck louder than you could ever have expected. This is an example of that.

Has this changed how you look at the wicker in your own home?

Rubano: I have this tiny little wicker basket, and it’s not necessarily sexy, but I do love it. I look at it with love and adoration. It’s just this tiny little basket I put all my jewelry bits and pieces in. But, of course, the hottest basket of all is Alexander Skarsgård. Definitely.

Julia Cancilla is the social media & news editor at ELLE Decor, where she oversees the brand's socials and covers design, pop culture, and emerging trends. She also authors the monthly ELLE Decoroscope column. Her work has appeared in Inked magazine, House Beautiful, Marie Claire, and more.

#7015. 5 MAJOR Mistakes You're Making in Your Living Room Design, According to Designers

A living room has to accommodate a lot of life. Truly a multipurpose space, it needs to be a quiet theater for TV, a bespoke den for hosting, and a cozy place to lounge or read. With all the roles that the space has to satisfy, it’s no wonder homeowners struggle to find the perfect way to decorate.

“A beautiful living room that’s uncomfortable to sit in, difficult to navigate, or poorly lit will never truly feel stylish, no matter how visually appealing it may be,” says designer Mindy Kelson O’Connor. “No matter how beautiful a room is, if people have to do gymnastics to make it work, absolutely no one will focus on the pretty elements.”

Designers are often called in when style and function become too incongruent to ignore, so you can bet that they’ve seen their share of tragic, tacky, and disorganized living rooms. “A room that’s not functional is quite literally useless,” says Rachel Sherman, principal at Rachel Sloane Interiors. “Think about rooms that you love hanging out in, then consider why. It’s probably because they're easy and comfortable to be in, while also feeling beautiful.”

If that vision doesn’t match your current reality, read on as four designers share the biggest mistakes you could be making in your living room—and how to fix them. Put this advice into practice, and your living room may actually do it all.

In the rush to get settled into your home, there’s a good chance that the layout you landed on when you first moved in is still the layout you’re working with now. But if the furniture resembles an obstacle course or creates the feeling of too much airiness, it’s time to reconsider where everything is positioned.

“Furniture that doesn't encourage conversation, or doesn't consider flow, can feel uninviting. A good layout supports both socializing and solitude,” says Sarah Magness, the principal and founder of Studio Magness. The layout should be as intuitive as possible, and there should always be a surface near a seat where you can place a drink.

Do you entertain often? Sherman would make sure you have seating solutions for every guest, whether that means stashing two ottomans between the coffee table and fireplace (she recommends moveable pieces!) or upgrading a loveseat to a sectional. Don’t get too caught up in how things are “supposed to” look; find a solution based on how people will move through the room.

“It’s easy to become overly focused on symmetry or anchoring everything around a central fireplace or architectural element,” O’Connor says. “But a truly functional living room prioritizes conversation and ease. Designing solely for a visual focal point often means sacrificing seating arrangements that foster relaxed socializing.”

While overhead lighting makes it easy to transition from day to night, it doesn’t do much to add ambiance. “This makes a living room feel like a conference room instead of a warm gathering in someone’s home,” Sherman says. If you’d rather feel warm and cozy—and this is the place for that, after all—then invest in alternative sources.

“Layered lighting is so important, especially in a living room, because it allows for flexibility and creates mood,” O’Connor says. “A central overhead decorative fixture or two, depending on the space’s length, is great for lively parties or game nights. It can also create visual interest and style.” Add a floor lamp beside a side chair for reading, a table lamp near a sectional, and a pair of sconces flanking a fireplace, and you’re all set.

No one wants to worry about stains on their sofa when friends are over to watch the big game, and yet, it’s common to secretly wish for those squeaky plastic sofa covers of the past whenever there’s a party. If you’re someone who wants to guarantee that furniture stays pristine while hosting regularly, then consider investing in finishes that let you exhale.

“Using precious or high-maintenance fabrics discourages everyday use,” Magness says. “Fortunately, many high-performance fabrics are now designed to handle everyday wear and spills, so there's no need to sacrifice durability for style.”

Speaking of creating harmony between form and function, be honest about how comfortable your sofa truly is. If it feels too rigid, too deep, or too shallow, then it might be time for a change. “An elegant sofa loses its charm if guests are constantly adjusting or grimacing while seated,” O’Connor adds.

The textiles of a living room can really make or break the space, particularly when it comes to the size of the rug. It’s not unheard of to have one that’s too small, making it so the coffee table floats on top while the rest of the furniture awkwardly surrounds it. “This unintentionally makes the room feel smaller,” Sherman says.

The replacement rug should be large enough to sit at the center of the room, with the main furnishings all touching it. “Items against the wall don’t need the rug underneath as well, but the center of the room should have abundant coverage,” Sherman adds. A well-sized rug will make the room feel more complete, alongside pillows for softness and layering. If you do fall in love with an impractical fabric, this is your opportunity to go for it. “Use highly decorative or expensive fabrics for drapery or decorative pillows,” Magness says. Again, it’s all about finding a balance.

Interior designer Louis Lin wants you to stop putting your sofa’s back against the wall, literally. Instead of focusing on maximizing the amount of floor space, Lin suggests shifting your attention to the room’s “spatial dialogue.”

“The goal isn’t to maximize emptiness, but rather to choreograph the spatial flow and create visual rhythm,’ says Lin. “Always float a sofa off the wall, even by a few inches to reduce visual clutter.”

Intentionally placing your furniture not only leads to a more curated feel, but also allows you to create pathways for physical and visual flow, minimizing overcrowding and maximizing ease. After all, you shouldn’t be dodging corners in your own home.

#7014. Microsoft Expands AI Security Platform to Address Agentic AI Security Risks

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

In a company blog post, Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security, outlined how Microsoft plans to use Project Perception to monitor, manage, and contain what it calls "agentic insiders." These are AI agents deployed within an environment that can access data, use software tools, and make decisions without constant human supervision. While they can improve productivity, Microsoft warns that they also introduce security risks, even when they haven't been compromised by an attacker.

"Agents are unpredictable," Gallot wrote. "They will adapt as the context changes, may not behave as intended, and will collaborate and collude with other agents to reach their objectives, without needing to be compromised."

Project Perception was first introduced in July. The platform, which entered public preview Aug. 3, is designed to help security teams respond to increasingly sophisticated cyber threats using AI-powered automation.

Instead of leaning only on traditional security alerts and manual investigations, Project Perception uses three kinds of specialized AI agents working together. Red team agents hunt for possible vulnerabilities, blue team agents look into suspicious activity, and green team agents step in to fix problems and strengthen defenses.

To spot threats and respond faster, the system pulls in security data from identities, endpoints, cloud environments, applications, and other sources.

Now Microsoft wants to use those same capabilities on the AI agents already running inside enterprise networks. Project Perception would find those agents, give them identities and permissions, and keep an eye on what they do across different systems. It would also keep assessing the risks that come with their actions.

Microsoft also described a plan for "Guardian Agents," specialized AI agents that supervise other agents and step in when their behavior gets risky. That might mean limiting an agent's permissions or blocking certain actions before they cause harm.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," Gallot wrote.

The initiative also reflects concerns raised by Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella in an Oct. 10 essay on AI security.

Nadella argued that enterprises should treat powerful AI models much like potential insider security risks, particularly when those models can access confidential information or perform critical business operations.

"We simply can't outsource responsibility for what intelligence does on our behalf," Nadella wrote.

He called for security controls that operate independently of AI models, including restricted permissions, detailed activity logging, and mechanisms that allow administrators to stop an agent's actions.

"In other words, we need to separate the supply of intelligence from the authority over it," Nadella wrote.

Microsoft admits that watching what AI agents do is only part of the answer. Gallot said that future security tools will also need to show why AI models make the choices they do, not just what actions they end up taking.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

Microsoft is combining its security information and event management and threat protection products within Microsoft Defender, creating what the company calls an Integrated Security Operations Center built for an era of AI-driven attacks.

A recent Cloud Security Alliance report urges organizations to inventory, govern, and continuously monitor machine and agent identities.

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT.

#7012. Tim McGraw Knows How to Make a Marriage Work

“There are times you don’t get along ... but that’s not a reason to walk out the door,” the country singer says in a new episode of “What I’ve Learned.”

Tim McGraw wants to set the record straight. He’s heard the rumors that he performed at the fabled Taylor Swift and Travis Kelce wedding this summer. But the three-time Grammy Award winner and recent inductee to the Country Music Hall of Fame reveals now that the headlines got it wrong.

“I didn’t perform at her wedding,” McGraw tells Esquire editor in chief Michael Sebastian in a new episode of “What I’ve Learned,” which you can listen to here. “That was a rumor,” he says.

But the “Live Like You Were Dying” singer confirms that he did attend the wedding with his wife, fellow country music powerhouse Faith Hill, as they celebrate 30 years of marriage together. He wishes the same for Swift. “Taylor’s always been close to our family, our girls,” McGraw says. “We couldn’t be happier for her to find Travis, who’s a cool guy as well.”

The lore between Tim McGraw and Taylor Swift goes back to the start of her own music career, with her 2006 single “Tim McGraw.” Then, in 2013, the two recorded McGraw’s “Highway Don’t Care” for his album Taste of Country. “She’s a special person, and one of the most talented people I’ve ever met,” McGraw says in the episode. “She’s the biggest artist in the world ... but the biggest thing that maybe gets overlooked sometimes with Taylor is the way she writes to connect with her audience. [It] is such an incredible thing.”

But did they leave the newlyweds any advice? What’s the secret to making a marriage work for three decades?

“Just a commitment that you want it to work,” McGraw tells Esquire. “People are so easy to walk away from troubled times or bad times, or times where you don’t get along. But going into a marriage and being in a marriage for a long time, you start to realize that, yeah, there are gonna be times you don’t like each other. There are times you don’t get along ... but that’s not a reason to walk out the door.”

According to McGraw, “We made a commitment early on in our lives that [leaving] wasn’t going to be our first response to any kind of fight or any kind of disagreement. Because the more you do that, that starts opening the door for that choice. It lessens the commitment, and it lessens the pride and respect that you have in your marriage.”

The singer adds that people change over time, which should not be a deal-breaker for any marriage. “You change as you grow older. And you change individually as you grow older, and you change as a couple as you grow older in time spent together. And so the beauty, I think, in a long marriage, is you get the chance to fall in love with a new person every so often. Because people change.”

The rest of McGraw’s “What I’ve Learned” conversation covers his growth as a father (which required him to learn patience, “another level of love that you didn’t know existed”), adapting to an empty-nest household, and the story of how he learned he would be inducted into the Country Music Hall of Fame.

He also offers some advice to today’s younger men. “Just get out, be social,” McGraw says. “Being a man is how you treat people and certainly how you treat women.” His 30 years of marriage can attest to that.

#7011. This Designer’s Long Island Family Home Has Been 40 Years in the Making

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Phillip Thomas’s Bellport retreat is a laboratory for color, collecting, and the occasional improbable antique.

Phillip Thomas’s first shop had a captive clientele. As a child, he commandeered the mudroom of his family’s house in Bellport, New York, filled it with yard-sale finds he had repainted or refinished, and ushered his parents’ friends inside. He called the enterprise Phillip & Co. (“There was no Co.,” he says. It was just him.) His customers, he recalls, were more or less obligated to buy something.

The Phillip & Co. space is now a pantry for extra tableware, but Thomas is still rearranging the house around it. His family has owned the late-19th-century home for 40 years, and the New York–based interior designer returns almost every weekend. The four-bedroom house includes two porches, a sunny sitting room overlooking the bay, and a living room anchored by a large fireplace. Thomas still sleeps in his childhood bedroom when there, and his parents, siblings, nieces, and friends gather at the property regularly, too.

Thomas’s Manhattan apartment is white and contemporary, filled with Latin American art. Bellport called for something different: “an American cottage by the sea,” he says. “I really just want to feel comfortable and I want everyone around me to feel at ease.” Getting there has involved decades of experimentation, a few unlikely purchases, and regular consultation with a formidable client committee: his family. “It’s a democracy,” he says. “It’s not a dictatorship.”

His love of Bellport extends well beyond the borders of his family’s property. The South Shore village has long been a mecca for creatives. The South Bay Art Association held its first official meeting at the village’s community center in 1957. Thomas, who has been coming to Bellport since he was four, remembers spending time in that same building as a child. In 2019, he led the renovation of its basement bowling alley and recreation space through the Bellport Village Program Fund.

There, he took Long Island’s Great South Bay as a starting point, introducing blue floors punctuated with red circles that evoked buoys, which village workers helped paint. The project restored a childhood gathering place for another generation, with color doing much of the work on a limited budget. It is a public expression of the instincts that have shaped his family’s house: keep what matters, reconsider what no longer works, and make the result inviting enough that people will use it.

At home, those decisions are rarely final. The dining room has gone from lacquered hunter green to red to its current painted pattern, where undulating lines surround a large, exuberantly colored painting. Elsewhere, Thomas used stencils to give walls the irregularity he seeks out in handmade things. “Having something painted onto the walls gives the room soul,” he says. “There is perfection in imperfection.”

The collections have accumulated on similar terms. Antique tools, gathered with his father over decades, line a hallway in carefully ordered arrangements. Thomas admires them as objects made by hand to facilitate other handmade things; displayed together, their worn wooden forms are sculptural. In the powder room, a coal-mining cart found a second career as a basin, paired with a vintage mirror frame fitted with glass cut to accommodate the plumbing. “You’d be surprised what I’ll put in the back of my car,” he says.

Other pieces traveled even farther. A campy painting of dogs playing poker came with his mother’s family from Yugoslavia to northern Chile, then to Santiago. After his grandmother died, the family agreed it belonged in Bellport, where it hangs in Thomas’s studio. The family’s routines shift with the seasons, which in turn shape how they use the house. Winter draws everyone toward the living-room fireplace, where the sofa is covered in a Schumacher fabric reminiscent of an American rag rug and throw pillows are made from textiles Thomas found in Turkey. From May through October, life moves out to the porch, where varied flexible seating facilitates long dinners and several conversations at once.

For the porch fireplace, Thomas collaborated with local artist Jay Sylvester on a sculpture of foraged branches surrounding a bull’s-eye mirror. The reflection ensures that even someone seated with their back to the bay gets a view.

Even the garden has benefited from his instinct to collect. Thomas acquired Chilean sculptures, including a Corten-steel piece inspired by wind-gnarled trees. For the latest overhaul, he collaborated with John Beitel of Landspace Inc., who has tended the gardens for 35 years. Together, they shaped a landscape where formal beds give way to looser planting along paths that frame views of the water, with red dogwood branches providing color through winter.

For all its evolution, the house’s greater purpose has remained remarkably consistent. Thomas’s nieces now invite their own friends; bedrooms are invariably full on summer weekends; and everyone helps cook or set the table. His mother, Sava, spends time on the porch before the household wakes, with a cup of coffee and the breeze coming off the water. After 40 years, she is still photographing the view. “I take photos and photos and they’re all the same,” she says. “But it’s beautiful.”

Julia Cancilla is the social media & news editor at ELLE Decor, where she oversees the brand's socials and covers design, pop culture, and emerging trends. She also authors the monthly ELLE Decoroscope column. Her work has appeared in Inked magazine, House Beautiful, Marie Claire, and more.

#7009. ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Pro Unexpectedly Skipping Disney Night Caused “Chaos” on Set

Here’s how the show reworked 3 dances in the 11th hour.

Dancing With the Stars was down one pro during Disney Night! The Next Pro winner Adele Zaikman told fans she’d be skipping Monday night’s show, explaining in an Instagram Stories statement that she’s sick.

“I’m sadly feeling unwell and won’t be performing in the opening number on Dancing with the Stars tomorrow,” she wrote. “I’m so sad to miss a show day, but I’ll be taking this time to take care of myself. Sending so much love to everyone on that ballroom floor. I’ll be cheering you on from home.”

Cut to The Sun, which reports that Adele’s absence “left producers and dancers racing to rework multiple performances,” with the rehearsals “becoming even more chaotic” since Adele was booked for three different dances.

“She had to drop out very last minute, so that mixed with the week already being one day shorter has been really hectic behind the scenes,” a source said. “Multiple troupe members are going to fill in for her. The opening number was the big one. The other two she just had a tiny part, so it wasn’t as big of a deal to fill. But the troupe has been practicing all Monday before showtime.”

The night ended up going off pretty flawlessly despite Adele’s absence, and she’ll presumably be back next week (when Dancing With the Stars returns in its usual Tuesday night time slot), but no confirmation just yet.

#7005. Survey: Organizations Report Positive ROI on AI Investments, Yet Data Readiness and Oversight Lag Behind

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

The company's recent State of Enterprise AI Maturity research surveyed 515 U.S.-based AI decision-makers. Just 5% believed their organizations' data was largely ready for AI.

The findings suggest that gaining value from individual AI applications and preparing to operate them more broadly are separate challenges. Reported returns alone do not establish that an organization has the foundations to sustain or expand those results.

Further found that 37% of respondents' organizations had paused, scaled back, or abandoned an underperforming AI initiative in the previous 12 months. Among that group, data quality or availability was the most frequently cited main reason, at 24%.

Meanwhile, 29% reported completing a formal data audit and remediation plan. The research also placed 69% of organizations in the exploring, experimenting, or implementing stages of adoption.

Further distinguishes data prepared for conventional reporting from data prepared for AI. Its explanation emphasizes data origins and documentation, along with access controls that support ongoing evaluation and governance.

For readers assessing their own readiness, the practical issue is whether data supports the requirements of a particular application. A collection suitable for a dashboard may need additional preparation before it can reliably support an AI system.

Seventy-seven percent reported a positive return on AI investment. However, only 3% said their organizations had a documented AI-audit process.

The findings measure different things: perceived financial benefit and the existence of a repeatable oversight process. Neither substitutes for the other.

"In reality, confidence without a documented process is exposure," said Kierra Dotson, associate director of AI strategy and governance at Further. "Closing that gap doesn't take new technology; it requires an auditable governance process."

For the full report, visit the Further site (registration required).

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

Microsoft is combining its security information and event management and threat protection products within Microsoft Defender, creating what the company calls an Integrated Security Operations Center built for an era of AI-driven attacks.

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT.

#7001. Does The Pitt Need More Romance?

Amy Poehler told Noah Wyle on her podcast that the actor should add a little steam to the medical drama. We agree.

Want to know one of the reasons why shows like ER and Grey’s Anatomy were such huge hits? Romance. People love to see people kissing! It’s hot! But The Pitt, a serious drama on HBO praised for its handling of grief and real-life healthcare problems, still only toys with the idea that their doctors could smooch.

That hasn’t stopped fans from setting characters up together. But when Amy Poehler directly asked The Pitt star Noah Wyle about the lack of romance, the four-time Emmy winner—who came on Poehler’s podcast Good Hang to promote that he’s People’s “Sexiest Man Alive” this year—responded that they’re more concerned with putting together a realistic medical drama.

“If you're gonna show so much guts and blood, if you're gonna go there, which you go there, and it's incredible, your makeup team is incredible, the stuff you do is bananas ... but if you're gonna show that much, every time you do that, you got to get a little smooch,” Poehler suggested. “Someone's got to do a little smooch.”

When Wyle joked that the blood-to-romance ratio could be “four-to-one, but certainly not one-to-one,” Poehler insisted, “You’re gonna show the guts, you gotta show some butts.” Wyle fired back by saying the show does have naked butts, but Poehler argued it should be steamier.

Wyle conceded: “We don’t show those kinds of butts.”

To be honest, Poehler is onto something. In contrast to Wyle’s previous hospital drama ER, which was chock-full of romantic subplots, The Pitt is devoid of that sort of soap opera business. While that’s allowed The Pitt to be a “serious” show about the grave responsibility of emergency doctors and frontline healthcare workers, it’s still television. This is an entertainment medium built on spectacle and fantasies. People are lonelier than ever, with men and women opting out of dating. We crave touch!

Sure, we might tune in to The Pitt anyway. But while we’re here, why can’t we also see Dr. Mohan (Supriya Ganesh) and Dr. Abbott (Shawn Hatosy) get a little frisky? Dr. Robby (Wyle) clearly had a situationship brewing with case manager Noelle Hastings (Meta Golding) in season 2. What happened there?

Production on The Pitt season 3 is set to begin this very week in Pittsburgh, so it may be too late for the show’s writers to incorporate Poehler’s (correct) note about kissing and butts. But maybe we can hope that the creators have heard the legion of very outspoken Pitt fans and wrote in a few treats for those who live and die by their favorite “ships” from the show. If you don’t know what that means, trust me when I say it will probably never influence how you already watch The Pitt.

I’ll be honest: The project was anything but easy, though that was partly because I tackled it solo. Yes, having a second set of hands would have made the process much smoother, as peeling off the backing and applying the contact paper can get tricky.

Whether you’re taking on the project alone or with a helping hand, here’s step one: Clean your countertops so the paper goes on smoothly. I’d also suggest buying this smoothing tool kit from Amazon, as it was a real game-changer for me. When you’re ready to get started, peel back a small section of the backing and carefully press the adhesive side onto the counter. I worked little by little, using the smoothing tool to eliminate any air bubbles as I went. There are also grid lines on the backing, making it easy to measure and trim the paper to fit your countertops.

My countertops before I installed the contact paper.

Mine are far from perfect, but I don’t regret installing them in the slightest. Not only have they brightened up my kitchen tenfold, but they also haven’t budged in the year or so I’ve had them. If marble doesn’t align with your style, there are a slew of other iterations on Amazon, from wood finishes to stark white. The beauty of this contact paper lies in its versatility—it can also double as wallpaper or be used to spruce up a nightstand or dresser. I’ll leave the decision up to you.

#6999. Frank Lloyd Wright’s Only Planetarium Reopens in Florida After 6-Year Restoration

The Florida Southern College landmark is open for the first time in more than two decades.

The only planetarium that Frank Lloyd Wright ever built shut down in 2004, more than four decades after the famous American architect completed the dome-topped structure on the grounds of Florida Southern College (FSC). The projector died, according to the Lakeland-based school, rendering the building functionally obsolete.

Now, the entire planetarium—with a new, state-of-the-art projector—just emerged from a six-year restoration and is once again welcoming students and the public.

Led by campus architect Jeff Baker of MCWB Architects, the long-awaited project is part of a larger effort by FSC to revive all 13 of its buildings by Wright, who conceived a master plan called “Child of the Sun” for the Lakeland college between 1938 and 1958. Complete with academic buildings, esplanades, chapels, and a library, the 113-acre campus is home to the largest collection of Wright-designed buildings anywhere in the world. In 2012, the National Park Service granted National Historic Landmark status to the school.

The two-phase renovation of the planetarium, which is located in the Polk Science Building, began in 2021 with the exterior. The team updated the roof, flashing (weatherproofing), and stucco cladding, and replaced 280 custom textile-block tiles on the facade. Wright developed his signature textile-block system in the 1920s, applying geometric patterns to hand-cast concrete tiles and then sheathing both exteriors and interiors of his buildings to create a tactile, woven look. The planetarium’s tiles are among the architect’s most straightforward, defined by simple horizontal bands and a vertical “stitching” motif.

By 2023, the project moved indoors. The main objective with the interior was to restore Wright’s original vision, which had been augmented through the years with awkward additions, like a computer closet from the 1980s.

Now, formerly deep-blue walls are a calming neutral hue, while the flooring is back to “Cherokee Red,” another Wright signature found throughout many of his Usonian designs. Local artisans also collaborated on restoring the structure’s 60-seat theater, reviving the original circular floor plan with a seating system crafted out of plywood and red vinyl upholstery.

Capping off the project is the addition of a new Cosm projector and Bowens sound system, both of which provide state-of-the-art functionality befitting a modern planetarium. Perhaps best of all, it’s not just FSC students who will benefit: The public can experience the planetarium as intended through a series of four rotating shows each week. Among the scheduled offerings is “Destination Mars: The New Frontier,” which focuses on humanity’s epic endeavors to the Red Planet.

“Reopening the planetarium allows us to bring an important part of Florida Southern’s history back to life while creating new opportunities for our students and the community,” said Tory King, director of tourism operations and visitor experience at FSC, in a statement. “The architectural restoration brought the space back, and the new technology now allows us to return it to active educational and public use.”

Tickets are $20 per person and $15 per person for families and groups of four or more.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6997. Researchers Find AI Is Accelerating Vulnerability Discovery

AI-powered vulnerability research is contributing to a major increase in security updates, raising concerns that IT teams could fall behind as patching demands grow, according to cybersecurity company ThreatDown.

In a recent blog post, the company stated that in September 2026, vendors addressed 998 common vulnerabilities and exposures (CVEs) around Patch Tuesday, compared with 96 during the same period last year. In addition, the three-month rolling average of patched Linux kernel vulnerabilities tripled year over year, while Google's Chrome browser experienced a 36-fold increase.

The researchers point to advanced AI models, including Anthropic's Claude Mythos Preview, as a major factor behind the increase in vulnerability discovery.

Introduced in April 2026, Mythos Preview is being used through Project Glasswing, an initiative involving Microsoft, the Linux Foundation and other organizations to identify vulnerabilities in critical software.

While AI could ultimately make software more secure, the cybersecurity company warns that vulnerability discovery is accelerating faster than organizations can deploy fixes.

IT teams must still evaluate patches, test compatibility and schedule updates without disrupting critical business operations.

The challenge is particularly significant for organizations already struggling with patch management.

The cybersecurity vendor cites Verizon's 2026 Data Breach Investigations Report, which found that 35% of known actively exploited vulnerabilities remained unpatched after 28 days in 2025.

Ryan Powell, vice president of IT and security at ThreatDown, said automated patching systems can help organizations manage the growing workload. However, additional patches still require more testing and validation, increasing operational overhead and the risks associated with frequent system changes.

The report also highlights the risks of vulnerabilities in widely used software components such as OpenSSL, embedded in applications, network devices, and security products.

Unlike standalone applications, these components often depend on downstream vendors to release updates before organizations can deploy fixes.

The security firm's analysis points to CVE-2022-0778, an OpenSSL vulnerability patched in March 2022, as an example. Some affected products did not receive updates until several months later.

As AI accelerates vulnerability discovery, these dependencies could delay remediation and extend organizations' exposure to known security flaws.

ThreatDown's findings illustrate a growing challenge for enterprise cybersecurity: AI is making vulnerabilities easier to discover but fixing them remains a complex and time-consuming process.

For the full report, visit the ThreatDown blog here.

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

Microsoft is combining its security information and event management and threat protection products within Microsoft Defender, creating what the company calls an Integrated Security Operations Center built for an era of AI-driven attacks.

A recent Cloud Security Alliance report urges organizations to inventory, govern, and continuously monitor machine and agent identities.

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT.
