#6995. A Jewel-Toned Chicago Home Inspired by Pakistani Culture

Kate Haynes of Noble Studio brought bold personality to her client’s family home.

A room painted in rich eggplant isn’t exactly a subtle introduction. But in this Wilmette, Illinois, home, that’s precisely the point. Just beyond the foyer, a dining room drenched in Farrow & Ball’s Brinjal, complete with a glossy lacquered ceiling, offers the first sign that the designer at the wheel had little interest in playing it safe. And, as Kate Haynes of Noble Studio quickly discovered, neither did her client.

The pair connected over Instagram as construction on the family’s 6,500-square-foot home neared completion. With the architectural details and hard finishes already in place, Haynes was tasked with giving the new build some personality. Luckily, the homeowner, a former graphic designer, had plenty to spare. “We talked about incorporating deeply saturated colors along with a lot of personal touches,” recalls Haynes. “She is one of the warmest, most delightful people, and her home totally reflects her personality.”

What Haynes created for the family is a masterclass in exuberance, informed in part by the homeowner’s Pakistani heritage. Rather than lean into overt cultural references, Haynes borrowed from the richness of Mughal architecture and the bold, jewel-toned palettes of South Asia, weaving both together throughout the five-bedroom home. In the daughter’s bedroom, a scalloped headboard recalls a multifoil arch; elsewhere, vintage rugs lend the new construction a sense of history. “We wanted to have that original depth that can only come from a textile that has seen some life and years,” Haynes explains.

There’s plenty of levity, too: a hanging chair in the blue reading room, a rock wall in the playroom, and a custom circular banquette that rivals the best restaurant in town. Each detail speaks to a family that takes its pleasures seriously, whether that’s gathering with friends for dinner or curling up with a book. “She was just a person who looks for things to be delighted by,” Haynes says of her client. Consider the house a reflection of that philosophy.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Noble StudioBuilder: Sterling Wilson DevelopmentLocation: Wilmette, ILThe Space: A five-bedroom, seven-bathroom home across 6,500 square feet

An expansive crane mural sets a serene, transportive mood in the primary suite, where “subtle eastern influence and massive graphic appeal” establish an earthy palette, says Haynes. The blush pink upholstered bed is flanked by khaki green Lawson-Fenning nightstands, while a rustic vintage Indian teak bench adds a timeworn counterpoint to the room'så more refined furnishings.

Cornflower blue wainscoting and patterned Penny Morrison wallpaper establish a cheerful backdrop in the daughter’s bedroom, where saffron yellow accents tie the space into the rest of the home’s palette. “The homeowner had seen an inspirational image with geometric wainscoting topped with a patterned wallpaper, and it really resonated with her, so she asked us to run with that idea,” adds Haynes.

“We wanted to create serene escapes in each of the two guest rooms that we worked on,” explains Haynes of this enveloping escape, swathed in a taupey pink. “We loved the Penny Morrison block printed graphic fabric that is on the drapes—the coppery brown is so rich against the quieter wall.”

“Our client is an avid reader, and she had wanted to create a dreamy blue space with a hanging chair where she can eventually cultivate plants that will become a living, breathing part of this space,” says Haynes. A Murphy bed is built into the bookshelves for additional sleeping space when the family has a full house, while the graphic sofa plays into the client’s adventurous side.

Designed to keep the couple’s daughter entertained through Chicago’s long winters, the playroom is equal parts imagination and movement. A modular foam sofa can be reconfigured into a fort or gymnastics landing pad, while a custom climbing wall encourages active play. Haynes’s favorite detail? A hand-crocheted saffron yellow swing that brings the home’s recurring signature hue into its most playful space.

Drama was on the menu in the dining room, where walls painted in Farrow & Ball’s Brinjal meet a matching lacquered ceiling. Getting the glossy aubergine finish just right took several attempts, but the result was worth the trouble.

In the home’s soaring living room, Haynes tempered the airy architecture with a collection of tactile, earthy furnishings. Seafoam sofas dressed in block-printed pillows mingle with woven armchairs, a pair of travertine coffee tables, and a vintage African stool. The mix lends visual weight to the expansive space without sacrificing the comfortable, family-friendly atmosphere.

A deep archway visible from the front door offered the perfect opportunity for a little surprise. Haynes tucked a pair of custom banquettes around a circular dining table, upholstering the snug seating in a saffron-and-blue block print sourced from Etsy. The palette echoes the nearby reading room, while the clever configuration makes everyday meals feel wonderfully intimate.

Founded originally in 2018 as Kate Haynes Interiors and reborn in 2023 as Noble Studio, Kate Haynes has been honing a fascination with spaces, architecture, and art since childhood. Her company has always been about beautiful spaces (naturally), but the rebirth as Noble Studio comes with a sense of mature intentionality to create quiet luxury for clients. Kate brings to her team a relentless commitment to the intangible result of joy present in the aesthetics and flow of a place, no matter what style it wears.

#6993. A Crash Course in Corduroy

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

It’s the perfect time of year for a tough, warm fabric with tons of texture. Here’s how to work it into your wardrobe.

According to a recent and deeply unscientific poll conducted by yours truly, some guys are confounded by corduroy. They like it. They want to wear it. But they’re not quite sure how it figures into their wardrobes. This is a pity, because corduroy is great. It’s warm, it’s hard-wearing, it delivers texture galore, and it can work beautifully in the context of all different types of personal style.

So in the interest of getting these guys—and you!—properly outfitted, I’m offering this crash course in corduroy. It’ll give you the key information you need in order to understand the fabric’s history and how you can wear it now. Let’s dive in.

There’s a great story about the name corduroy being derived from the French phrase “corde du roi,” or “cord of the king.” It conjures up all sorts of noble associations and sounds cool, like so much menswear lore does. The problem is, it’s likely apocryphal. No one really knows the root of the word for sure. (But if you want to bust out that tidbit at a dinner party and let your friends believe it, we wouldn’t blame you.)

What we do know is that corduroy traces its roots back to a category of fabric called fustian—basically heavy cloth, often used for menswear, that was usually woven from cotton (though there have been woolen and linen variations over the many centuries that fustian has existed). The name might come from the city of Fustat, near Cairo, which manufactured that sort of cloth. Or maybe it doesn’t. More menswear lore!

What sets corduroy apart from other fustian cloths like velveteen and moleskin is its distinctive ribbing. That innovation is newer; it was developed in Manchester around the middle of the 18th century, when the city became a center of production for cotton fabrics like velvet and the modern iteration of corduroy. The ribs are known as wales, and they’re created by weaving extra thread into the base fabric and then trimming them to created tufted, vertical ridges offset by channels between them.

This “modern” corduroy quickly became a workwear staple thanks to its durability and warmth. It was only later that it migrated to British country clothing and, eventually, to the rest of the globe. It slowly filtered into U.S. academia throughout the first half of the 20th century, eventually leading to the well-known stereotype of the college professor with the well-worn and elbow-patched corduroy jacket. It hit the counterculture in the ’60s. And it absolutely boomed in the ’70s, showing up on the era’s leading men both on and off the silver screen.

Since then, as with all menswear staples, its popularity has ebbed and flowed. But like denim, it never truly goes out of style. It’s all about how you wear it.

The width of the wales is measured by how many you can fit into an inch, so an 8-wale corduroy has thicker ridges than, say, a 12-wale corduroy. Most “standard” corduroy sits around 11 to 13 wales per inch. Chunkier stuff has a lower number, while “pinwale” or “needlecord” corduroy is higher, around 16 or above. As you might guess, the chunkier stuff reads more rugged and the finer stuff feels more refined.

Developed in and named after the town of New Bedford, Massachusetts, this fabric has distinctive vertical ridges, loads of durability, and roots in the world of workwear. It’s not quite corduroy, though. The ridges are created in the way the base fabric is woven, not by adding additional threads and trimming them. Still, it’s worth having on your radar if you like the look and feel of corduroy.

Just … wear it. I know that sounds like fake advice, but it’s really not worth overthinking it. Yes, you’ll want to think about the weather, because corduroy can wear warmer than other fabrics. And you might want to get yourself used to the idea of the occasional whoosh of the ribbed fabric rubbing against itself. (It’s not as distracting as the crinkle of a nylon shell suit, but it’s audible.) But corduroy is in its current form because it works. It wears in beautifully, fends off the cold, and adds some always welcome depth and texture to any outfit.

If you’re still nervous, start with something simple, like a corduroy jacket—your call on whether it’s a sport coat or something more utilitarian—or some five-pocket cord pants. You could rock it as an overshirt (go wide wale) or as a button-up. You could even channel everyone from Wes Anderson to Robert Redford with a full corduroy suit, though you might want to avoid the elbow patches if you don’t want to look too professorial.

As for what to wear it with? Well, nothing too techy. A super-stretchy golf shirt doesn’t exactly match the vibe; ditto for polyester joggers. And nothing too fine or fancy, either. Because of its more casual and countryish vibe, corduroy might not be the best complement to spit-polished oxford shoes or a particularly delicate worsted wool. The idea is to look for a sense of cohesion with your outfit, and anything that reads as deliberately futuristic or self-consciously dressy could detract from that.

Otherwise, options abound, especially when you start talking about other classic menswear fabrics and items. Corduroy looks great with beefy oxford-cloth button-downs. It plays well with denim and chino and flannel and hopsack. And don’t be afraid of going all in on texture. Bust out a shaggy Shetland or cable-knit sweater. Bring some tweed into the mix. Corduroy shouldn’t be confusing; it should be easy. So play around a little. You’re bound to have a (sorry) wale of a good time.

#6991. I Finally Found a Reclining Sofa That Doesn’t Look Like a Reclining Sofa—and I'm Never Going Back

It has held up against kids and the family cat, too.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The last time I had a recliner in my house was in 1999—a polyester-blend, marshmallow-like sectional that had a side lever to pop out a footrest. Stylish it was not, but most of my friends had something similar in their parents’ house. That’s one of the reasons I resisted even looking at recliners for almost 30 years. That is until I noticed a wave of modern variations with a stealth reclining option.

My regular sectional suddenly felt like a dud. It had a chaise and an ottoman, but whoever sat in the middle had to prop their feet on the coffee table, which is far from comfy. Plus, it looked a bit bulky in my sunroom, which doubles as a family hangout spot and movie night hub.

When I saw the POVISON Elara reclining sectional, I was smitten. I could customize it to have three recliners—a win for my family of four. I ordered it in a silvery gray upholstery, which added an airy vibe to my sunroom. Since the carpet underneath is light blue, I needed some depth. I accessorized it with woodsy hues via the geometric Frank Lloyd Wright print pillows from Faribault Mill and a Boll & Branch grand waffle Marino wool blanket. The look was something straight out of House Beautiful pages, IMHO.

I’ve had the POVISON for over three months now, and it has become my family’s favorite furniture piece in the house. Read my full review of it ahead.

Best For: This sectional is all about TLC and suits anyone who wants to sink into a sofa and put their feet up, quite literally. Great for families and roommates.

Not ideal for: Very small spaces. While you can order a two-seater sofa that is 83 inches long, the armrest is 43.5 inches wide, and that’s before you extend the foot recliner.

Price at review (3 armless chairs and 2 armrests): $3,699

Bottom Line: The smooth reclining technology of the Elara sectional makes relaxing easy, but it folds up into a sleek silhouette. Since there are no loose cushions or seating, you don’t have to worry about sagging or fluffing.

While front-door delivery is free, I opted for White Glove delivery, which is $119. Two people delivered four boxes—three to accommodate each seat and one for the arms of the sofa. Each piece was wrapped in a quilted “baggie” that was definitely a step up from the flimsier covers of other boxed furniture that had shipped to my house. Because these are on par with storage seat covers, I kept them and plan to use them to store my outdoor seating in the basement during winter.

The team unpacked all the boxes and assembled the pieces, making sure that the wiring worked properly before leaving. Each piece needs to be plugged in, so I had to get an extension cord. They took away the boxes, and the entire delivery/assembly process took less than 30 minutes.

My kids volunteered to push all the buttons to make sure the recliner sections worked. I was a little anxious about all the back and forth as they figured out the ideal reclining position, but my sofa comes with a two-year warranty, so that puts me at ease. (New orders qualify for a limited five-year warranty.)

If someone wants to recline in the middle seat they can.

The headrest is adjustable too to your desired position.

When asked how this sectional feels, the word that pops into my head is “super.” When I watch movies, I can tweak the seating just so. I don’t like to lean back all the way, so I like that the headrest can be more vertical and support my neck, but my legs can be fully extended.

The high-resilience cushions are described as having a “cloud bounce,” and I would agree. They are very soft, but not so much that I feel like the couch is going to swallow me up. There is a nice give that is still comfortable, not too firm. Because the upholstery is chenille, it is soft to the touch, and this only adds to the luxurious lounging moment.

When the head portion is not facing up, it folds down to a platform-like top that my cat adores. He will sit on it like it is a throne and look out of the windows. This is also important to me because my sunroom has two big picture windows, a selling point, and I would be devastated if they were blocked by a sofa headrest. I like that the Elara sits low.

That said, I, too, look out the window and sit on the sofa with my legs stretched out across the seats. I use two pillows to give my back more support as I rest against the arm.

While this is not a sleeper sofa, one person can definitely sleep or nap on the Elara. I wasn’t planning on spending the night on the sofa, but when I was tossing and turning in my bed and needed a change of scenery to fight my insomnia, this was the spot. I used one of the three included lumbar pillows and a weighted Luxome blanket and slept soundly until the morning. I’d do it again in a heartbeat.

When my father-in-law was visiting, he reclined all the way and took more than one nap with zero complaints.

I ordered the Elara in a Mist gray colorway, which is a bit lighter than I anticipated. It looks beautiful, and I want to keep it that way. I have a strict no eating on the sectional policy. We are a no-shoes-indoors family, but even so, I hound my kids about keeping their feet and hands clean. (And no one is allowed on here with their soccer socks!) My family may think I am a bit of a nuisance, but I just really want to keep the sofa from getting streaky marks or stains. The performance fabric is supposed to be water resistant, but I don’t want to take any chances.

My cat has not scratched at it yet, but there was one section where he stretched with his claws out and pulled out a thread. I snipped it off, and no one can tell. Three months into ownership, and the sectional looks the same as it did the day it was delivered.

I would definitely recommend the POVISON Elara sectional to anyone who wants to up their TV nights and R&R. When I am on the sofa, it reminds me of those fancy movie theater seats, but because the upholstery is so soft and cozy, I feel like I am in a spa.

My entire family (and cat) love this sectional. It is hands down a welcome addition to the house. Because it is a recliner, you can tuck the headrest and the footrest away, so the silhouette looks sleek and actually takes up less space than my old sectional with its chaise and ottoman. The only catch is if you have a heavy coffee table, it might be annoying having to move it out to make room for the foot extender.

The arms are already wide, which works great for resting, but I also added the Apto side table to create a little station for books, and I don’t need to worry about having anything loose on the coffee table. This combo makes the sectional function like an island where we could all gather and relax.

#6990. Ransomware Attacks Move Beyond Enterprise Systems to Theaten Employees' Families

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

In a recent announcement, the company highlighted findings from its 2026 Ransomware Risk Report, which examines AI-related attack tactics, personal intimidation, and recovery preparedness. Censuswide surveyed 1,200 information technology and security professionals across nine countries and eight industry sectors in the first half of 2026.

The sample has an important limit: This year's study included only organizations where at least one ransomware attack caused business disruption. The results describe those victims' experiences, rather than the likelihood that an organization will suffer an attack. The report says that sampling approach changed from previous studies.

Semperis sells identity security, recovery and crisis management products.

The report says 84% of respondents experienced attacks that exploited AI in some way. Its breakdown distinguishes AI-based attacks, reported by 40%, from attacks that manipulated victims' own AI agents or systems, reported by 44%. That distinction matters: The broader figure covers both attackers using AI and attackers exploiting AI deployed inside an organization.

Defensive use of AI also produced mixed responses. Nearly half of respondents, 49%, said they successfully used AI for detection or response, while 23% said their AI tools failed to detect or respond successfully. Those are survey responses about victimized organizations, rather than a controlled comparison of security products.

Chris Inglis, former U.S. national cyber director and a Semperis strategic adviser, described the difficulty of recognizing AI's involvement. "Many attacks are not perceived as being aided and abetted by AI because they're just too slick, too sophisticated," he said in the report.

Data erasure, destruction, or release remained the most frequently reported threat, at 56%. Blackmail, extortion or public shaming followed at 41%, with system lockouts or operational sabotage at 40%. The report also recorded psychological intimidation at 23% and physical threats at 17%.

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

Microsoft is combining its security information and event management and threat protection products within Microsoft Defender, creating what the company calls an Integrated Security Operations Center built for an era of AI-driven attacks.

A recent Cloud Security Alliance report urges organizations to inventory, govern, and continuously monitor machine and agent identities.

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT.

#6982. IDC Report Finds Significant Gap Between AI Spending and Measurable Financial Returns

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

The research firm's 2026 Worldwide AI Economics and Cost of Ownership Survey found that 69% reported such a gap, while only 15% had achieved an AI return on investment exceeding 100%.

In a separate May 2026 survey, 73% of respondents identified excessive AI costs as a significant risk.

IDC highlighted the findings in an October 20 analysis examining what enterprise customers expect from AI technology vendors.

Enterprise customers are placing greater emphasis on measurable business outcomes when evaluating AI products, according to IDC.

The firm identified three priorities for vendors: demonstrating financial value, addressing governance and security requirements, and building support beyond IT departments.

The analysis also highlighted the gap between initial agentic AI deployments and broader adoption. Although 80% of organizations have started deploying agentic AI, only 17% are using it across the enterprise.

IDC said evaluating AI investments can be difficult when the technology changes how employees work and how business processes operate.

The firm expects AI infrastructure capacity to catch up with demand around 2029, potentially easing costs. Until then, vendors face pressure to demonstrate the long-term financial value of their products.

AI funding decisions involve a broad range of executives. IDC's September 2026 C-Suite Tech Survey found that CIOs and CTOs were involved in 63% of those decisions, business-function executives in 62%, and chief financial officers in 61%.

A separate IDC survey found that 42% of CEOs planned to hire a chief AI officer within the next 12 months.

Buyers also reported concerns about the products and terms vendors offer. Limited integration with existing systems, insufficient model and data transparency, and concerns about vendor lock-in were each cited by 34% of respondents.

Overpromising and underdelivering, along with unclear pricing models, were each cited by 31%.

IDC said vendors need to substantiate financial returns and address security and governance requirements while working with decision-makers across IT, finance, and other business departments.

For more information, go to the IDC blog.

#6974. Report: Global Ransomware Attacks Rise 23% in Q3

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

The company's Cyber Threat Intelligence Report Review of Q3 2026 recorded 2,865 ransomware victim claims, up from 2,325 in the previous quarter.

Despite the quarterly increase, attacks declined 26% month over month in September to 821. North America recorded the most attacks that month with 390, followed by Europe with 211.

The industrial sector accounted for 29% of quarterly attacks, while Qilin remained the most active ransomware group for the sixth consecutive quarter, recording 365 victims. The Gentlemen followed with 356, and INC Ransom ranked third with 109.

The report highlighted a July cyber attack against a small UK power generation facility that forced operations to stop for approximately four days.

Although the incident did not affect the national electricity grid, it demonstrated how cyber attacks can disrupt physical infrastructure.

The security researchers said the incident was consistent with techniques previously associated with Iranian-affiliated campaigns, including attacks against internet-exposed industrial controllers.

However, the UK government has not formally attributed the attack, and the techniques used remain unconfirmed.

The report also identified growing security challenges as organizations connect traditional IT networks with operational technology (OT) systems for remote maintenance and centralized management.

The firm recommended network segmentation, stronger privileged-access controls, and continuous monitoring of OT environments.

The research found that more than 40 vulnerabilities affecting enterprise applications, VPN appliances, e-mail gateways, and management platforms were added to CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog during the quarter.

The report examined Gunra, a ransomware-as-a-service operation that has targeted 53 victims across multiple industries as of Q3.

According to the cybersecurity company, Gunra exploited vulnerabilities CVE-2024-55591 and CVE-2025-24472 in internet-facing Fortinet devices to gain access to corporate networks.

The group also used stolen credentials, session hijacking, and administrative tools to move between systems before deploying ransomware.

Other emerging ransomware groups included Deadlock and Storm, with the latter claiming 78 victims by the end of September.

Researchers also observed initial access brokers advertising compromised VPN, Remote Desktop Protocol, and SSH access, alongside access to cloud identities, source code repositories, and security management platforms.

The quarterly review also highlighted BraZetsu, an AI-enhanced Windows malware framework that evolved into a specialized initial access toolkit.

According to the report, BraZetsu uses AI models to automate victim profiling, analyze compromised environments, and identify valuable corporate systems.

The framework searches for enterprise software, financial data, digital certificates, and cloud credentials, helping operators identify compromised access that can be sold to other cyber criminals.

The cybersecurity firm also identified risks involving legitimate AI agents, including prompt injection, excessive permissions, and unintended access to external systems.

The report cited incidents involving U.S. and Australian government systems as examples of agents accessing resources beyond their intended scope.

Researchers warned that these incidents do not necessarily involve malicious intent but can create consequences resembling conventional cyber intrusions.

For the full report, visit the NCC Group site.

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

Cloud Security Alliance's 2026 Top Threats report ranks identity as the leading cloud security concern, followed by two new AI-related risks.

#6973. The Blue Door House from Notting Hill Just Hit the Market

The fictional London home of Hugh Grant’s bashful bookseller is now available for $9.9 million.

It’s perhaps the most famous house in one of London’s most charming neighborhoods, so beloved that crowds are known to gather around it like the English version of Carrie Bradshaw’s West Village apartment. It’s the blue-doored townhouse where Hugh Grant’s fictional bookseller lived and wooed Julia Roberts’s paparazzi-plagued Hollywood actress in 1999’s Notting Hill.

Despite starring in a blockbuster rom-com, the 2,750-square-foot house in question is very much real—and now it can be yours. That is, for roughly $10 million.

Not coincidentally, the three-bedroom residence also once belonged to Richard Curtis, the screenwriter behind Notting Hill, which was directed by Roger Michell and went on to gross some $364 million worldwide.

Behind the recognizable front door—painted a glossy, rich shade of royal blue—the house unfolds with an entrance vestibule and courtyard before reaching a cavernous great room topped by a vaulted ceiling and a row of skylights. Nearly 50 feet in length, the soaring space was originally an artist’s studio and now does triple duty as a kitchen, dining, and reception area. The room has hardwood floors, a stone fireplace, deep-green cabinetry, and a marble-topped island. Two guest rooms can also be found elsewhere on the floor, each with its own private bath.

Upstairs, the primary suite comes with a dual-sink bathroom equipped with both a freestanding tub and a shower. This floor also features a lofted library and office that overlooks the ground level’s central room, plus a spiral staircase that leads to the roof terrace.

Other outdoor spaces include a small balcony off the second floor and an expansive ground-level courtyard. It might not come with a bumbling Hugh Grant, but the house certainly has charm to spare.

The Blue Door House is listed for £7.5 million with Josh Ratcliff of UK Sotheby’s International Realty.

#6972. The Tod's Red Dot Sneaker Is Built for Men on the Move

A good sneaker should be able to keep up with a full day without making you look as though you dressed for one. There's the morning meeting where you need to look like you have a handle on things, the work lunch that runs long, and the dinner reservation you made three weeks ago that you still intend to keep. Somewhere in between, you might squeeze in a gallery opening, a drink with an old friend, or a detour home to change before the evening gets going. The right footwear makes all of it easier. A polished leather sneaker works with tailored trousers and an unstructured jacket, looks equally at home with dark denim. It saves you the trouble of carrying a second pair of shoes. And for a man whose calendar rarely leaves room for a wardrobe change, that's a fairly compelling proposition.

Tod's has spent decades making the case for shoes that combine good looks with real utility. The Italian house traces its roots to a family shoemaking workshop in the Marche region, where Filippo Della Valle began making shoes by hand in the early 20th century. This commitment to craftsmanship remains central to the business today, with skilled artisans working across its production facilities and specialist workshops. The brand built its reputation on footwear that marries quality materials, considered design, and a distinctly Italian approach to dressing. The Gommino driving shoe, with its supple leather and signature rubber pebbles, remains the clearest expression of that thinking. The Red Dot Sneaker brings the same instinct to a shoe built for the way men dress now, pairing artisanal craftsmanship with high-performance sole materials and advanced construction techniques informed by extensive research. A signature red dot on the heel of the sole distinguishes every model.

The appeal starts with the materials. The Red Dot Sneaker is made from Pashmy suede, a soft, velvety lambskin that gives the sneaker texture without making it feel fussy. Its streamlined shape keeps the look clean, while a leather lining and insole add to the considered construction. Elastic drawstring lacing makes getting in and out of the shoe easy, and a branded slider keeps the fastening neat. The extra-light rubber sole, finished with embossed detailing, gives the design a sporty foundation without overwhelming its proportions. Then there's the red dot itself, set against the heel as a small flash of color.

This balance is what makes the Red Dot Sneaker useful. Wear it with a navy suit when the dress code leaves some room for interpretation. Pair with pleated trousers and a knit polo for lunch, or with jeans and a good jacket when the weekend calls for something more elevated. The shoe can move between these settings because its design doesn't try too hard to announce itself. It has enough restraint to work with what you already own and enough comfort-minded engineering to make a long day on your feet more manageable.

Building a wardrobe is ultimately about knowing what deserves a place in it. Buy too many shoes for too few occasions and your closet becomes a record of increasingly specific decisions. Buy well, and a handful of considered pieces can do much more. The Tod's Red Dot Sneaker makes a persuasive argument for the latter: one well-made pair, ready for the meeting, the dinner, and whatever happens after. Your calendar may be complicated. Getting dressed shouldn't be.

#6968. This Heritage Pattern Is Replacing Flat, Beige Walls—Here’s How Designers Are Using It in 2026

The centuries-old motif also makes lampshades, rugs, and seating feel bespoke.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

In an age of encroaching AI, designers are turning to heritage looks to create bespoke interiors with personality. That may be one reason decorative marbling is having a moment.

"People are tired of flat, safe walls,” says designer Cara Woodhouse. “After years of greige, clients want surfaces with a point of view, and marbling has an artisanal history behind it. It's also flexible: it can read painterly, graphic, or soft depending on the colorway."

Its versatility is part of the appeal. “It doesn't necessarily have to be traditional or even executed on paper,” adds designer Sasha Bikoff. “It's really about the movement of color and the organic, unpredictable patterns that emerge, which can translate beautifully across wallpaper, furniture, textiles, and decorative objects.”

The paper marbling technique has a long history. In 10th-century Japan, artisans created “floating ink” designs by placing ink on water and manipulating it as it moved. By the 15th century, the practice was known in Turkey as “the art of clouds,” before making its way to Venetian and Florentine bookbinders.

The painterly look is appealing because its undulating lines feel expressive and vibrant without harsh edges, says Jo Plant, chief creative officer at Pooky, which is a brand well-known for its marbled lampshades.

Designers are bringing marbling into all kinds of interiors, from murals and upholstery to lampshades and even sink skirts. Analisse Taft-Gersten, cofounder of the 1818 Collective, used billowy marbled silk to elevate a powder room. And studios like Rule of Three are experimenting with the technique in floral fabrics that resemble vines.

Should you need further convincing, Woodhouse puts it best: "Marbling is pattern without repetition. No two passes are the same, so a wall reads more like art than wallpaper. It brings depth and a handmade quality to a room that might otherwise feel too finished."

Here are nine designer-approved ways to bring marbled motifs into your home.

Designer Michelle Fahmy of Haus of Meeshie likes things cheeky and playful. So it was a no-brainer to introduce groovy swirls into a Culver City pied-à-terre via a large-scale mural from Mitchell Black.

“I wanted something dramatic for the stairwell wall which bleeds into the living room and is the first thing you see when you walk in the door,” she says. “When I came across this wallpaper, it stopped me in my tracks! Usually, you see this paper marbling technique done on fabric or in smaller applications, like a lampshade, so this was unusual in scale and exactly the conversation starter I was looking for.”

For Bikoff, interior design should feel like a natural extension of a home’s architectural language. To bring her own sense of color, movement, and personality into a project within a Zaha Hadid building, she opted for Pierre Frey wallpaper with a liquid, dripping marble effect rather than a traditional, tightly swirled pattern.

“I love how the elongated, flowing lines create a sense of movement, almost as though the walls are melting or the colors are cascading down the surface,” Bikoff says. “Traditional marbling can feel very classical, whereas this interpretation feels more contemporary, psychedelic, and expressive.”

The dialogue between the wallpaper and Hadid's architecture is all about challenging conventional forms and embracing organic movement. “I wanted the room to feel immersive, almost like stepping inside a piece of abstract art,” she adds.

It was love at first sight. Marta St. Amour, interior designer and project manager at Braswell Design + Build was enchanted by the beautiful movement and layered tones of amber, soft blue, and sandy beige portrayed in the Lee Jofa Taplow wallpaper.

“The pattern became the inspiration for the bathroom's color palette, tying together the warm veining of Taj Mahal quartzite, rich caramel-colored shower tile, and soft blue tile behind the freestanding tub,” she says.

“We wanted the tub to feel like a statement piece, and the wallpaper's organic, flowing pattern complements its sculptural shape and warm metallic finish.”

To capture the spirit of the marbling technique but in a contemporary way, Bikoff opted for resin chairs with the telltale pattern in the above space.

“The way the colors blend and swirl through the resin gives them this incredible sense of depth and movement, almost like a liquid material that has been frozen in time,” she says.

Juxtaposing materials can challenge the perception of design. Here, the chairs feel like functional sculptures as they balance the visual richness of marble and the playful transparency of resin.

“They bring together color, texture, and fluidity while reinforcing the playful, futuristic spirit of the space."

If a full room of swirls is a bit much for you, settle on something smaller-scale, such as a lampshade. Pooky’s hand-marbled shades show off the dance of acrylic paint.

“Marbling is having a resurgence because we’re all craving craft, texture, and character in our homes,” says Plant. “People want pieces that feel human and handmade rather than mass-produced.” Adding a marbled shade is like decor candy that can add just the right amount of maximalism.

What makes the marbling technique unique is that the patterns are inherently organic, and so they lend themselves to pairing well with other organic shapes. Taft-Gersten anchored this powder room with a silk sink skirt featuring Rule of Three fabric that almost looks like it was kissed by bubbles.

“There’s a rhythm to it, but also an irregularity that keeps your eye moving; it never feels rigid,” Taft-Gersten says. The print also echoes the convex pebble mirrors above.

This girl’s bedroom may look dreamy, but that’s not what Cara Woodhouse was after when she landed on cotton candy-like Merenda Wallpaper by artist Michelle Weinberg.

“I wanted movement,” Woodhouse says. “A kid's room should feel alive, and marbled paper gives the walls energy without a single literal motif. I'd call it soft modern with an edge: rounded forms, blush tones, and a pattern that keeps it from going saccharine. The swirl does the work so the furniture can stay simple and sculptural."

The look of poured paint over a rug is one that will surely raise an eyebrow. Floor_Story designer Bekah Smith was experimenting with duct tape when she stumbled upon unexpected patterns that look like creases and shadows.

“There’s a similar sense of spontaneity to marbling, where patterns emerge through experimentation rather than being entirely predetermined,” Smith says.

For those looking for a ripple effect without the psychedelic swirls, consider ceramics as a way to dabble in the style without being so literal. Henry Holland Studio employs a traditional Japanese ceramic technique, nerikomi, to create irregular earthenware marbled motifs.

The pattern is created by stacking, folding, and rolling colored clay into slabs, then hand-molding them into functional shapes like lamp bases, bowls, and vases.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

Turns out holiday cheer comes with tasting notes.

#6964. 11 IKEA Kitchen Finds Designers Swear By in Their Own Homes

These finds deliver big style without breaking the budget.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Interior designers always know where to source the “good stuff,” and over the years we’ve gotten them to divulge their favorite secret spots for furniture and decor. The selections run the gamut from luxurious to affordable, showing that as long as you have an eye for design, even a budget-friendly pick can feel like a million bucks.

One affordable spot all designers keep coming back to is, unsurprisingly, IKEA. You can find anything you need at the Swedish retailer, often at unbeatable prices. We’ve already spoken with designers about what they generally like to buy from the brand, so this time around we’re getting more granular: we asked several pros what they like to buy for their kitchen, specifically.

Why this space? Well, our team is personally obsessed with this area of the home, and if you didn’t know, IKEA has an expansive array of kitchen products. From glassware and tableware to built-ins that make your cookspace look custom, IKEA has just about everything you need for your kitchen. Whether you’re looking for shopping inspiration or want to pick up designer-approved picks, check out what our favorite experts are buying from IKEA ahead.

“I use simple white dinnerware from IKEA at my own vacation home. It’s basic enough for everyday meals and can be paired with beautiful linens, chargers, and glassware for entertaining.” —Diane Hemmersbaugh, founder of Theo & Co. Design

“Stainless steel is a beautiful foil for warm wood and richly veined stone—it has such a distinct way of bringing that contemporary feeling into a warm, cozy space. I love this bowl’s clean curve and the way its surface catches the light. I’d leave it on the counter filled with pears, alongside a vintage ceramic vessel. Mixing reflective and matte surfaces gives even a small grouping depth.” —Anu Jain, founder of Atelier Oleana

“I'm a big carafe girlie and love using them as a vase, for drinks, or pure decoration. The cork stopper gives this simple glass carafe a lovely tactile detail. I’d use it for water at the dining table, paired with colored glasses and patterned linens. I like combining an understated everyday object with pieces that have more pattern or history—that mix makes a table feel personal.” —Anu Jain of Atelier Oleana

“It can be tricky to add personality to a kitchen with all of the necessary hardscape in place (cabinetry, appliances, countertops). A rug is a great way to bring in some color and personality. Since most people have kitchen rugs, why not choose something stylish? I prefer flatwoven rugs that have texture and color variations in kitchen spaces so that they can be easily cleaned and can hide small spills or crumbs.” —Vicki Taylor of Tailored Spaces

“We make a lot of skirted tables with a Kallax bookshelf raised a few inches with legs, and the divided storage is sort of a secret bonus.” —Lilse McKenna, founder of Lilse McKenna Inc.

“A multi-purpose stool has a place in every kitchen. This unique and beautifully designed stool can serve as a boost to reach high-up items. It can function as a plant stand in a kitchen that gets a good amount of light. And it can be pulled into a living space if extra seating is needed.”—Vicki Taylor of Tailored Spaces

“For the perfect countertop hutch, I’d start with the SEKTION high cabinet. Paired with the EKOPING glass doors, it looks just like a custom built-in on a budget, and ENKOPING also comes in a matching drawer front. I’d choose the brown walnut finish for all of them, since I love incorporating darker wood tones into any kitchen. To finish it off, I’d always add a custom crown piece above so it looks truly built-in.” —Brooke Mennen-Talsma, founder of Brooke MT Interiors

“I really like IKEA’s dishtowels. The blue and white patterned Valvardad collection, in a linen-cotton blend, is classic-looking. It's pretty enough to hang in the kitchen when not in use!” —Diane Hemmersbaugh, founder of Theo & Co. Design

“The Gladelig dinnerware set has a handmade pottery feel to it with the contrasting edges. This collection feels expensive and adds a little organic touch to each meal.” —Sarah Fontanez, founder of Pomme House

“We are ordering these to use as wastebaskets because I love their jute products and, bonus, these come with a liner.” —Lilse McKenna, founder of Lilse McKenna Inc.

“The Lohals jute rugs are a great neutral option, and you cannot beat the price for anyone with dogs or little kids who is not ready to commit to a pricier rug option.” —Lilse McKenna, founder of Lilse McKenna Inc.

#6963. Billy Evans Breaks His Silence on Full-Frontal ‘You Can See Everything’ Shower Scene, Says He Feels “Violated”

When Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim named their Elizabeth Holmes doc You Can See Everything, viewers weren’t really anticipating full-frontal nudity from her fiancé, Billy Evans. But here we are! A few weeks after the film broke box office records, Billy has officially broken his silence on that shower scene.

He discussed how he really felt about his manhood being exposed on the big screen during an “Ask Me Anything” Q&A on social media, where he also revealed his “favorite pop diva.” (Yes, that’s real, and no, it’s not who you’d expect.)

When someone asked, “Honestly why the shower scene that doesnt happen unless you want it to,” he claimed it was a “false narrative.” Then, someone else chimed in, “They forced themselves into the shower with you or they just started filming and you didn’t tell them to stop?”

Billy dropped a link to People’s recent report about the scene, where sources shared how he really felt about his nudity making the final cut. “Trust the ‘sources,’” he said.

One of People’s sources claimed Billy had been told his nudity would be “cut from the film,” while TMZ reported that he was told “it would not make the final cut for legal reasons.”

“The shower scene wasn’t Billy’s idea. He only agreed to it with an understanding they would keep it PG,” another insider told the outlet. “Finding out otherwise via media coverage would be unsettling for anyone, to say the least. It’s a violation of trust and exploiting that trust for shock value.”

Both the report and Billy’s doubling down come after one of the film’s directors, Lance Oppenheim, insisted that he “didn’t care” that they had filmed him during a post-gym shower.

During an interview with Vulture, Lance suggested that Billy “really had something he wanted to show to us and to the world” when it came to the infamous full-frontal scene.

“I feel like there’s enough stuff in there that gives me the impression that maybe he was excited that people could see everything and that there was full access, I guess,” Lance also told GQ, in part. “We were in the car ride on the way back from the gym, and I said to Billy, ‘We will give you some time to shower.’ And Billy responded, ‘Want to film me showering?’ I offered to film him in a way that could avoid seeing his genitals, but Billy said he didn’t care about being seen naked.”

Well…that’s the latest on this wildly unexpected saga.

#6962. The Top Five Brands to Know This Winter

With sharp tailoring and standout knitwear, MR PORTER offers a variety of collections for dressing well in the cold.

Winter cold gives you a reason to wear the clothes that make getting dressed interesting: a proper wool coat, a substantial sweater, trousers with some weight to them, and boots built for more than walking from the car to the restaurant. Then the holiday parties start, and your usual uniform needs an upgrade. A good wardrobe should handle all of it without requiring you to develop a different personality for every occasion. And because you’re wearing more of your clothes at once, the details matter. Fit, fabric, and proportion can make the difference between looking put together and looking like you got dressed in the dark.

MR PORTER is a great place to start building that versatile wardrobe. Its assortment brings together brands with a clear understanding of what each does well, from Tom Ford’s sharp tailoring to Brunello Cucinelli’s easy sophistication and Begg & Co’s expertise in knitwear and accessories. Yoke offers a more experimental take on everyday dressing, while Mr P focuses on the everyday staples that make getting dressed easier. The common threads are thoughtful design, good materials, and construction that holds up to repeated wear.

We’ve picked two pieces from each brand that make a compelling case for adding them to your cold-weather wardrobe.

Tom Ford launched his namesake label in 2005 after revitalizing Gucci throughout the 1990s with a sexier, more provocative take on luxury. The brand is known for sharp tailoring, sleek silhouettes, and a glamorous sensibility that extends from eveningwear to leather goods and footwear. Today, designer Haider Ackermann leads its creative direction, carrying that legacy forward with his own modern vision.

The designer’s tailoring has always favored a sharp silhouette, and these brown wool tuxedo pants put a more relaxed spin on formalwear. Double pleats give the straight legs room to move, while pressed creases keep the shape crisp. Wear them with a black dinner jacket for a holiday party that calls for something beyond the usual uniform, or pair them with a fine-gauge black sweater and polished loafers for a winter dinner.

A good winter night-out look starts from the ground up. Tom Ford’s Chelsea boots bring a little rock-and-roll swagger to the occasion, with glossy, lizard-effect leather, sharply pointed toes, and stacked heels that give the classic slip-on silhouette a Western edge. They’re the sort of boots that can turn a dark suit into a statement or give black jeans and a wool coat some much-needed attitude. Wear them to a holiday party, a late dinner, or anywhere your sneakers might send the wrong message.

Founded in Umbria in 1978, Brunello Cucinelli began with a simple idea: make cashmere sweaters for women in colors that felt fresh and modern. The Italian label has since expanded into a full menswear and womenswear collection, becoming known for luxurious knitwear, fine tailoring, and an easy approach to dressing well. Its clothes combine traditional Italian craftsmanship with a relaxed sophistication that works just as well for everyday wear as it does for more formal occasions.

Brunello Cucinelli has built a following around luxurious fabrics and Italian tailoring that manage to feel relaxed. This wool-felt overshirt puts that approach to work in a more casual silhouette, with flap chest pockets, silver-tone snap fastenings, and a curved hem. The black wool is substantial enough to take the edge off a cold city morning, while the clean design makes it a smart alternative to a traditional jacket. Wear it over a fine-knit sweater with wool trousers for the office, then head straight to dinner.

Brunello Cucinelli’s cashmere expertise is on full display in this black ribbed zip-up sweater, knitted in Italy with a relaxed fit and a stand collar that keeps the cold off your neck. The two-way zipper lets you adjust the coverage, whether you’re layering it under a wool overcoat for a weekend spent exploring the city or wearing it on its own during a long flight. It’s a clever way to make travel-day comfort feel considerably more considered.

Mr P. has an advantage most menswear labels don’t: it was developed by the people at MR PORTER who spend their days studying what makes clothes worth buying. Launched in 2017, the in-house brand focuses on wardrobe staples, from knitwear and denim to outerwear, with an emphasis on quality materials and details that make sense in everyday life. The idea is to get the fundamentals right, then give men plenty of ways to wear them.

A polo shirt can do more than a button-down when the occasion calls for looking presentable without getting too dressed up. Mr P.’s long-sleeved version is knitted in Italy from 75 percent merino wool and 25 percent silk, giving the fine-gauge fabric a soft feel and subtle sheen. An open collar and ribbed trims keep the design streamlined. A smart alternative to another crewneck sweater, this can be worn with flannel trousers for a holiday lunch or under a tailored coat when you’re meeting friends for a winter matinee.

Some winter days require a coat that does more than keep you warm. Mr P.’s green shearling number has plenty of personality, with a suede exterior, plush lining, and a broad collar that frames the face. The loose fit leaves room for a chunky sweater underneath, while the button front and slanted pockets keep things practical. Wear it with dark denim and sturdy boots for a Saturday spent browsing record shops, grabbing lunch, or walking home the long way. You might even forget how cold it is.

Founded by designer Norio Terada in Tokyo in 2018, Yoke takes its name from the garment panel that joins pieces of fabric together. That idea of connection carries through the label’s approach to design, which draws on art and reworks familiar menswear with unexpected proportions, interesting textures, and subtle twists. The result is clothing that feels relaxed and easy to wear, with enough edge to keep a wardrobe of everyday staples from looking predictable.

Yoke approaches everyday clothing with a Japanese design sensibility that puts proportion and considered details at the forefront. These wide-leg jeans show how much a change in silhouette can do. Cut from black cotton denim, they have a roomy leg and a classic five-pocket design, with enough volume to balance a chunky knit or a boxy winter coat. Wear them with a pair of leather sneakers for an afternoon antique hunting or checking out a gallery.

Made in Japan from a blend of 85 percent wool and 15 percent cashmere, this gray felt overcoat has a modern silhouette withdropped shoulders, wide notch lapels, and an oversized double-breasted cut that leaves plenty of room for winter layers. The fabric feels substantial without weighing you down, making it the perfect choice for days when you’re walking across town rather than hopping between cabs. Throw it over a chunky sweater and wide-leg jeans for a look that feels polished without being overly formal.

Begg & Co’s story begins in Paisley, Scotland, in 1866, when Alex Begg established a textile business that would become known for its scarves and cashmere. Today, the brand makes knitwear and accessories at mills in Ayr and Hawick, combining traditional Scottish textile techniques with modern design. Its cashmere collection ranges from classic scarves to substantial sweaters, with texture and color often doing the work that logos do elsewhere.

Begg & Co has been making textiles in Scotland since the 19th century, and its experience shows in the details of this navy scarf. The Bernard is knitted from a blend of 56 percent cashmere and 44 percent lambswool, with a repeating cable pattern that gives it texture and wide ribbed ends that keep the design clean. At nearly 7 feet long, it can be wrapped snugly around your neck on a windy morning or left loose over a wool coat when you want a little more warmth on the way to a weekend lunch.

Begg & Co’s Surfer cardigan is knitted in Scotland from pure cashmere, with blended yarns that give the ribbed surface subtle tonal variation. Horn buttons, welt pockets, and a shawl collar lend it a classic look, while the substantial knit makes it a practical alternative to a lightweight jacket. Wear it over a T-shirt with relaxed trousers for a slow Sunday morning at home, then keep it on when you head out for coffee or an impromptu lunch. It’s an easy way to look put together without getting dressed up.

#6960. 15 Paint Colors HGTV Stars Swear By for a Home That Looks Expensive

From sea blue to crisp white, these designer favorites are timeless.

Everyone knows that a quick coat of paint is the fastest way to transform a room. It’s a favorite trick among professional designers and DIYers alike, but choosing the perfect shade isn't always easy. With hundreds of colors to choose from, finding “the one” can quickly become overwhelming. If you’re feeling a little stuck, don’t fret: HGTV’s biggest stars make these tough decisions every day.

Whether they’re renovating an outdated home or putting the finishing touches on a dream build, HGTV personalities are constantly selecting paint colors that make a space feel fresh, spacious, inviting, and beautifully designed. Over the years, they’ve crowned a select few as go-to favorites that are foolproof and versatile enough to work in almost any room. When every renovation comes with deadlines and budgets, dependable paint colors become invaluable.

To help narrow your search, we asked some of our favorite HGTV stars to reveal the paint colors they reach for most often. Their picks include everything from versatile neutrals to powdery pastels that work across a variety of homes and aesthetics. Additional copy by Alyssa Khan.

““My current favorite paint color is Black Fox by Sherwin-Williams. It’s the perfect mix of sophisticated and moody, with just enough warmth to make any space feel grounded and timeless. It pairs beautifully with so many other tones and textures. I love it in an office!”—Chelsea DeBoer, Down Home Fab

#6959. Discover the Modest St. Louis Home Where Chuck Berry Wrote His First Hits

The Father of Rock n’ Roll spent some of his most formative years as a musician in this modest brick house.

From the sidewalk, 3137 Whittier Street looks like any other early-20th century home in St. Louis. The red brick exterior, modest size, and simple covered porch don’t hint at its musical past. But this is where Chuck Berry lived in the 1950s, raising his family and creating the sound of rock and roll—a “mixture of a lot of music,” he once told St. Louis Magazine. In this petite house, he wrote songs that would resonate with a global audience and make him one of the most influential musicians of the 20th century.

Built in 1910, the single-story brick cottage is located in the Greater Ville neighborhood in north St. Louis. Berry and wife Themetta Suggs bought it for $4,500 in 1950, back when he was still trying to make a name for himself as a musician. They lived there with their growing family until 1958.

The three-bedroom, one-bathroom house was a simple home for someone whose music would change pop culture. In 1956, Berry used his carpentry skills to personalize the house, adding a two-room concrete-block extension at the back. The renovation gave his family more space and provided him with a place to rehearse. He also set up a studio in the basement, where he recorded and worked on songs as his career took off.

During these years, Berry helped define the rock-and-roll sound, writing records and blending blues, rhythm and blues, and country with his energetic electric guitar style. His hit single “Maybellene,” released in 1955, brought him national attention, followed by popular tracks like “Roll Over Beethoven” (1956), “Rock and Roll Music” (1957), “Sweet Little Sixteen” (1958), and “Johnny B. Goode” (1958).

Berry’s influence reached far beyond his own records. His sharp guitar riffs, driving rhythms, and lyrics created a musical blueprint that inspired generations of performers. The Beatles and the Rolling Stones, for instance, were just some of the British artists who recorded his songs and introduced his music to new fans, helping cement his legacy on both sides of the Atlantic. In 1986, Berry was inducted into the inaugural class of the Rock & Roll Hall of Fame.

The Whittier Street house holds a special place in St. Louis’s Black history. The nearby Ville neighborhood was a cultural hub for Black residents during segregation and home to influential figures in music, sports, and the arts, including Tina Turner, Arthur Ashe, and Josephine Baker. In 2008, Berry’s home was added to the National Register of Historic Places, honoring its connection to his stardom and Black performing arts history.

Berry died on March 18, 2017, at age 90. But his old home stands as a reminder of the formative years of his groundbreaking career.

Berry’s house isn’t open for tours—most people only catch a glimpse from the street—and few, if any, public interior photos are widely available. But according to St. Louis Realtors, original features like hardwood floors, plaster, doors, and fixtures are still there.

Preservation efforts are currently underway to save the house after years of sitting empty and deteriorating. In 2022, the St. Louis Development Corporation made the house a priority project, with goals that include community involvement and future redevelopment. According to a March report, the City of St. Louis has completed initial stabilization work, including floor repairs and masonry restoration.

The house’s long-term use is still undecided. But regardless of what it becomes, it stands as a part of history—an ordinary family home that became the backdrop for music that would echo around the world.

Brea Cubit is an award-winning journalist who covers culture, entertainment, fashion, business, and more. When she’s not writing and editing, she enjoys listening to live music, binge-watching nostalgic TV, and shopping at artisan markets.

#6958. The 20 Best- and Worst-Dressed Celebs at the 2026 CFDA Awards

Tom + Lorenzo recap the 45th annual CFDAs red carpet as only they can.

Everyone always says that the Met Gala is fashion’s biggest night, but for our money, if you really want to see celebrities trying to outdo each other and impress the world of fashion with their style, the CFDA Awards is where it’s at. The Met Gala is a fashion parade, but the CFDAs are more of a fashion battle. After all, wouldn’t you want to impress the Council of Fashion Designers of America? Let’s see who won the fashion war this year and who had to retreat and regroup for next time. Here are our picks for the best and worst of the night.

While we think a floor-length leather coat can be dramatic on a red carpet, it’s really all about what you pair it with, and we just don’t think this somewhat drab dusty pink dress does much for the look. It gives the impression she threw a coat on over a nightgown because her hotel room was on fire in the middle of the night. A coat like that needs a stronger design underneath it. Cute bag, but it doesn’t vibe well with the rest of it.

We know we should probably tut-tut her antics, but we honestly think she’s one of the most interesting people you could ever hope to invite to a red carpet event. She always delivers a look. It may not be a chic look or a high-end look or even a pretty look, but it’s always bold and leaves heads turning. What else is a red carpet for? The design gets a little sloppy at the hem, but it’s a witty and well-styled look. We just don’t love the matchy slides. The tiara is hilarious.

It’s very old-school debutante and she’s well-suited to it, but it sits too low on her. We certainly don’t have a problem with chic, elegant restraint in style, but we do think you need to do something with it to make it red carpet-ready. There’s nothing wrong with plainly pretty, but a metallic shoe, a pendant, some hair with a little life to it—all of these would improve the look, we think.

It’s very showgirl, but that’s what you get when you pick a vintage Bob Mackie. We don’t know if any of his most celebrated divas wore this, but it’s definitely giving Cher, Ann-Margret, and Tina Turner, which can never be bad references for any look. Our only quibble is with the center slit and the hem length, but we love the gold sandals. She’s giving throwback glam and she looks great.

We half-love this. The hair is exquisite, the jacket is beautifully tailored, and we love the delicate jewelry and lace touches. But we’re sorry to say, everything below the waist is disappointing. If anyone could make a pair of bloomers look chic, it’s probably them, but if you’re not going to commit to wearing tights with them, we don’t see the point. The pants bring the whole look down.

This is lovely. Polka dots are pretty trendy at the moment, and there were several takes on this red carpet, but this was our favorite. It’s a little rare to see the print rendered in anything but black and white, so this pale and pretty blue was a pleasant surprise. We’re not the biggest fans of floppy bows, but we like that this one provides a sharp contrast with the rest of the dress. The ruching is lightly applied and the styling is flawless.

We’ve been banging the “sheer dresses are played out” drum for at least a year now, but designers, stylists, and stars love them way too much. Our one rule about the style is to make it interesting, because there’s nothing worse than showing off your underwear in a boring dress. This is anything but boring. It’s borderline drag queen, but that’s not a bad thing. The feather effect on the cuffs, collar, and hem are fun and fabulous, but it’s her hair and makeup that really sell this look.

Now that’s what we’re talking about! Why come to the CFDAs to be normal? This is an insane design, but what impresses us most about this look is how well-styled she is. Her hair and makeup are very pretty, and her jewelry choices are fairly low-key. You really don’t want your styling to be fighting the dress, which doesn’t need any help. We can’t imagine she had an easy time sitting all night, but we’re not opposed to a little suffering in order to serve a look.

If there’s one style of dress we dislike above all others, it’s the “my dress is falling off” dress. We tend to think it’s gimmicky, and we also tend to think it’s weird to pretend you’re getting undressed in public. Having said that, we’re surprised to admit how much we love this. The color story is elegant, the exposed bustier is very pretty, and we love how the blocking gives a very simple dress so much visual interest. The relatively clean and low-key styling is perfect.

We’re sorry, but we simply just don’t like long, sheer overlays over miniskirts. The colors look amazing on her, but we’re not sure we’ve ever seen a dress that gave such a strong impression that it isn’t finished yet. This is like one-third of a good design, and it should’ve been left on the dress form until it was finished.

Designer Zac Posen has been doing a bang-up job of changing the image of Gap by putting high-end custom pieces on the red carpet that work with Gap styles and textiles. What could be more Gap than a red t-shirt and classic denim jacket? We like how the tee has been altered, and the jewelry touches really help elevate the look. It’s cute, it’s chic, and it feels like it came in off the street—in a good way.

Go on, girl. Give us nothing. We’re surprised she picked something so sedate and relatively casual to wear, since she’s usually pretty good about serving a look. Nothing about this says “gala” or even “night.” It feels like she stopped by on her way from work. The skirt’s okay, but the top is a big nothing.

It was a big night for crop tops and cutouts, and we wish we could say that we love this take, but it’s a tad over-designed. We tend to hate tassels on sight, and we can’t say this look is changing our mind. The color looks spectacular and we like the design of the bust and halter, but the fit is too tight. All of the embellishments are overdone and could have used an edit. Those hip beads look like they’d be pretty annoying all night.

This is kind of an oddly unbalanced look. The textile is beautiful, but the design leaves it looking a little flimsy. At the very least, a slightly more interesting bandeau would have helped the look; maybe with some pleating to give it shape. We also think you need to really dress up a crop top for the red carpet so it doesn’t look too “poolside.” She needs some statement jewelry. We don’t understand the black train or sandals at all.

To us, this is an instance of a beautiful textile being used badly. It’s a rich, luxurious-looking metallic brocade that would make a fantastic coat or ball gown, but it’s too stiff for a crop top and knee-length skirt. We also don’t think it photographs all that well and the color does nothing for her.

Elegant, but a tad boring. Plain black gowns need smart styling to help them stand out, and while we get that she’s not really into a lot of flash in her style, this really could’ve used better jewelry and a more interesting wrap.

This one took us a minute. At first glance, we disliked the color and we felt the design was a little stale, but the longer we took it in, the more it started to appeal to us. The design is deceptive, because it comes across like a standard goddess gown, but the pleating and draping give it real interest and shape. We love the bold lip, which really helps set that pale green off. We might have liked a little bit more in the jewelry department, but the delicate pieces are lovely.

Can a girl ever really go wrong in a hot metallic dress and a killer pair of earrings? This is a nice change of pace for her, since she tends to go for slip dresses and sheer dresses more often than not. We love how the bodice looks like they just brushed gilt onto her. The crocodile-like texture gives it more of an old world armored feel, but the shape is pure modern sexy. Those slingbacks are gorgeous.

It’s an impressive design, and it fits with Zac Posen’s plans to glam up the brand, but turning denim into a formal gown is always a little tricky. We tend to think it works best if you don’t overwork the textile and if you don’t make too many cutesy references to jean pockets or buttons. In other words, a dress that looks like it was made out of old jeans just doesn’t look chic to us. The bodice works, but the skirt is not great.

This is a lot of fun and she looks great in it. We keep coming back to the same point about crop tops and bra tops on a formal red carpet: They need some oomph in the styling. To be fair, this design is bold and bright and doesn’t need too much help, but the color blocking gives it a sporty feel, and we can’t help thinking some bright bangles, a cocktail ring, and a more interesting shoe choice would really elevate this.
