#6956. 20 Lucky Plants That Bring Good Fortune and Major Good Vibes to Your Home

Houseplants are more than just mere accessories. Plants are beacons of vibrant, flowing, living energy, symbolizing many sentiments, from “Get Well” to “Congrats on the new gig.” Like colors, crystals, and trinkets, it’s always fun to explore the meanings behind your greenery to add an extra layer of intention to your windowsill, flower beds, or next potted gift. If you're looking to usher in more good vibes (and who isnt?), adding lucky plants to your home might be just what you need.

Feng shui principles have long held that plants bring good energy to your home. Simply appreciating their leaves and branches instantly adds vivaciousness to any room. That said, wealth, peace, and health aren’t the only things that good luck plants provide. Research shows plants can boost mood, improve sleep, and help us feel happier, even less isolated. When you factor those benefits into the equation, it'd be silly not to snag yourself a plant or two, right?

Before you rush to the nearest nursery, you’ll need to account for the conditions plants need to thrive—a dying plant won’t bring you an abundance of much. As a general rule of thumb, most indoor houseplants (especially tropicals) prefer to bask in moderate to bright light, easily achieved by placing them near a window that gets lots of soft morning light. If your rooms are dark or windowless, you can always opt for a grow light. Watering is also important, but hitting the proper cadence is essential. In fact, more plants die from overwatering than from under. To avoid drowning your plant, poke your finger in the soil before giving it a drink; if soil clings to your skin, it’s probably moist enough. Wait a few more days, then recheck.

Ahead, you'll find 20 good luck plants that thrive in a variety of indoor settings. From glam orchids to classic lucky bamboo (of course!), these greens will bring good juju to your home for years to come.

Despite its name, this good-luck plant isn't true bamboo. It's a type of dracaena that has the lower foliage stripped off to resemble bamboo. It's incredibly easy to grow and care for, making it ideal for plant parents of all experience levels. Lucky bamboo does best in medium-to-bright indirect light but will tolerate low light. If yours is growing in a container of water, change the water weekly—if planted in soil, keep it slightly moist.

Beloved for their durable nature and eye-catching blooms, orchids have a glamorous air about them—and a reputation for symbolizing fertility and abundance. Though they may look high-maintenance, orchids are relatively easy to care for; place them in a spot that gets at least six hours of indirect light daily and "water" them by placing three ice cubes on the soil once a week.

Oxalis, or lucky shamrock plant, is associated with St. Patrick's Day and is said to bring good luck to the home. With three-lobed green or burgundy foliage and delicate flowers that bloom for weeks, these seasonal favorites are long-lived and low maintenance. Give this good-luck plant bright, indirect light and allow it to dry out slightly between waterings.

Vibrant, funky, and known to bring good fortune, bromeliad plants are the answer to any plant lover looking to add a little color to their collection. A close cousin to the pineapple, these bright tropicals come in a variety of beautiful shades (think bright pink, zesty yellow, or fiery red). Place the plant somewhere with plenty of light and water it every one to two weeks in warmer months, reducing your cadence to every two to three weeks in colder months.

The glossy, coin-shaped leaves of a pilea plant (aka Chinese money plant) are purported to bring wealth and luck. It's also super easy to propagate and share with loved ones, which lends a sense of prosperity to the giver. Give it bright, indirect light, and water when the soil feels mostly dry.

Though it may be known by rather unpleasant names (it also goes by mother-in-law's tongue), snake plants make a beautiful addition to any home, thanks to their graphic variegated leaves and vibrant green hue. These good-luck plants are known to invite fortune, good health, and creativity into the home and are incredibly easy to care for, thriving in a variety of light settings and only requiring watering every two to three weeks.

Also called Pachira aquatica, the money tree is said to bring its owner wealth and is often given to new businesses to wish them good fortune. Some even consider money trees to be the luckiest houseplants of all. This sturdy plant prefers medium to bright indirect light, but will tolerate low light levels just fine too. Rotate the pot once a week so that the branches will grow evenly, and water when the top inch or two of soil feels dry.

It's no surprise that this elegant plant is purported to bring harmony to your home. Peace lilies have glossy green leaves and pretty spoon-shaped flowers. Give this good-luck plant bright, indirect light, and keep the soil lightly moist. Pet parents: It's best to avoid this variety, since it's especially toxic to cats.

A symbol of longevity and good luck, chrysanthemums are a beautiful plant to enjoy indoors and out. Also called mums, these flowers are perennial if you plant them in the spring in your garden and can be enjoyed as gifts any time of year (they'll bloom indoors for several weeks). Just water them when the soil feels slightly dry on top.

Jade plants are said to bring wealth and longevity and can live for decades with the proper care. Resembling tiny trees with plump, glossy leaves, they prefer bright, indirect light over direct rays, which can scorch their delicate leaves. Because they're succulents, you should only water this good-luck plant when the top inch or two of soil feels dry—overwatering will cause them to turn to mush.

The gorgeous peony is a symbol of wealth and rank. This stunning perennial can live for decades when planted in the proper USDA growing zone. Choose a spot that receives direct sunlight for six or more hours a day and water the plant weekly to get it established; after that, peonies are drought tolerant.

A symbol of prosperity since ancient times, citrus trees—Meyer lemon, kumquat, take your pick—make a wonderful indoor or patio plant. Give these trees direct sun for six or more hours per day, or use a grow light—without ample light, they're unlikely to produce fruit. Water when the soil feels slightly dry on top.

Lavender flowers are said to bring serenity, and studies show that they actually live up to the hype, thanks to powerful phytochemicals that can be mood boosters for both people and pets. Lavender can be fussy to grow indoors, but it's a cinch to grow outdoors. Just give it full sun and make sure it's in a well-draining spot (or a container, if you have clay soil). Water only when very dry.

With a single, heart-shaped leaf, this adorable good-luck plant is a wonderful gift to show affection or care—in fact, hoya is known to represent true love and harmony. Give it bright, indirect light, and water when the top of the soil feels dry to the touch, usually every week or two.

The scent of a rosemary plant can calm the body and energize the mind, and it was once thought to bring good luck to travelers. This culinary herb is a tender perennial, which means it can be planted in the garden in warm climates. Give it full sun, and water it only sparingly; this native Mediterranean plant prefers to be on the dry side. Indoors, it needs a sunny windowsill with direct sunlight or a grow light.

The bold leaves of the zebra plant bring good fortune, wealth, and steady progress. Feng Shui traditions believed that the upward-growing leaves and resilient roots acted as a protective charm to ward off negative energy and balance indoor spaces.

Like its cousin, the common snake plant, the copper-toned variety is also known to represent resilience, protection, and good fortune, just with a shinier hue.

Feng Shui philosophy believes the heart-shaped leaves of the philodendron bring love, balance, and mental calmness. The plant is also a known air purifier that loves low-light environments. It must be the ambiance.

There’s a reason the olive tree is recognized as a universal symbol of peace. In Greek mythology, the Goddess Athena gifted the tree to the people of Athens, ultimately helping them win the city. Today, the plant is rumored to bring that same sense of peace, safety, and balance into homes.

Known for its fresh scent and medicinal properties, eucalyptus is thought to bring spa-like serenity and elevated wellness into your home.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6955. Before & After: How an Artist and Architect Couple Turned the “Worst House” on the Block Into Their Dream Home

In Brooklyn, artist Katherine Keltner and architect Douglas Gauthier transform a crumbling townhouse with equal parts drama and restraint.

Before they stepped foot inside the crumbling brownstone on a landmark block in Brooklyn’s Boerum Hill, artist Katherine Keltner and architect Douglas Gauthier had toured 30 homes and browsed more than 200 online. Neither of them expected to become instantly captivated by the 1869 Italianate townhouse, which was being sold in a stately yet dilapidated condition, having once served as a single-room occupancy (SRO) residence. But it happened nonetheless.

“This was the first one that had the bones that Katherine wanted,” recalls Gauthier, who runs the firm GAuthier Architects. “It follows the classic real estate rule of buying the worst house on the best block.”

The strategy paid off: Once their offer was accepted, the couple embarked on an 18-month-long odyssey that saw them jump through a series of hoops (preservationists; concerned neighbors; skeptical engineers) to completely transform the moldering pile into a creative haven for them to live, work, and raise their daughter.

A true joint-effort, Gauthier oversaw a modest two-story rear expansion and structural alterations, while Keltner took charge of spatial arrangements as well as color, pattern, texture, and artwork—much of it her own.

Once deteriorating, the interiors now have an easy elegance, with plaster walls, arched passageways, and curved corners that give the soaring ceilings an intimate feel. Pink takes an unexpected starring role, appearing throughout the parlor floor and also in the zellige-clad primary bath, but other adventurous hues like mint green and crimson also feature prominently. Capping it off is a plywood-sheathed office for Gauthier’s practice, allowing the family retreat to double as a place of creative inspiration.

Below, Keltner and Gauthier share their biggest lessons (and some “before” pictures) from the painstaking passion project.

When the townhouse was converted to an SRO, it had been chopped up into at least 13 units and two kitchens, and few of the original decorative elements were still intact. A couple had bought it in the 1960s and made it a family home again and added an art gallery space. But by 2021, when Keltner and Gauthier first saw it, the house was in a state of severe neglect, with peeling paint, crumbling floors, ancient appliances—not to mention a 1,500-pound printing press in the studio (that the couple later preserved). Yet, Keltner and Gauthier saw an opportunity, not to recreate the past, but rather forge their own set of rules.

“The house wasn’t burdened with pristine historic details—our moldings were trashed,” says Gauthier. “There were a few things that we kept and celebrated, but to me, it was like a tabula rasa. We can just go in and make it ours.” Katherine agreed: “I feel like sometimes having to preserve things is more expensive than just starting clean,” she says. “And so I thought that was an advantage for us.”

Not having to be too precious with ornamentation allowed the couple to make a bold statement about crown moldings, eschewing tradition and installing curving corners and coved ceilings instead. Nowhere does the effect have more impact than in the parlor, where Gauthier devised a ceiling light in the shape of a giant oval that casts light upward rather than down. “I really like indirect lighting,” he says.

Crafted out of laser-cut plywood, the elliptical fixture is an abstracted homage to the ceiling medallions found in neighboring historic brownstones. “Some of them are incredibly ornate and beautiful, but they don't really have any use—they’re just decoration,” says Gauthier. “Adding ambient lighting to the living space takes that historical reference in pocket.”

Once they were through the process of changing the structure’s zoning from an SRO to a single-family home, Gauthier and Keltner had to contend with its landmarked status, meaning a series of approvals were required for any additions or exterior modifications.

The couple originally planned to construct a three-story extension in the back, but they ended up compromising on two floors. This expansion includes a bump-out for the parlor floor’s kitchen, leading to a rear deck that steps down to the garden. On top, a private terrace connects to the primary bath. “Instead of having that third story, we now have a deck off of our primary suite,” says Keltner. “It’s kind of amazing,” adds Gauthier.

One decision the couple wrestled with mightily was whether to replace the original, 150-year-old pine flooring with ebony-stained white oak. “The floors were part of the reason we liked the house so much,” says Gauthier. But their contractor talked them out of keeping them. “It’s a lot of extra work for them,” says Keltner. “They have to take it up, save it, put something else down, and then put it back. I think we reached a point in time and money where we were like, Okay, fine.”

Another flooring regret was not adding underfoot heating. “I was pushing the mechanical engineers to do radiant heating and radiant cooling,” says Gauthier. “Radiant heat is really lovely—you have warmth under your feet all the time.” But the mechanics behind the system proved complicated and technically redundant, as the home was already configured with a forced-air HVAC system.

“In doing research and other projects, it’s clear that the cost-to-pleasure ratio would have been worth it,” he says now. “We regret not putting the warm floors in.”

While windows might sometimes be an afterthought, Keltner and Gauthier have advice: Go with triple-glazed. “Windows provide heat and cool insulation as well as security,” explains Keltner. “And they can define both the outside and the inside of a space.” The couple went with triple-glazed windows from Austrian-based firm Bewiso that come pre-wired for easy connection to alarm systems. “They’re almost like hurricane windows,” she says.

“You can't break through three panes of glass,” says Gauthier. Due to the building’s landmark status, they had to use historic-style windows on the front facade. “We were able to get these old-fashioned type of windows that when you close them, they feel like you're closing a Mercedes,” says Gauthier. “They completely silence the outdoors.”

The final piece of advice is perhaps the most crucial: “Hire the best contractor you can afford,” says Gauthier.

“The typical advice is to get a few bids and throw out the lowest and the highest,” says Keltner. “People usually take the second lowest.” However, that could be the determining factor between an on-time project and one that drags. “You’ll end up paying the difference both in money and time,” she says. “It’s often hard for people to bite the bullet and go with the slightly higher price, but it really pays off.”

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6954. Sarah J. Maas Has a Taylor Swift Problem

Both women are at the height of their game, but building an empire comes with a price.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Welcome back to Prythian, the fictional faerie world Sarah J. Maas has held us hostage in for years now (complimentary). A Court of Splintered Harmony, the sixth book in Sarah’s hugely successful A Court of Thorns and Roses (ACOTAR) series, hit shelves on October 27, 2026, after a five-year wait from its predecessor. That sound you’re hearing right now? That’s the internet collectively falling into Maas Hysteria.

There’s no denying the chokehold Sarah has over romantasy readers. Ever since she exploded onto BookTok during the pandemic, her career has taken on a pop-star level of celebrity status. She sells out bookstores for midnight book release parties like Harry Styles sold out his MSG residency. Professional celeb gossiper Deuxmoi covers blind items about her. To announce the next two books in her ACOTAR series, she went on Alex Cooper’s Call Her Daddy podcast. You know, the same venue Chappell Roan used to reveal her new relationship status and Zayn Malik used to break his six-year interview hiatus.

It’s fitting that her career has taken off like a pop star’s because each new release feels like a Taylor Swift album drop. The noise on social media surrounding their new releases could literally shake the ground you walk on—and you just know they’re going to immediately sell out everywhere. Both their audiences are hugely engaged with sky-high, emotionally specific expectations, but it’s also what’s starting to get them into trouble.

Both women have reached godlike levels of commercial success in their respective fields—and each has faced questions about whether the artistry of their recent work still matches that commercial success. Like Taylor, Sarah’s success and her brand might be the very things usurping her creative output.

Taylor and Sarah built their careers on being true Machiavellians. They have visions for their work that extend beyond one album or book. Sarah described her latest installments to the ACOTAR world as “a four-part volume-defying narrative.” Part one takes place in the newly released A Court of Splintered Harmony (ACOTAR 6). Parts two and three will be released together in January as A Court of Forgotten Melody (ACOTAR 7). Part four has yet to be announced.

If that sounds confusing, well, welcome to the Maasverse. Like Taylor, Sarah has created a universe for her fans complete with its own languages, inside jokes, and, of course, an intricate network of fan theories that would take an FBI task force decades to disentangle. Not only does each book in her series build on the last, exposing twists and turns that were hinted at four or five books ago, but she leaves breadcrumbs for readers from the larger Sarah J. Maas multiverse connecting her three worlds together: Erilia (Throne of Glass), Prythian (ACOTAR), and Midgard (Crescent City).

And it’s making her millions. Since 2015, Maas has sold more than 75 million copies. Her net worth sits somewhere around $60 million. She’s also building on it in a way that was previously only reserved for the likes of The Hunger Games and Twilight—books that crossed over to Hollywood via box-office-breaking films—even with a canceled TV project that is back on the market after several years of development hell. Target released its own ACOTAR-themed collection. Knix dropped a line of lingerie, complete with court-specific panties and negligees (the Nesta Silver Flames corset retails for $150). The series has even ventured into the world of luxury goods with its For Love & Lemons collection, where a Night Court minidress will run you almost $700.

In terms of critical success, though, both women have seen a downward trend with their last works: Taylor’s The Life of a Showgirl and Sarah’s Crescent City: House of Flame and Shadow.

Kirkus called House of Flame and Shadow “pure entertainment, for better or for worse.” Meanwhile, Miss Willa’s Book Club, a popular account on TikTok, said it was an “info dump” noting that, “It was so long and so dense that I absorbed so much less of it.”

“This is kind of the issue with these authors who decide to build these huge universes,” BookTokker Antoinette Cea-Lopes says. “The audiences are left waiting until the end of the universe in order to determine whether or not the author is any good.”

It speaks to the pressure monoliths like Sarah and Taylor face when releasing new work. There is an intense pressure to write more and release faster than ever. However, it’s not enough to write a good story anymore; it needs to be epic. It needs to bring in new fans while upping the stakes for old fans.

When fan expectations are this high, how can anyone—even the reigning queen of faerie smut—ever live up to that hype? Where is the line between giving fans more at the expense of good writing?

As a longtime Maassassin myself (yes, she totally has her own form of Swifties), I hold the bar high because I know what she is capable of. I’ll admit that when she announced a four-part volume-defying narrative, I worried the art was being sacrificed in the name of having a Maasverse. The bigger the worlds got and the more they crossed over, the less attention to detail there would be in the writing. As much as I’d like to see Manon and Nesta have a queen off in the future, that doesn’t mean it serves the larger narrative arc. Precision has always been Sarah’s strength, but the ballooning of her worldview feels more like a brand move and not an artistic one.

Taylor, too, has been accused of putting the brand before the art. The Life of a Showgirl has fans split on whether the album represents a deeply personal account of her happily ever after era or if it’s a full-on marketing cash grab.

The truth is good art is counterintuitive to good branding. It requires time and space, something Sarah, who publishes at a breakneck pace these days (16 books in 14 years over multiple series, to be exact), hasn’t taken advantage of since the early days of her career.

But that’s the thing about branding—it requires a near-constant stream of content. When Sarah first started gaining popularity on TikTok, she already had two completed series for readers to dive into: Throne of Glass and ACOTAR. She then decided to revisit the ACOTAR world and turn it from a trilogy into a massive multi-part series.

Even if I don’t always know where Sarah is going with a plotline, there’s a moment at the end of each book when, as she would say, even the gods give pause. A moment when I can feel in my bones something big is on the horizon. When the story shifts from character study to fantasy epic. But those high-impact moments were missing from the last two ACOTAR offerings, A Court of Frost and Starlight and A Court of Silver Flames.

Despite the criticism, there’s a reason Taylor and Sarah have amassed the fan bases they have and that stick with them no matter what products they produce. Even my recent disappointment won’t stop me from purchasing future reads by her (shoutout to Trope Bookstore’s midnight release party!). Part of being a Maassassin—and a Swiftie—is a willingness to go on the journey with them. And as they’ve both proven, they operate well from the underdog position. Just when the world counts them out, that’s when they strike the hardest.

Although I sometimes like to think of myself as a Suriel (hot, pale, mysterious, gossipy), right now, I can’t see the truth when it comes to the future of Sarah J. Maas. I’ve been wrong about her before and I’m happy to be wrong again. Maybe she’s like the Asteri (IYKYK) gobbling down the book market and pop culture at large—at the risk of her own implosion. Or maybe she’s the true High Queen of Faerie, just biding her time until her vision reaches maturation.

One of her characters once asked, “What if we go on only to more pain and despair?” Dramatically, that is sometimes how I feel as a reader. But I’m reminded of Sarah’s aptly written response to the question, a response that reflects all my feelings about reader loyalty: “Then it is not the end.”

Kennedy first ran for Congress, two candidates named Joe Russo mysteriously turned up against him in the Democratic primary. One of the Joe Russos was a Boston city councilor and a popular local politician. The other one was a janitor. Local historians long ago revealed that Janitor Joe was paid by the Kennedy campaign to run in the primary and split the Joe Russo vote. JFK won in a landslide anyway. Voters are not as dumb as Alaska Republicans say they are.

It allowed people in the same precinct to vote essentially by proxy for those people who otherwise could not vote because, for example, they’d just moved and don’t yet have acceptable proof of residence. At its best, it was an act of kindness, which to people like James O’Keefe is like garlic to a vampire.

#6951. ELLE Decoroscope: Your November Design Horoscope Is Here

The retrogrades end just in time for hosting season.

Welcome to Decoroscopes, ELLE DECOR’s monthly astrology column. Each month, we tap into the cosmic forecast to guide your home design decisions, from major renovations to subtle refreshes. Whether you’re considering a bold paint color or debating a furniture upgrade, let the stars be your guide.

November brings a welcome development for anyone tired of discussing the same thing over and over again: a chance to make a decision. Mercury and Venus both turn direct on November 13, ending weeks of reconsideration around communication, money, relationships, and taste. Before then, the Scorpio New Moon on November 9 asks for some honesty.

Once the decisions start coming, watch how quickly the plans grow. Mars meets Jupiter in Leo on November 16, lending creative projects plenty of confidence—and very little restraint. The Gemini Full Moon on the 24th brings conversations to a head, followed by Mars’s move into practical Virgo on the 25th. Read on for each sign’s November horoscope, design note, and color of the month.

November begins with the less photogenic side of sharing a life: joint expenses, obligations, and agreements that may need updating. The Scorpio New Moon on the 9th encourages a fresh approach, but start by establishing what everyone is actually contributing. When Mercury and Venus turn direct on the 13th, a financial discussion or relationship question may become easier to resolve. Then Mars, your ruling planet, meets Jupiter in your creative sector on the 16th, giving you plenty of appetite for something more enjoyable. Pursue the idea, but leave enough time and money to finish it. Mars enters your routines sector on the 25th; the practical work will want its turn.

Design note: Give a creative project a place to stay out between sessions. This month’s enthusiasm deserves better than being packed away after every use.

Your preferences are not necessarily the problem this month. The challenge is making room for someone else’s without abandoning your own. The Scorpio New Moon on November 9 falls in your partnership sector, opening a conversation about what a relationship or collaboration needs next. Venus, your ruling planet, turns direct on the 13th, alongside Mercury, helping you move beyond the review stage. Meanwhile, Mars and Jupiter meet in your home sector on the 16th, potentially expanding your domestic ambitions. Before the project grows another room, the Gemini Full Moon on the 24th asks you to look at the cost. Enthusiasm is useful, but an agreed budget is better.

Design note: Before expanding a project, finish the part you originally planned.

The first half of November is about making your days less cumbersome. The Scorpio New Moon on the 9th highlights work and routines, while Mercury, your ruling planet, turns direct on the 13th. A process that has required repeated explanations may finally be ready for a simpler version. Later, the Full Moon in your sign on November 24 brings your own needs into focus, particularly where you have been accommodating other people’s expectations. Then Mars enters your home sector on the 25th, bringing energy to domestic projects—and potentially less patience with anyone standing in the way. Ask for help before issuing instructions.

Design note: Start with the part of your home that creates extra work every day—fixing that will matter more than taking on the most visible thing first.

The Scorpio New Moon on November 9 puts creativity and enjoyment ahead of another round of household administration. Give an idea you like enough attention to discover whether you want to pursue it. Venus turning direct in your home sector on the 13th may also bring clarity to a decorating choice or family discussion that has been difficult to settle. Mid-month, Mars and Jupiter meet in your money sector, encouraging confidence about your resources. Just distinguish between having room in the budget and feeling briefly invincible. By the Gemini Full Moon on the 24th, a quieter schedule may sound more appealing than another invitation.

Design note: Spend on something you’ll enjoy using, not something you’ll feel obliged to save for company. Pleasure is part of the budget, too.

You have energy to spare this month, but the people at home may have their own ideas about where it should go. The Scorpio New Moon on November 9 brings attention to your living situation, family, and domestic plans. Mercury turning direct there on the 13th can help restart a conversation that has gone in circles. Then Mars meets Jupiter in your sign on the 16th, making you more inclined to take the lead. That confidence is useful, but assuming everyone has already agreed is less so. When Mars enters your money sector on the 25th, the focus shifts from what you want to do to what you are prepared to pay for.

Design note: Separate what you want to change from what genuinely needs changing.

November starts with a crowded inbox and several details that would benefit from being written down. The Scorpio New Moon on the 9th highlights communication and everyday logistics; Mercury turning direct on the 13th helps you revisit a muddled exchange with a clearer head. The pace changes late in the month: The Sun enters your home sector on the 22nd, the Gemini Full Moon highlights your career on the 24th, and Mars enters your sign on the 25th. You may suddenly feel ready to tackle everything at once, but choose an order. Being capable of handling a project does not mean you need to begin it tonight.

Design note: Decide which project gets your attention first—and let the others wait.

Venus, your ruling planet, turns direct in your sign on November 13. After weeks of reconsidering your preferences, relationships, or presentation, you may feel less inclined to ask for one more opinion. Mercury turns direct the same day in your money sector, where the Scorpio New Moon on the 9th has already encouraged a closer look at income and spending. Together, these shifts favor a clearer sense of what is worth your investment. Late in the month, the Gemini Full Moon draws your attention toward travel, study, or an idea outside your usual frame of reference. Look beyond the accounts and shops you already know by heart.

Design note: Choose the piece you want to live with, not the one that gets the most compliments.

The New Moon in your sign on November 9 gives you a chance to set a personal priority before the month becomes crowded with other people’s requests. Mercury turns direct in Scorpio on the 13th, making it easier to explain a decision you may still have been working out yourself. You do not need elaborate justification, just a clear answer. Mars and Jupiter meet in your career sector on the 16th, supporting a more visible or ambitious move. Then the Gemini Full Moon on the 24th brings shared money and commitments into focus. Make sure the terms support the opportunity, rather than hoping the opportunity will make the terms worthwhile.

Design note: Leave room in the budget for the unglamorous parts of an upgrade.

The first part of November is quieter than your birthday-season reputation might suggest. The Scorpio New Moon on the 9th falls in your private sector, favoring time to think without immediately turning the result into a plan. Mercury and Venus turn direct on the 13th, helping a private concern or friendship question become less tangled. Your season begins on November 22, followed by a Gemini Full Moon in your partnership sector on the 24th. Someone else’s needs may be especially clear, but that does not make yours negotiable by default. With Mars entering your career sector on the 25th, agree on what you can realistically give before work claims the rest .

Design note: If your home doubles as an office, make it possible to put work away. You shouldn’t have to look at tomorrow’s assignments throughout dinner.

November’s opening weeks turn your attention towards friends, collaborators, and plans that require more than your own effort. The Scorpio New Moon on the 9th could help you establish a new connection or give an existing project a clearer purpose. When Mercury and Venus turn direct on the 13th, discussions inviting your wider circle and professional direction may start moving again. The Gemini Full Moon on the 24th highlights your workload and routines, making it easier to see what has become unnecessarily complicated. Before accepting another responsibility, consider whether you want it—or whether everyone has simply become accustomed to you saying yes.

Design note: Retire a household routine that creates more work than it saves.

The Scorpio New Moon on November 9 brings a professional goal into focus. Mercury is still retrograde in that part of your chart until the 13th, so use the early days to refine what you want to say rather than assuming the first version is final. Mid-month, Mars meets Jupiter in your partnership sector, giving a collaboration considerable momentum. It may also make another person’s confidence difficult to distinguish from a workable plan. Ask practical questions. The Gemini Full Moon on the 24th turns attention towards creativity and enjoyment, reminding you that a personal project does not need a business case to deserve your time.

Design note: Give an unfinished creative project another chance before clearing it away.

November begins with an invitation to expand your references. The Scorpio New Moon on the 9th favors study, travel, or a subject that draws you beyond your usual interests. Mercury turning direct there on the 13th helps you return to a plan with better information. Later, the Gemini Full Moon on November 24 lands in your home sector, bringing a domestic matter to a point of clarity. You may recognize that a room, arrangement, or family expectation needs to change. Mars enters your partnership sector the following day, making direct discussion more important—and potentially more heated. State the problem without assigning someone the role of villain.

Design note: Identify the change you want before discussing how to achieve it.

#6950. Get Ready for the Holiday Season With These Timeless, Customizable Cards

Minted makes it easy to send personalized cheer to everyone you love.

Last year I spent a long weekend with a friend I hadn’t seen in a decade. When I got home, I sent her the usual ebullient text about how wonderful it was to see her again at last and how much the time meant to me. Oddly, she didn’t reply; I thought she’d ghosted me.

About two weeks later, I dragged myself begrudgingly to my mailbox to collect the usual bunch of mailers, bills, credit card offers, and insurance solicitations. I’d planned to stop by the recycle bucket and toss the whole stack. Then I saw something amid the circulars: a thick, silvery envelope, addressed with lavishly scrawled panache, stamped with sealing wax and postmarked with a limited edition portrait postage stamp featuring a favorite poet. The return address showed it came from my friend.

I was over the moon. Amid all the usual generic garbage, here was a gem—something written just for me. I rushed into the hall, found an actual letter opener in a drawer, and mindfully—lovingly—slit the envelope open to find an artist-designed card by Minted. Inside was a loving note from my friend, far more profound and personal than my erasable text to her. This card was something almost sacred that I could touch, feel, save, and read over and over again.

In a digital age of AI generated missives, disappearing chats, and emails nobody takes the time to read more than the first two sentences of, a letter or note in the form of a greeting card is an anomaly. Even holiday cards have, in many cases, gone digital, crammed between promotional emails and work obligations in our inboxes, blasé versions of their former snazzy, personalized selves. But aren’t we all craving more? Something we can treasure? Don’t we hanker to send something to friends and loved ones as special as the festive season itself?

Enter Minted, a clever stationery and design brand with a mission to use the designs of independent artists and elevated printing techniques—such as letterpress and foil—to bring back the art of card sending and letter writing. Their high end products provide first class alternatives that infuse any dispatch with gravitas, making communicating a mindful act. Bring on the sealing wax and calligraphy pens!

These stunning cards make a statement anytime of the year—but especially over the holidays. Their soul-felt artistry embodies the Japanese concept of geidō: the way of the arts, where every action is meant to be a ceremonial—artistic—celebration of life. Minted’s 2026 array of holiday options embrace heritage motifs, vintage details, vibrant colours, and candid photography. Minted Concierge offers complimentary one-on-one guidance to help you find holiday cards that fit your style and message, then personalize every detail—from the layout and photo to envelopes, liners, and stamps.

For a more keepsake-worthy finish, Photo Mount™ pairs a semi-gloss photo print, mounted by hand on thick premium cardstock, with a choice of foil, letterpress, or standard printing; Minted’s letterpress option gives soft cotton paper a deep, tactile impression, with ink hand-mixed in small batches and each piece reviewed for color and texture. New this year, both Peel & Seal and Mail It For Me make sending holiday cards easier. For larger mailings, Peel & Seal envelopes make sending simpler: a concealed adhesive strip seals with a press, no moisture needed, and select envelope colors are available. On select eligible card formats, Mail It For Me can handle printing, addressing, assembly, postage, and mailing through USPS for an additional fee. Minted confirms when the cards are handed off and will ship you any extra unaddressed cards and envelopes for last-minute list adds.

Greeting cards weren’t always computer generated, overly edited, and filtered to death; they’ve evolved over two centuries. While the first holiday greeting card was introduced in the UK in 1843 as a festive, shorthand option for Christmas letter writers who lacked the time to pen several missives at once, they were popularized during the Victorian era. For decades afterwards, decorative cards trended, each an elaborate work of art. As printing and personal photography became more sophisticated and widespread, so did the cards. Sending and receiving holiday cards was a de rigueur tradition, their appearance evoking the styles of each decade. But in digital times, greeting cards began to lose favor, often replaced (poorly!) by a quick email or text. The pandemic brought a resurgence—an indication that people sought to connect in old school ways—and new generations have taken a renewed interest in sending unique holiday cards.

Designed by independent artists, more relevant than ever, Minted’s custom cards bring the age-old tradition into the future. Combining modern tools and the human touch, Minted ensures that sending holiday cards personalizes the moment, with artistry and emotion that make opening every card feel like unwrapping a meaningful gift. Offering cards with contemporary flourishes as well as those that are imbued with vintage vibes, Minted’s options allow the senders to express themselves and set a mood.

Consider Starry Wonder by Alston Foster, one of 2026’s most celestial designs. It boasts a retro-inspired border made of glittery stars, designed to frame a favorite photo.

Whimsical Cut Paper Party by Inkblot Design pairs fluid, colorful shapes with a look that feels both sophisticated and playful. Fully customizable, the artist-designed card is available in myriad shapes and formats.

Photomatic by Ani Somi delves into the photo booth realm, presenting a perfect place to display your silliness and party spirit. The foil-pressed accents gleam, catching the light like tinsel on a Christmas tree.

Choose Modjeska by chocomocacino to connect with the past, perhaps your own childhood memories of the season. From the atelier of an independent artist, this design comes in a range of shapes and formats, each crafted to help tell your story.

Customize your cards with distinctive fonts and greetings, plus colorful backers or envelope liners, to make them uniquely yours. With dots that call to mind holiday lights aglow, Delicate Dots by Ekko Studio pairs shimmering colors with foil-pressed accents for a celebratory, bespoke card.

In the same way you might enjoy meticulously wrapping a gift, baking a holiday dish, or trimming a tree, taking the extra time to send a meaningful greeting to families and friends may be as much a gift to yourself as it is to those who receive your brilliant Minted-made card. Minted makes it easy to celebrate the season and foster connection.

#6948. Did Sharna Burgess and Emma Slater Accidentally Confirm This Celeb and ‘DWTS’ Pro Are Secretly Dating?

In today’s edition of surprise Dancing With the Stars updates, it seems Emma Slater and Sharna Burgess might be playing matchmaker.

During a TikTok livestream on the official DWTS account on Monday night, the besties (who were both eliminated last week) answered fan questions alongside fellow eliminated Hailey Bills…and subtly addressed the serious chemistry between Hailey and one of this season’s contestants in the process.

In response to Sharna asking, “Do people want to see Ezra Frech?” Hailey responded, “They probably do!” And it didn’t take long for Emma to turn the attention to Hailey—which is pretty telling, given the rumors that Hailey and Ezra might be secretly dating.

“How much do you wanna see Ezra Frech?” Emma asked with a smile, to which Hailey replied, “Me?” before she realized what was going on. “I would love to see Ezra Frech!” she responded with laughter, as Emma added with a wink, “Ezra, Hailey would love to see you!”

Elsewhere during the same livestream, Ezra confirmed he wasn’t seeing anyone, saying, “I’m single. I’m locked in on dancing.”

When Sharna conveniently asked if the athlete was “interested in that changing,” after chatting with Hailey, he doubled down, saying, “I’m locked in on dancing right now. That’s all I’ll say.”

Neither Hailey nor Ezra has publicly addressed the romance rumors, but I’m all for this ballroom connection. Also, Emma and Sharna…I’m single too, just saying!!

#6947. Why Is Everything Suddenly Coming Up Christopher Abbott?

It’s not just the actor’s You Can See Everything shocker—it’s his ability to discover something meaningful in East of Eden, Death of a Salesman, and soon, a Marvel superhero blockbuster, that few in Hollywood could unlock before.

Christopher Abbott has a secret. We’re sitting in the Bowery Hotel’s outdoor patio in Lower Manhattan, making small talk about the New York Knicks and life in the city as we gauge who might bring it up first. He disguises well. No one around us recognizes the forty-year-old actor in his washed red button-up and jeans. And they don’t know it yet, but when the movie premieres in a few days, Abbott will make a surprise appearance in Nathan Fielder’s exceptional Elizabeth Holmes documentary, You Can See Everything.

He’s hesitant to say anything, especially in a room full of potential eavesdroppers. Spoilers of any kind in the film industry are to be guarded with your life. Abbott’s already kept his role hidden for more than a year since they filmed. Why stop now? Eventually, I cave and reveal that I’ve seen the movie already. His part in the new film is impossible to ignore, and if you haven’t seen You Can See Everything yet, here’s your warning that a major spoiler is coming in the next paragraph.

“I don’t think I even knew what I was going to do,” Abbott says about joining the documentary. The actor appears in the second half of the film—in theaters now—because the director lost all access to his subject after Holmes was sent to prison mid-filming. So Fielder pulled a familiar trick out of his sleeve. He called in Amanda Seyfried (who won an Emmy Award for playing Holmes in Hulu’s The Dropout) and Abbott to reenact previously unrecorded scenes of Holmes and her equally manipulative partner, Billy Evans, respectively. It’s a transcendent twist.

Watching how the gears turned in Fielder’s mind was simply an opportunity Abbott couldn’t pass up. “I wanted to see how these things work,” he says. “After a long time of doing this, you realize you can curate a little bit. Because of how long these things take to come out, I don’t know what the world is going to look like then. It might all blow up.” But when I saw the film in a packed, sold-out theater on opening weekend, the audience gasped in delight when the camera panned up to reveal Abbott on screen. Now The New York Times and others are predicting that You Can See Everything might become the first documentary to receive a nomination among the feature films for Best Picture at the Academy Awards.

For Abbott, recognition is new. The actor has long excelled in brooding indie films. He can draw you into a scene just with his eyes—deep brown and ardent—which are capable of evoking great feeling before he even says a word. It’s a skill many actors work toward but may never achieve—the ability to engage an audience even when your character is simply listening to your costars in between your own lines. He’s as skilled at staring off in anguish as he is at intensely boring into his scene mate’s soul. So why is Hollywood just now catching up? Well, “you got to kind of hunt for it,” Abbott says.

Since his breakout role on HBO’s Girls as Marnie’s (Allison Williams) smothering boyfriend Charlie, Abbott’s career has been a series of almosts. James White, a 2015 Sundance drama about a self-destructive twenty-something who takes care of his terminally ill mother, first garnered him accolades as a leading man capable of a serious dramatic role. At the premiere, people clapped him on the back and told him it was time to get his tickets to the rocket ship. It’s only up from here, they said.

“The number of times people would be like, ‘Chris, when this movie comes out, get ready!’ ” he continues. Abbott went on to costar in First Man with Ryan Gosling. He appeared alongside Emma Stone in Yorgos Lanthimos’s Poor Things, and he led Bring Them Down with Barry Keoghan. “Things did happen, in little ways,” he says. “But there’s been so many ups and downs.” He kept working.

“I’ve been doing this long enough where I’ve had multiple moments where I felt like I was having ‘moments,’ ” Abbott says. It’s a somber thought, but Abbott knows more than most that plenty of working actors never hear their numbers called. “I don’t feel like I’m owed anything,” he adds. But the moment’s here.

This year, Abbott took on two roles that many actors have struggled to bring something new and meaningful to for the past seventy years. He was nominated for a Tony Award for playing Death of a Salesman’s Biff Loman as a character to be feared instead of pitied. The revival wrapped up in August following 167 shows. Abbott also starred on Netflix’s critically acclaimed East of Eden miniseries, adapted from John Steinbeck’s sweeping 1952 novel­, as Adam Trask—a despondent father far more beaten down by the world than previous portrayals would ever allow.

Still, it’s more than just that he earned the parts, or that he has seemed suddenly ubiquitous. Where others failed to leave a mark, Abbott found a deeper humanity in roles that few in Hollywood could unlock before. Even when the industry feels like it’s eating itself and spitting itself back out, churning out content and forgetting to care about the result, Abbott is a reminder that great performances are still waiting to be discovered. If we let him, he knows the way to take us there.

Another very big change in his life may have something to do with it.

Two months ago, Christopher Abbott and his partner, actress Aubrey Plaza, walked into a hospital and left with a baby. One minute, you’re two people. Then you’re three. Wild, he thought.

“You can be a wholesome person, but for whatever reason at that moment you feel like a delinquent,” Abbott tells me. “All of a sudden, you’re Sid and Nancy, just going, ‘You’re going to let us do this?’ ”

When Abbott was younger, he never envisioned himself as a father. He was a hungry rookie. He ran the indie-film-festival circuit, snuck into auditions, and took the stage for an off-Broadway show nearly every night of the week. He dreaded the nightmare of being the parent trying to calm a crying baby on an airplane. Hell, he feared the commitment of even staying on Girls for more than two seasons.

“But at a certain age, I would start to see a father with his kid, just randomly on the street, and think, I would like to find out what that’s all about,” Abbott says now. “It’s something that obviously a lot of people do, but when it’s yourself, it’s better than any movie, really.”

It’s the same way he talks about how he started acting in the first place. “It wasn’t like some holy moment,” he says of the first time he read for a scene. He was a bit lost, taking random electives while studying at Norwalk Community College in Connecticut, where he grew up. Psychology was his passion at the time, but he guesses that his interest in human behavior fit with what seemed to be an instinct for acting. It just clicked.

When you see Abbott act now, even as the naive yet determined Adam Trask in East of Eden, you wonder just whom he might have picked up these idiosyncrasies from. How long does it take to erase Christopher Abbott from the viewers’ mind and replace it with Trask?

“At the end of the day, the process is the only thing I have to take home with me,” he says. “Most of the things I’ve done, I’m not in control of the end of product. All I have is the memory of the doing of the thing. So I need to make sure that I try my best to enjoy the pure acting part of it.”

And Abbott will make sure he enjoys it, even it kills him. In 2023, he refused to step away from an off-Broadway production after he tore his meniscus. Costarring with Plaza in 2023’s revival of Danny and the Deep Blue Sea, he performed for five weeks on crutches.

“I work hard. I really do,” Abbott says. “I can joke around a lot about this business. That’s how I cope. But the thing is, I need to give myself some credit, because I work my ass off.”

No one can say Abbott didn’t fight for this moment.

How about the big, bald-headed news? Disney announced this summer that Abbott is set to play superhero mentor Charles Xavier, also known as Professor X, in Marvel’s X-Men reboot. The blockbuster film, directed by Jake Schreier (Beef, Thunderbolts), hits theaters in May 2028. He’ll star alongside just about every up-and-coming actor in Hollywood right now, including Inde Navarrette, Sadie Sink, and Kit Connor. While Abbott waits to read the script—there isn’t one yet—he’s keeping it all in perspective.

“I’m excited to approach it with some real-deal kind of character work,” he says. “There’s going to be a lot of things to chew on. But I got to read it first to see where they go with it.”

Even my question of Bald cap or shaved head? for the famously cue-ball character couldn’t be further from where Abbott’s mind is right now. But he’s undecided, if you’re wondering. “My hair’s pretty thick,” he says. “I’ve shaved my head for things before, and I’m down. But if my head is anything like my face, three hours later it’s going to be back. So I’m not sure because he’s not shaving his head every day. I want it to feel right.”

The actor is painfully aware that one X-Men film doesn’t turn into the job security of a half dozen Marvel outings unless it’s a massive hit. For now, Abbott just wants to make sure X-Men is a great film, period. “It’s also just nice to look forward to something and a character that I think actually has some layers and depth,” he adds.

It helps that Abbott has a baby at home to think of now too. “There are hard parts, but it’s also fun to watch everything, the little growths,” he says. It’s a long way from the twenty-something who felt trapped if he stayed on one job for too long.

But raising a child? There may be emotional centers in your brain that you can’t even access until you become a father. Then it all comes pouring out. As he says, it’s better than any movie. Plus, it’s sure to show up in his work. He figured out Death of a Salesman and East of Eden before his daughter was even born. Imagine what he’s capable of next.

#6944. Why Tatyana Ali’s Subtle ‘DWTS’ Team Dance Comment Has Fans Fully Speculating

It has DWTS fans connecting some very dramatic dots.

The people’s princess of Dancing With the Stars, Tatyana Ali, isn’t holding back after last night’s team dance. The Fresh Prince of Bel-Air alum thanked fans for their support on Threads after the show and encouraged them to vote for her and her pro partner, Jan Ravnik. She also took some time to answer her followers’ questions, and let’s just say one particular response had fans raising eyebrows.

When someone asked how she went about choosing who she’d dance with on Team Slaytopia, the actor opened up about the real intention behind her decision. “Tatyana… as the lowest scored person why wouldn’t you choose atleast ONE of the stars with a dance background for tour team ?” one person wrote on Threads Monday night.

Another fan responded, “Maybe I’m reading too much into it but she strikes me as someone who is very intentional about who she shares space with. And I think who she picked was a reflection of that.”

Tatyana seemingly confirmed the second fan was on point with their theory when she responded, “Very much so.”

Tatyana didn’t say much, but that didn’t stop people from reading into her three-word response. Fans theorized she may have taken a subtle swipe at Val Chmerkovskiy and Jenna Johnson after all that LiftGate drama that went down a few weeks ago.

“I saw this on threads this morning and it looks like folks were right that she wanted to avoid working with Val/Jenna J after the controversy a couple weeks prior,” one fan on Reddit speculated, while another added, “Not even necessarily about avoiding them but I think she did want to be with Maura [Higgins], Amber [Glenn] and Julia [Stiles] more too. She seems like a girls girl so it makes sense even if we’re ignoring the drama with that family.”

While there’s no confirmation that her team dance decision tied back to the controversy, it did surround Tatyana’s show, The Fresh Prince of Bel-Air, when Val referenced Will Smith’s infamous Oscars slap in 2022 while defending his wife against Carrie Ann Inaba’s critiques.

Either way…so happy to see Tatyana and Jan are still in the running to win the coveted Mirrorball Trophy!

#6941. Stephen Graham’s Wife Would Like Him to Stop Following Strangers (Literally)

The Adolescence star plays an alluring serial killer in Nocturne. But his own fixation on studying people often leads to awkward encounters. “It was a lot easier when my face wasn’t as recognizable as it is now,” the actor tells Esquire.

Stephen Graham can’t help himself. The Emmy-winning actor doesn’t mean any harm. He just gets fixated on someone, and then...

“I watch people,” he says. “I used to watch people in train stations. Watch walks. And watch how they behave. Watch their mannerisms.” He thinks of this as ongoing performer training. “I’ll store it for later in a Rolodex in me mind,” Graham says in his lilting Liverpool accent, tapping his temple. “You absorb as much as you can. Then you just allow these things to come out.”

Graham plays a charismatic serial killer in the new Apple TV series Nocturne, and while he obviously doesn’t have the character’s murderous tendencies, the two do share a fascination with silently studying those around them. “It was a lot easier when my face wasn’t as recognizable as it is now,” the 53-year-old actor adds.

He became a familiar face in his role as Al Capone on Boardwalk Empire. Then with his scene-stealing performances in Martin Scorsese’s The Irishman and as Bruce Springsteen’s volatile father in Deliver Me from Nowhere. Winning Emmys, BAFTAs, and a Golden Globe for his work in front of and behind the camera on the harrowing crime drama Adolescence made it much harder to study people without being noticed. “I still do it now,” he says, before his wife cuts in.

“You get caught a lot now,” she corrects him.

Graham shrugs. “Now I get caught a lot,” he agrees.

It’s a sunny afternoon in the English Midlands, which is where Graham and his wife, Hannah Walters, keep their home. He’s speaking to Esquire via Zoom, his face covered with a scruffy beard and his hair lightly mussed. He’s wearing a bright-blue Teamsters Memorial Picnic T-shirt and a black-rimmed pair of glasses. The actor could pass as either a pub bouncer or a college professor.

“I’m at home, in the kitchen. We’re being very English,” he says. “My wife is making a cottage pie.” He spins the laptop around to face her. “Say hello, darling.”

Walters is also an actor, best known for the film and TV series Boiling Point, and she was a producer with her husband on Adolescence, costarring as the schoolteacher Mrs. Bailey. But she’s not feeling camera ready at the moment, stirring a steaming saucepan full of minced meat as loose locks of blond hair frame her face. “How very dare you…?” she says to Graham in her most haughty dowager voice before waving cheerfully.

She wasn’t officially involved in the making of Nocturne, but Walters was crucial in helping her husband wrap his head around the character of Jurek Walter, a serial killer whose crimes inexplicably continue even though he is locked up in a criminal asylum. He’s a soft-spoken manipulator, a master of analyzing behavior, and he’s able to wheedle into the minds of virtually anyone he encounters. Liev Schreiber plays a lawman on the outside who’s trying to figure out how Walter continues to puppeteer disappearances, and Zazie Beetz is an FBI agent who goes undercover in the detention facility to get close to him, at great personal risk. “And she was like, ‘Well, okay…’ ” The instruction began. “My research was watching crime documentaries with my wife.”

What did she say makes a good killer? “What makes a good killer?” he repeats to the cottage-pie baker.

She laughs off camera. “I don’t think anybody’s a good killer, are they? That’s an ironic statement,” she says. “But what makes a successful serial killer? I think intelligence. There’s a lot of serial killers that aren’t intelligent, but they get found out very quickly.”

“Highly intelligent. Pragmatic,” he says.

“We watched a lot of different people and just nicked little bits from each one,” Graham says. “Then your job as an actor is to try and create somebody who is believable, who is three-dimensional. Looking at the script, I saw the manipulation that Jurek is capable of and how he is a chameleon and he’ll adapt to each situation. So he reads the situation first and plays what he thinks it is that you want.”

That was about getting into Walter’s head. The second part of his prep was getting the right body. “I was getting myself in shape physically, where I looked like I could kill someone with my bare hands,” Graham says, holding his mitts out in front of him. “That was also one of my aims. Then you just build the character together with the director and with the writers.”

Showrunner John Hlavin (The Shield) says a lot of the work was already done by the time Graham arrived on the Pittsburgh set. “I mean, I’m not going to tell Stephen Graham how to do it. You know what I mean?” Hlavin says. “You’re dealing with a top line of professional athlete.” His contribution was making sure that Jurek Walter remained distinct from other infamous screen sociopaths, like Hannibal Lecter, who overpowers people with his erudition and mind games. Jurek Walter wins them over with vulnerability and sympathy.

Hlavin was struck by how quickly Graham could flip into character. “You’ve met him. He really likes to play. He’s very funny. He’s empathetic and personable. He sometimes would come to set singing, and it would echo around,” Hlavin says. “Then he snaps in. I would rehearse with him, we could be going over lines or making adjustments, and he would drop into character and I would be terrified. He’d go from friendly Stephen, chatting about the Steelers game he was invited to, to Jurek Walter.”

Graham gets physically animated when he talks about playing the role, like a little kid opening up a gift box that has something very dangerous inside. While his characters all have different mannerisms and voices, they often share a common trait: intimidation.

Maybe it’s his stocky, bruiser build that gets him cast in these roles, or maybe the cheerful Liverpudlian is just especially good at suppressing his own upbeat nature. He admits to getting a little overeager, especially when it comes to studying people in the wild.

That’s why it’s ironic that his wife, who was so helpful in guiding him to find the character of Jurek Walter, is so determined to hold him back from getting too close to passersby who intrigue him. “We were in London about two weeks ago, and some fella just started walking,” Graham says, rolling his shoulders back and forth. Imagine a person who moves like a too-tall stack of dishes.

“We both looked at him and she grabbed me by the arm and went, ‘Don’t,’ ” Graham says. “Because I’ll follow his walk, I’ll do his walk. I went, ‘But it’s a great walk!’ And she went, ‘Don’t you dare. Don’t you dare.’ I was like, ‘Arrgh … okay.’ ”

Not only is it awkward when the person spots him, but now that he’s renowned around the world, there are public-relations concerns to think about. His wife speaks up from across the kitchen: “Stephen Graham Followed Me into the London Underground—Headline!”“We’re influenced by so many different things,” Graham says. “Do you know what I mean?”

So if you find yourself on the street someday, being watched by someone who looks like the Emmy winner, don’t be alarmed. But you might see your distinctive walk end up onscreen someday.

#6938. Plant Experts Say Right Now Is the Best Time to Buy These 13 Essentials for Next Year’s Garden

You can save some serious cash by shopping early.

If you love to dig in the dirt, it’s never too early to think about next year’s garden. While this year’s growing season is winding down, planning ahead can save you money and time next spring. Most garden centers, local nurseries, and online vendors are clearing out stock now. Plus, anything you buy now will be ready for the first sunny days next spring when you can’t wait to get outdoors again.

“Fall is the ideal time to take advantage of opportunities like helping garden centers clear out what they can’t store over winter or sell the following year,” says Stephanie Rose, author of The Wild & Free Garden and founder of Garden Therapy. “This is also true for the secondhand marketplace, as many neighbors will want to rehome garden supplies and plants, particularly perennial divisions, to avoid winter storage. Check your community buy-and-sell groups in fall to stock up for the spring garden.”

As a garden writer, my personal favorite stock-up item this time of year is garden gloves. I typically go through two to three pairs of the nitrile-coated gloves per season. Because you can toss them in the washer after use, I like to keep a few pairs on hand—so I’m always grabbing a second or third pair when I see them on sale in the fall.

Ahead, here’s what else gardening experts say to stock up on now to save money and get ready for next spring.

Spreading compost or amendments in garden beds now gives them more time to work into the soil before spring planting season, and you’ll probably find some good deals on them.

“I also like to stock up on end of season mulch so it’s there in the spring when I want it,” says Natalie Carmolli, public relations specialist and shrub expert with Proven Winners ColorChoice Shrubs. “Just be sure to cover it so it doesn’t get waterlogged in the winter.” Stash a bag or two in your shed or under tarps so it’s ready for early spring plantings without needing to make a trip to the home store.

Even if you’re careful, outdoor containers get weathered and don’t last forever.

“Look especially for decorative containers, which can get really expensive. If you know you'll need some next year, fall clearance is a great time to grab them,” says landscape designer Katherine Aul Cervoni, founder and principal of Staghorn NYC. “Pottery Barn and Rejuvenation have some of my favorites.”

And if it’s time to repot some of your houseplants that have outgrown their containers, you’ll find some deals now.

“Anything that retailers don’t want to store for the season is easy to pick up at a discount,” says Rose. “Lanterns, string lights, and pathway lights are fabulous finds for fall, given that the days are shorter and a little extra lighting will help extend your time in the garden.”

You may think it’s too late to plant, but fall actually is one of the best times to plant deciduous shrubs!

“Cooler air and warm soil let roots establish without summer heat stress, so plants come out of dormancy in spring with a head start,” says Carmolli. “The exception is evergreens: they don’t love being planted late in the season and are better off waiting until spring. Because they don’t go dormant, planting them before their roots have a chance to get fully established can hurt their survival.”

The same goes for perennials. “They get deeply discounted in fall when they are finished blooming,” says Rose. “Garden centers won’t want to spend a year tending to them, so they will often price them on clearance. Fall is the best time to plant perennials, so you can do a major garden glow-up on a budget.”

Are your pruning shears dull and don’t cut cleanly any longer? Is the handle on your trowel loose? Do you need a sturdier garden spade? Autumn is a great time to replace and upgrade tools.

“Grab them now when you’re thinking about it,” says horticulturist Justin Hancock, director of research and development and horticultural content with Costa Farms and author of The New Complete Guide to Houseplants. “Plus you’ll usually find them on clearance in the fall.”

“As long as they're stored somewhere cool and dry, most seeds will keep for at least a few years,” says Cervoni. “Heads up, though: some varieties, such as carrots and spinach, have a much shorter shelf life of one year tops.”

Think trays, heat mats, starter pots, and seed-starting mix. If you start seeds indoors in late winter to early spring, any supplies you find now will likely be cheaper than in February when they hit the shelves again, says Carmolli.

Tomato cages, trellises, stakes, and plant rings are things you don't necessarily think about until you need them, and then suddenly you're scrambling to find them.

“I personally am going to invest in a taller tomato cage for my cherry tomatoes since they always end up taller than I remember,” says Cervoni.

Hose nozzles, watering wands, sprinklers, and other fittings are a great category for purchasing in the fall.

“I feel like there's always something that needs replacing come spring and you'll be happy you already have them on hand once the warm weather arrives,” says Cervoni.

Whether you’re planning to start seeds indoors next spring, bringing houseplants indoors to overwinter, or you just want to give your year-round houseplants an extra boost of light during the dark winter months, consider buying grow lights.

“If you want to keep your houseplants growing optimally through the winter, investing in some supplemental lighting can make a big difference as days grow shorter and the light gets more intense,” says Hancock. “You don’t necessarily need fancy and expensive plant lights. Even an LED light can give your houseplants a little extra boost.”

Fall is the time to buy these anyway, but you can sometimes find great deals late in the season. Get them planted before the ground freezes. This varies by zone, but it’s typically no later than the end of the year across most zones because bulbs need time in the ground to stimulate spring blooming, says Cervoni.

What’s better than curling up with a good book and a cup of hot cocoa as the weather gets too cold to garden?

“Fall is the perfect time to buy gardening and houseplant books so you can dig in and get information and inspiration all winter long for an even more successful spring garden, keeping your green thumb alive all winter,” says Hancock.

Everyone's skin is different, and an eye cream that works for one reviewer may not work for you. There are a few different methods for quickly reducing eye puffiness. To depuff your eyes fast, you can dunk your face in an ice bath or apply cold compresses to your eyes, Cheung says. She also says that placing chilled cucumber slices or damp tea bags on your eyes can depuff in a pinch as well.

Damp tea bags are especially useful because you'll get the cooling effects as well as some caffeine for reducing inflammation. Giving yourself a lymphatic massage (with your hands or an ice roller) can help to move any puffiness-inducing fluid around and deflate under-eye bags as well.

#6934. Rosie Huntington-Whiteley on the Quiet Power of Saint Laurent

The latest Women’s Ready-to-Wear collection and the house’s new Simone bag speak to a style built on restraint, tension, and silent confidence.

Rosie Huntington-Whiteley has one rule when she gets dressed: “I never want to feel as though the clothes are wearing me.” It has served her well for more than two decades, first as one of fashion’s most recognizable faces, then as muse, and now now as an entrepreneur in her own right. It also embodies why Saint Laurent has been a constant.

“I have always felt like a Saint Laurent woman,” she says. Part of the pull is the house’s history, a way of dressing that Yves Saint Laurent defined in the 1960s that still feels charged today. Part of it is simpler: she has always wanted clothes that let her feel like herself, and Saint Laurent does that artfully.

For Fall 26, the collection harkens the house’s founding codes with tailoring that feels looser and more sensual than ever. It was the first thing Huntington-Whiteley noticed. “There’s something incredibly powerful about that Saint Laurent silhouette, particularly the way it feels so fluid and sensual this season,” she reflects. “I’ve always loved a beautifully cut suit worn against bare skin.”

The season’s other headline is Simone, a new bag drawn from Monsieur Saint Laurent’s personal letters. The envelope pouches he favored in the 1970s and 1980s were made for evening. This iteration of the shape is made for every day, with a softer, more structured build, two compartments, a zipped pocket inside, and a magnetic snap that keeps the line clean. On the front sits the new double Cassandre, a raised, dimensional take on the house monogram.

It suits the way Huntington-Whiteley thinks about her own closet. Her most treasured pieces are the Saint Laurent leather jackets she has collected over the years. “There’s something about a Saint Laurent leather jacket that only gets better with time,” she says. “Each one begins to hold memories and becomes part of your identity.” Simone is made in that same spirit. Cut in supple leather and meant to be carried every day rather than saved for a night out, it is a bag designed to be lived in, and to collect a few memories of its own.

Huntington-Whiteley’s own style comes is built on constants. “I’m drawn to a sense of restraint, clean lines, strong silhouettes, beautiful materials and pieces that have a timeless quality to them,” she offers. “There’s often a tension between the masculine and feminine in the way I dress.”

That push and pull is what keeps her coming back to Saint Laurent. “Masculine and feminine, restraint and sensuality, strength and vulnerability,” she says. “Those contradictions have always been at the heart of Saint Laurent.” More than six decades after Yves Saint Laurent founded his house in Paris, it is still finding new ways to make those codes feel modern.

Ask her to sum up the Saint Laurent woman and she doesn’t hesitate. “She’s sensual without being overtly feminine, powerful without losing her sexuality, and there’s always an element of mystery,” she says. “It’s an aesthetic that doesn’t ask for attention, but inevitably commands it.”

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

When it comes to buying home decor, I follow a strict “bougie on a budget” philosophy. All of the above, and then some.

It’s all too easy to turn on a portable lamp, forget about it, and wind up with zero battery the next time you need a light. With this fixture, I can light my kitchen for 15, 30, or 60 minutes and have it magically turn off on its own. Now that’s a bright idea.
