#6932. 6 Safety Razors That’ll Make You Swear Off Multiblade and Electric Shaving

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

One blade gives you a smoother shave and is better for your skin.

If you shave your face with any sort of frequency (daily, every other day, weekly), chances are you use a multiblade cartridge razor—a Gillette razor is what smart money would bet on. It’s the most common option for a reason. Cartridge razors are cheap, readily available, and easy to pick up new blades for (which frequently aren’t as cheap). But if you’ve often found yourself suffering from razor bumps, ingrown hairs, or razor burn—all issues that come readily to those with coarse or thick hair—you might need a course correction. That is because multiblade razors tend to cut too close. Or, well ... it’s not that they cut too close; they cut too much. By the time three to five blades track over a hair follicle, it’s underneath the outer layer of the skin. At that point, it’s simple mathematics that some of these hairs will not grow back perfectly straight out of your skin. They get stuck underneath your epidermis and cause those big, fat ingrown pimples.

Out in the world of shaving, there are single-blade razors, safety razors, multiblade cartridge razors, hell, even electric razors. In the past decade or so, traditional single-blade razors, aka safety razors, have enjoyed a bit of a renaissance. These blades are particularly good for men with sensitive skin or that thick, coarse hair. Safety razors, since they have only one blade, are able to shave close to the skin without going overboard. That comes with some risk. It is easier to cut yourself with, since the blade is sharp and unprotected by some of the features—like moisturizing bars—that you find in cartridge razors. There’s also a pretty steep learning curve associated with them, particularly if you come from the cartridge-razor world.

For those who put in the work and get the hang of it, many say a safety razor gives a far superior shave. And they do save you money in the long run. What else are you waiting for? Oh, for me to tell you what the best ones are. Easy enough. For my money, these six are the best safety razors you can buy.

Some might say Bevel was responsible for the safety-razor renaissance of the past decade with the release of this razor (one of its very first products). The thought behind this design, and the reason many people prefer safety razors, is that they don’t cut as close to the skin as multiblade razors, which helps prevent razor bumps and ingrown hairs. That’s a great thing for men with coarse and/or curly hair, which tends to form ingrowns more easily—and it’s also great for anyone with sensitive skin.

This handy razor is on the weighty side at over four ounces, which can be a good thing for a safety razor, and the sleek design is easy to understand and use. The double-edged blades are easy to replace using the screw top. Whether you’re a safety-razor beginner or expert, this is always going to be a good pick.

If you’re new to safety razors, investing in an expensive one might not be your top priority. This budget-friendly razor is all you need in order to get the hang of safety-razor shaving. It has a traditional screw-top design that uses the same double-edged blades as some of the more expensive handles on this list. The handle has a good heft to it that, combined with an easy-to-grip handle, helps you get used to shaving at a 30-degree angle. The closed-comb design is ideal for beginners and pros alike and delivers a smooth, close shave with minimal risk of nicks and cuts. If you’re a beginner, it’s a great option to learn with. If you’re a pro, it’s a great travel option so you can leave your pricier tool at home.

If you’re starting shaving with a safety razor from scratch, there are a few things to know. The two key elements are the weight of the razor and the angle of the blade. You want a handle that is hefty and feels heavy in your hand, not so light that you’re tempted to move it too quickly over the surface of your skin. The right weight lets the blade do most of the work and allows you to shave your hair with minimal strokes and less risk of nicks. The blade angle should always be about 30 degrees, not perpendicular to your skin, so you usually want to hold the razor at a slight angle.

This well-designed razor from pubic grooming giants Manscaped takes both of those into account. The weight is perfectly hefty, and the blade is actually already designed to sit at a 30 degree angle, so you don’t need to worry as much about how you’re holding it. Both of those things make this razor a perfect jumping off point for those who are coming from the multiblade side.

Safety razors are good for a lot of things, but if you’ve ever used one to try to tackle a few days’ worth of stubble, you know it’s not always great at powering through a lot of growth—at least not with the risk of nicking or cutting yourself going up exponentially. That’s where this safety razor shines. There are six different adjustable settings that subtly widen the space between the razor and the comb, allowing for thicker and coarser hair to be shaved more easily. If you shave every day, you’ll likely want to keep it on the lowest (zero gap) setting, which is basically your classic safety-razor setting, but if you tend to go a few days between shaves, change the setting to allow you to shave your stubble more easily with fewer strokes.

Sometimes you gotta go with a classic. Merkur has been making razors in Germany for over 125 years, and you can’t argue with that pedigree. This traditional safety razor has a traditional screw-top design and a weighted head that allows you to get a silky-smooth shave pretty much immediately. There aren’t any newfangled features in this thing, but that’s kind of the point. The nonslip coating and the perfectly weighted head, as well as the closed-comb design, make this the ideal short handle traditional razor.

Up until this point, I’ve been talking about closed comb razors. The difference, as you can see, is the exposed teeth of the blade on this style, making it sharper but also more of a risky proposition. Knowing your safety razor basics is a must, but if you have the technique down, this is a way to tackle longer or coarser beards.

Don’t get me wrong, this is an expert move. For those of us who grow facial hair faster and can never get a shave as close as we want before it starts growing back, the open comb razor is one way to achieve this. The cleanest shave of your life won’t just feel great, many say it will make your hair grow back slower.

Whether or not you’re experienced with safety razors, there are a few things to take into account when choosing the right one for you.

Weight: You’ll notice that most safety razors are way heavier than the drugstore cartridge razors you may be used to, and it’s not just because they’re made of metal. That’s by design. Safety razors tend to concentrate their weight in the head, which allows them to use gravity to give you a smooth shave without applying too much pressure (which can lead to nicks and cuts). Not all safety razors are the same weight, though. Ultimately you want to find a razor that is heavy enough to give you a good, close shave but not so heavy that your hand gets tired halfway through the process.

Blades: Most safety razors will use a traditional double-edged blade. This allows you to use both sides of the blade to get a little more usage out of it. Some razors will use a single-edged blade, usually called an injector blade, which is often thicker and more durable than its double-edged counterparts. Double-edged blades are preferable, but whichever you choose, make sure refills are readily available, as you’ll need to change the blade every few uses. (Most safety razors, especially those that use double-edged blades, can be refilled with any brand of blade.)

Razor Head Type: Speaking of changing the blades, safety razors typically come with two types of head. The most common is screw-off, where you unscrew the head of the razor in order to change the blade. The downfall of these is that while they’re easy in theory, it means the head comes in multiple parts that must be unscrewed and then screwed back together, making for a somewhat complicated experience, especially for beginners. The other kind, butterfly, pops up in the middle and allows you to drop in the blade.

Open Comb vs. Closed Comb: In this case, “comb” refers to how much distance there is between the razor head and the blade itself. The more open the comb, the more aggressive the shave. Closed comb razors are especially ideal for beginners because they help cut down on the risk of nicks and cuts (as there is no space for skin to get caught). Open-comb razors are ideal for people with thicker beards or longer stubble, as they allow more hair to reach the blade at once, but they carry a higher risk for nicks and cuts.

Adjustable Settings: Some razors will allow you to adjust the settings to take it between a closed and an open comb. Usually this involves different inserts that push space between the blade and the razor head. While not always necessary for everyone, this is ideal if you like to allow time between shaves and sometimes shave with a few days’ worth of stubble or more.

#6926. Sarah Pidgeon’s Bouncy Supermodel Blowout Is Volumemaxxing at Its Finest

The star’s red-carpet beauty look at the Time100 Next Gala was effortlessly glam

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

When it comes to her red-carpet beauty styling, Love Story star Sarah Pidgeon has seamlessly embodied the art of easy romance, often wearing loose, undone waves with radiant skin and pared-back makeup. But at last night’s Time100 Next Gala in New York City, there was a notable shift in the actress’s hair look in favor of bouncy, bountiful volume.

Pidgeon’s ’90s supermodel-inspired look was crafted by celebrity makeup artist Charlie Riddle and hairstylist DJ Quintero using Living Proof essentials. “I love a good blowout, and this look is all about volume, movement, and that effortless, sexy hair that never goes out of style,” he says in a statement. “Think soft, touchable layers, lots of body, and a little bit of that '90s supermodel energy.”

Quintero laid the foundation for sky-high volume by applying Living Proof’s Full Texturizing Foam to the star’s damp, towel-dried hair. He next used the brand’s Full Volume & Root-Lifting Spray near Pidgeon’s scalp, focusing on the crown and around her face for enhanced lift. “Using a round brush and blow-dryer, I worked in sections, lifting the hair up and away from the scalp,” the stylist explains. “I like to really overdirect the hair at the crown to create that beautiful, airy volume.”

Once the hair was completely dry, he reached for a large-barrel curling iron to create the shape of Pidgeon’s springy curls, adding soft bends throughout the mid-length and ends of her hair, alternating directions, and keeping the sections around her face curled away from it. To ensure the actress’s style lasted all night, Quintero used several pin curls to lock the look in place and allowed them to cool before brushing them out for a soft, bouncy finish.

After gently brushing through Pidgeon’s curls, he used Living Proof’s No Frizz Invisible Oil to tame hair flyaways and frizz while boosting its shine. “I love this part because it's where the hair really comes to life,” he explains. “You get that gorgeous, full shape with soft, flowing layers.” Quintero applied the Style Lab Flex Hairspray as a finishing touch for lasting hold, along with its Full Dry Volume & Texture Spray throughout the crown and layers of the star’s hair for extra volume. “The result is big, beautiful, bouncy hair that feels soft, looks healthy, and moves effortlessly,” he adds.

#6922. Van Cleef & Arpels Announces Support for Montecito’s Lotusland

The jewelry house heads to Santa Barbara with a fitting gift from a brand captivated by botanic motifs.

Above: The Rose Garden Terrace at Lotusland in Montecito, CA.

Ganna Walska was a lifelong collector. First, there were the husbands: six of them, from a Russian baron to a yoga instructor. Then came the jewelry: hundreds of carats of emeralds, diamonds, and sapphires. Finally, there were plants, her love of which ultimately led her to create a 37-acre botanical garden in Montecito, California.

Walska, an opera singer born Hanna Puacz in Poland in 1887, purchased the Cuesta Linda estate in 1941. She intended to both live there and house Tibetan monks, but after her final divorce (and after the monks failed to show), Walska devoted herself to collecting rare plants. She renamed the property Lotusland, for the Indian lotus in one of the ponds, and poured her fortune into the project. In 1971 she auctioned off many of her jewels to fund her gardens. One piece was the Oiseau clip from Van Cleef & Arpels, a special order depicting a bird in flight carrying a 96.62-carat yellow diamond known as the “Walska Briolette.”

Following her death, the property was opened to the public, and now Van Cleef is making a return. The maison has announced its support of Lotusland, focusing in particular on the Rose Garden Terrace. It’s a gift that honors both Walska’s love of the brand and the inspiration that Van Cleef has pulled from nature for a century, from countless floral variations to the butterfly motif. Jewels will once again endow Lotusland—just as Ganna would have wanted.

She previously held positions at The Information, covering technology and culture, and Town & Country, writing about news, entertainment, and fashion. #6921. How One Mistake Unleashed a Horde of Night of the Living Dead Bootlegs

A simple copyright error allowed George A. Romero’s zombie classic to enter the public domain. For years, the filmmakers fought to get the rights back.

would you rather accidentally set your arm on fire with spilled gasoline or spend decades grappling with a paperwork mistake? Gary Streiner, who personally experienced both while making 1968’s Night of the Living Dead, says there’s no contest: “Oh, believe me, I would take the burn again.”

Streiner was the sound man on George A. Romero’s classic indie zombie flick, but since everyone on the makeshift Pittsburgh project did multiple jobs, he also helped create visual effects, like the fireball on a porch chair that would later engulf his limb. His brother, Russ Streiner, was another multitasker, playing the taunting brother Johnny in the movie’s opening, and serving as jack-of-all-trades co-producer. Both were loyal Romero confidantes who helped him found Image Ten, the production company that created the apocalyptic undead tale.

So both were among those bitten in the ass when Night of the Living Dead accidentally ended up in the public domain due to a copyright mistake that was anything but simple to fix. Even today, it hasn’t entirely been resolved, which is why there is a right way and a wrong way to watch the movie (at least, if you care about seeing it in the best possible format, while also not picking the pocket of the people who made it.)

Today, Romero is gone, and his debut feature is considered an all-time American classic. Stephen King himself calls it the scariest movie he’s ever seen. But the independent film garnered such success partly because it was so widely and freely shared. The friends and colleagues who helped Romero make Night of the Living Dead have finally figured out a way to pull the movie back into their legal possession. But the freebie versions are still out there, so they are hoping fans will be honest and honorable about the version of the film they choose to play this Halloween.

Don’t worry, they aren’t “coming to get you, Barbara,” as Russ intones in the iconic opening of the movie. They are just, once again, asking nicely.

Basically, look for the Criterion Collection logo. The beloved cinema preservation group helped the Streiners restore the original black-and-white negative for the Museum of Modern Art, and by refining the moody imagery as Romero originally intended, they technically created a “new” work that Image Ten could officially claim as their own. You can find it on the Criterion streaming service, but also HBO Max is among those that license their version of the movie.

“All of it’s licensed now. Now we get paid for it. That’s the point, every time it’s screened on any of those platforms,” Gary says.

“There are other streaming channels that aren’t,” adds Russ. If you don’t spot that big, silver C when Night of the Living Dead starts playing, you’re probably watching a bootleg.

The average joe might not care much whether these aging producers or Romero’s estate get their share, but keep in mind that these were average joes too, not Hollywood big shots. They were working stiffs who had a dream, made something immortal (in many senses of the word), and then got stiffed for real.

“To be honest with you,” Russ begins. “George—and I want to measure my words pretty carefully …”

“He was not a great businessman,” Gary cuts in, not measuring his words at all.

“None of us were great businessmen,” Russ adds with a sigh.

The Streiner brothers were toiling away on local Western Pennsylvania commercials when they helped Romero bring his feature film dream together, and their collective work inspired generations of other nobodies to max out their credit cards, load up on cameras and lights, and make a crazy movie with their friends. “It’s fair to say that without George A. Romero, I would not have the career I have now,” Shaun of the Dead and Hot Fuzz filmmaker Edgar Wright wrote when Romero died in July 2017. “A lot of people owe George a huge debt of gratitude for the inspiration. I am just one of many.”

It’s easy enough to find the right version of Night of the Living Dead, and it happens to be the one that looks the best too. Many of the bootlegs are copies of copies of copies. When the Streiners got MoMA and Criterion to revive a new version of the movie, they went back to the original negatives, part of which they possessed, and some of which belonged to Romero’s cowriter. “We gathered up all of the elements from Russ’s basement and John Russo’s garage, and we trekked out to their facility, and we were all quite amazed at the good condition that everything was still in, not having been kept in absolute ideal conditions,” Gary says.

Now it looks as good as it did when the very first prints were sent out into the world. That also happened to be the moment everything went wrong.

You know how old movies have a noticeable copyright tag in their opening credits, usually right under the title? Today, that’s an affectation, used by filmmakers like Quentin Tarantino and Rian Johnson to conjure a feeling of nostalgia. But once upon a time in real Hollywood, that was actually a legal requirement to establish ownership of a film.

Romero and his team understood this, and had it on their initial cut of the movie. The mix-up happened when they were negotiating to get the zombie picture into theaters and struck an agreement with the indie distribution company the Walter Reade Organization. “That was the beginning of our nightmare,” Russ says. “The movie at that time was called Night of the Flesh-Eaters. They didn’t like the title. They said, ‘We’ve got to come up with something new.’ ”

The executives had a point: Night of the Living Dead has a certain ring to it, so Romero and the Streiners agreed. They left the print in Walter Reade’s hands. “They were in charge of changing the title, but when the title got changed, the copyright was left off,” Russ says. “It became their responsibility to deal with their lab, and the copyright notice for Night of the Living Dead went by the wayside.”

No one realized the mistake until the prints were shipped out, and by that point it was too late. “Not that ownership was ever denied,” Gary says. “There’s never been any, ‘Well, no, you didn’t make Night of the Living Dead.’ We made it.”

Under the United States’ 1909 copyright law, this technical mistake just meant no one was legally obligated to account for tickets or pay them a portion of the sale. “That was the start of it, and we’ve been spending tons of money legally to get this thing on its right feet,” Gary adds.

If that seems both unbelievable and unfair, then welcome to the copyright law revision of the mid-1970s that eliminated this requirement. “It’s totally different now,” says Russ. “As a matter of fact, Night of the Living Dead was one of the reasons that the language for the copyright office changed, because it was preposterous.”

But it was too late for the zombie film and the team behind Image Ten, until the new restoration came about from MoMA and the Criterion Collection. The Streiners learned this lesson the hard way, but they were used to that.

Who could have guessed that a simple line of text could cause so much trouble? Well … who could have guessed that when you pour gasoline on a chair for a zombie attack scene that it could somehow also go wrong.

“George didn’t like the way it was looking, and it took about a half hour for him to reset and get the shot that he wanted,” Russ recalls of the infamous incident. “So the chair was put out, and when it came time to do another take in the new camera position, I had been all over that thing diligently making sure that there were no embers or whatever.”

He didn’t think about what was below the surface. “There was no fire on the chair itself, but the interior workings of the chair, the springs and the metal framework, were still hot,” Russ adds. “And when Gary started dousing it with gasoline—BOOM!—it was like an explosion that set him on fire.”

“I’m literally pouring gasoline out of a plastic jug, and the flame shot right up,” Gary says, mimicking the motion.

Spilled gas on his sleeve meant his whole arm was engulfed. “Fortunately, Bill Hinzman, who was a cemetery ghoul, was actually a volunteer fireman, and he had enough common sense to just tackle me and roll me to the ground,” Gary says.

It was over in seconds, with only relatively minor burns that eventually healed. The copyright conflagration took a lot longer to resolve, but now, finally, they don’t feel so scorched by it anymore.

#6918. Pamela Anderson’s Cranberry Nails Are the Most Elegant Fall Manicure: How to Recreate

Not only is the shape practical, but the color is timeless.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

I said it before, and I’ll say it again: Pamela Anderson has become one of the most unexpected beauty muses. From her pared-back approach to makeup to her ever-changing hairstyles, the actor has spent recent years setting trends that span generations. And her latest manicure is making a rather convincing case for swapping your usual autumnal burgundy for something a little juicier.

Taking to Instagram, Anderson recently shared a video reel documenting her recent evening spent gardening, reading, and simply living her best life. "Fall is the season of the soul… 🤍🍂," she wrote to caption the post. While the wholesome content had me questioning my own screen-time report (spoiler: it’s an unhealthy amount), it was her nails that left a lasting impression on the beauty editor in me. And she’s been rocking a versatile fall hue everywhere from her backyard to the CFDA Awards.

A far cry from her signature French manicure, Anderson debuted a glossy, cranberry-red shade painted onto short, squoval-shaped nails. Not only is the shape practical and universally flattering, but the color is timeless and an autumn manicure classic.

To recreate Pamela Anderson’s cranberry squoval nails, you’re going to want to invest some time into the prep. I’m talking trimming your cuticles, filing your nails down, and rounding the corners off for that signature square-meets-oval shape.

Then comes the polish. A bright-toned red like essie’s Forever Yummy or OPI’s Big Apple Red is going to be your best bet. Finish with a generous helping of a high-shine top coat for that freshly picked, almost wet-gloss look, and you’re good to go!

Who knew a trip down the fruit and vegetable aisle could double as a nail appointment? I’ll definitely be channeling the duality of Anderson both gardening and attending a chic red carpet with this fall-ready manicure.

#6914. Is the Fitbit Edge Worth Buying?

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The future form of the wearable tech is here.

It’s been a pretty big year for Fitbit. In May, the Fitbit Air joined the ever-growing list of no-screen fitness trackers alongside Whoop, and now, not even six months later, I have on my wrist the brand-new Fitbit Edge. It is, as the name suggests, a slimmer fitness tracker. It feels slightly like a step forward, slightly like a step back—a return to form. Let’s say. It’s new, but it’s also a throwback to the slim shape of the (not so) old Fitbit devices like the Charge and Inspire. Importantly, despite being Google’s latest wearable with a screen, it is not trying to be a Pixel Watch. Far from it. Some might complain about what it can’t do, but I’d argue the pared-back nature of the Fitbit Edge is its greatest strength.

At its core, this is the state-of-the-art Fitbit bringing a smooth, responsive modern touchscreen experience to a wearable focused on health tracking. It certainly does more than count your steps, monitor heart rate, and detect REM cycles; the Edge has plenty of smartwatch capabilities. Control your music, check the weather, even read and respond (depending on the phone you have) to messages. But the standard inundation of notifications is replaced by a clean interface that clearly does not like being disturbed. This is the casual fitness tracker for those who value a distraction-free workout.

First things first. I’ve been wearing mine 24/7 for the past couple of weeks, and it’s nothing if not supremely comfortable. Bands are simpler to attach and detach than any other watch I’ve tried, and the new magnet bands are easy to make snug.

From the get-go, the band selection has been excellent. My personal favorite is the magnetic silicone band in Mahogany. It’s got a look that comes off as more upscale than the materials described typically do. Paired with the slim bezel and overall slight shape, it’s a handsome wearable that draws a lot of compliments. I won’t repeat what one friend of mine said, but I’m pretty sure it means she liked it.

The completely revamped Fitbit interface is intuitive and has everything you need just a swipe or tap away by default. You can tinker with the home screen, but having heart rate and total steps, alongside the time and a quick workout start button, displayed by default feels instructional. Everything is readable and I genuinely feel like my mother-in-law could understand how to use it. Like I said, it’s not a Pixel Watch, but it’s certainly taking usability and design cues from Google’s other line of wearables.

As a Fitbit, nearly everything about the Edge is an evolution. The only problem is I’m also comparing it to all the smartwatches out on the market too. I can use literally any other smartwatch in my rotation to tap into my office building with a digital badge, and pay for lunch with Apple Pay or whatever equivalent. With the Edge, no luck on any of these NFC-powered interactions. And because I’m using it with my iPhone, I can read my incoming messages but can’t respond to anything.

On paper, these are flaws, but in reality they allow me to interact with my fitness tracker without getting too distracted. The lack of customizability compared to an Apple or Pixel Watch simply equals less time sitting around and setting up your watch, and hopefully more time going outside and living your life. Which isn’t to say I wasn’t bugged by some of the limitations. The viewable data is not quite as in-depth as some others, and looking at your phone to check your reps sorta ruins the whole distraction-free thing.

In that way, it’s more like a fitness band than a smartwatch. It can track a lot of data on its own, but ultimately the only way to see all of that is to eventually open your phone. For me, that trade-off is worth it to not be getting constant buzzes on my wrist every time I go for a run.

When it comes to fitness tracking, the hardcore still have Garmin. For something more casual, the Fitbit Edge has a great look, and as an Apple Watch user, certain aspects of the simplicity are genuinely refreshing.

#6910. Patrick Stewart Remembered as a Colossus of Stage and Screen

“He wore his heart on his sleeve, which made him a man to rely on, to admire and in my case, love,” Ian McKellen shared following the actor’s death.

Actor, writer, and activist Sir Patrick Stewart died on Wednesday. He was 86. In a statement issued via his social media on October 29, his family wrote that the Star Trek star and renowned Shakespearean thespian left us following “a brief and poignant journey” with Alzheimer’s disease and Lewy body dementia. “He will be remembered for the dignity, compassion, and humanity he brought to every role—in his art and life,” the statement read.

Since the news surfaced, an outpouring of grief rippled from his closest colleagues and collaborators. Notable among them, Sir Ian McKellen, a true friend and screen partner of Stewart’s for over 50 years across stage and film, wrote an emotional tribute to Stewart over social media.

“Before everything else, Patrick was defined by being born and bred in Yorkshire, the proud county in the north of England which he loved to the end,” McKellen wrote. “He wore his heart on his sleeve, which made him a man to rely on, to admire and in my case, love.”

In 1987, Stewart was already a renowned stage actor in the UK with a handful of credits in movies like Excalibur and David Lynch’s Dune when he landed his star-making role: Captain Jean-Luc Picard in Star Trek: The Next Generation. The series ran until 1994 and made Stewart a household name. Then, he starred in the summer blockbuster X-Men (2000), playing mentor Charles Xavier with McKellen opposite him as Xavier’s once-close friend turned philosophical antagonist, Magneto.

Fellow actors who worked with Stewart across Star Trek, Marvel, and beyond also penned tributes and personal anecdotes in memory of Stewart. The Next Generation co-star LeVar Burton wrote in a public statement: “He was family. There is no other context in which to hold him that makes sense. I am lucky to have known and loved so many of the facets of a soul so rich in talent and generous of heart. Of all the dames and knights I’ve known, he was deep down the silliest.”

Hugh Jackman, who starred in several X-Men installments with Stewart—including their acclaimed 2017 hit Logan—wrote on Instagram: “We lost one of the greats of stage and screen today. And, in my book, a tremendous friend and man. I learned so much from him. I will miss you, Patrick Stewart, all of my days.”

Television creator Seth MacFarlane, a noted Trekkie who cast Stewart in a handful of bit parts across Family Guy, American Dad, and his 2012 R-rated comedy Ted, said Stewart was “that rare actor who could take any bit of dialogue and elevate it, making a good writer sound great, and a great writer sound masterful.”

Wrote MacFarlane: “I remember exchanging emails with him a number of years ago when he was beginning day 1 of a new film. He commented to me that he always walked onto a new set feeling like the biggest novice there. My stunned reaction was the same as anyone’s would be. I said, ‘Patrick, if you feel that way, the rest of us are screwed.’ We have lost a legendary actor today, and for many of us, a good friend.”

Dafne Keen, who worked with Stewart extensively in Logan as a child actress, remembered the late performer on her Instagram: “He was such a kind, genuine, funny, unique, caring and charming man. I remember his endless jokes, nurturing nature and colourful reading glasses. Every single day spent working by his side was an honour and a privilege I can’t quite believe I had. He was a walking master of his craft, and of life. One of the greatest of our time, on stage and on screen.”

She continued, “I will forever hold those months we spent together close to my heart, and will always think of him as a pivotal part of how I view the world around me.”

Shortly after the news of Stewart’s passing broke, players on Star Trek Online gathered in the game’s social areas to hold a virtual vigil in Stewart’s honor.

Below, you can read more social media remembrances from Stewart’s many colleagues and creative partners, as well as theaters that were lucky enough to have Stewart grace their stages. As Jean-Luc Picard once said, “Make now always the most precious time. Now will never come again.”

#6908. House Party: Angelica Hicks

With a new Paperless Post collection, the illustrator shares her rules for a party worth RSVPing yes to.

Welcome to House Party, where we ask expert hosts for their top entertaining tips, tricks, and tabletop tactics. Come party with us.

Angelica Hicks has a talent for making high fashion out of everyday materials. The British artist, illustrator, and fashion satirist is known for using household supplies to recreate runway and red-carpet looks, capturing couture’s most elaborate details with candy wrappers and duct tape. A sharp eye runs in the family: Her father is the artist and interior designer Ashley Hicks, and her grandfather was the influential decorator David Hicks. But her visual wit is distinctly her own, evident in everything from her illustrative book Tongue in Chic to her collaborations with Gucci.

Her newly launched invitation collection for Paperless Post brings that humor to entertaining, with designs including a shrimp holding a martini. Hicks’s own hosting philosophy is similarly spirited, though she’s quite serious about a well-considered seating chart. For House Party, we asked her about the best parties she’s attended, the merits of an emergency ice delivery, and the extravagance she’d happily import from Japan.

ELLE Decor: What are your three essentials when hosting?

Angelica Hicks: A great invite that sets the tone before the event. A shrimp holding a martini inviting you to a cocktail party perhaps?

What’s your top entertaining/hosting tip?

People love to complain about placement, but I am pro placement. I think telling people where to sit, and actually spending time considering it, will only benefit the flow of conversation—especially when mixing people who might not already know each other. I think it’s a fabulous thing to do when hosting a dinner party.

Who, dead or alive, is coming to your dream dinner party?

Noël Coward, Joan Crawford, Aretha Franklin, Cristóbal Balenciaga.

Announcing dietary restrictions upon arrival.

If you could add one exorbitantly extravagant component to a party, what would it be?

I think I would fly in urchin from Japan if it were the season.

Who threw the best party you’ve ever attended?

Unemployed Magazine always throws an excellent Halloween party. What makes it so great is that they select one iconic person as a theme and everyone dresses up as that person or someone in the universe. Last year was Liz Taylor, and the year before was Princess Diana.

I'm ashamed to say I rarely bring anything. But once I have arrived, if I notice there is something missing, I will order it, which I think is far more helpful. A bag of ice can come in much more handy than a mid bottle of red.

What historic party would you have liked to have attended?

I think I would have had great fun dressing up for the Rothschild Surrealist Ball.

Buy too much wine; you'll probably go through it.

I don’t have a dishwasher, so I always lament the washing up.

Julia Cancilla is the social media & news editor at ELLE Decor, where she oversees the brand's socials and covers design, pop culture, and emerging trends. She also authors the monthly ELLE Decoroscope column. Her work has appeared in Inked magazine, House Beautiful, Marie Claire, and more.

#6902. For His 40th Birthday, Drake Flexed a Custom Rolex and a $250,000 Watch in the Space of 24 Hours

The rapper marked his milestone birthday with two extravagant timepieces, including a one-of-a-kind Daytona worth millions.

Turning 40 is a milestone worth celebrating in style. And to nobody's surprise, Drake did just that.

As you'd expect, the Canadian rapper's birthday outfit was not exactly understated. In fact, his watch rotation featured not one, but two rather extraordinary Rolexes. Why settle for one when you can have two?

The first was a custom, sapphire-covered Daytona, while the second was a colorful Day-Date 36 with a reported six-figure price tag. There are certainly worse ways to welcome a new decade.

First up was the Rolex Cosmograph Daytona, a watch that makes even the most extravagant diamond-set timepieces look positively modest. The one-of-a-kind creation features a diamond-pavé dial, brilliant blue sapphire hour markers, and a bezel set with baguette-cut blue sapphires. But the real showstopper is the bracelet, which is covered in a gradient of blue sapphires that gradually fade into diamonds.

And if all those precious stones weren't enough to make your eyes water, the estimated price tag certainly might. The one-of-a-kind creation has reportedly been valued at anywhere between $2 million and $5 million. Although, given that Rolex made just one, it's difficult to put an exact figure on it.

While you might assume the rapper had commissioned the watch especially for his big birthday, he actually debuted it back in August. Still, when you've got something quite this spectacular sitting in your watch collection, it would be a shame not to give it another outing.

The second Rolex was a rather different kind of extravagance. Drake also showed off a rose-gold Day-Date 36 featuring the brand's famously playful puzzle dial. Instead of traditional hour markers, baguette-cut sapphires surround a dial made up of brightly colored jigsaw pieces. And rather than displaying the usual day and date, the apertures reveal inspirational words and emojis, including a little red heart. It's about as far from your grandfather's Rolex as you can get.

The watch has been valued at around $250,000 which, rather remarkably, makes it the considerably cheaper of Drake's two birthday watches.

Of course, Drake has never been one for doing things by halves, particularly when it comes to watches. And while most people would be more than happy to receive a single Rolex for their 40th, it seems the rapper couldn't quite settle on one.

#6900. The Vintage Features That EVERY Homebuyer Wants Right Now, According to Experts

These are the authentic details that make a home extra special.

Pictured above: Designer Annie Kern showcased breathtaking stained glass windows throughout this historic home in Kansas City, Missouri.

I grew up in a family captivated—if not downright obsessed—by homes with character. My parents always took the long way home, winding slowly through our town's historic neighborhoods to admire the details on every house: immaculate stone walls, whimsical wrought-iron gates, ivy-covered façades, and shingles weathered to silvery perfection. Those drives became a kind of education, and each architectural flourish inspired the home I grew up in.

In recent decades, though, the real estate market has been flooded with sleek new builds and smart-home conveniences, which in turn have dimmed the prominence of intricate vintage details. Even worse, the structures often look incongruous with their surroundings. Prefab townhomes now rise from former parking lots, farmhouse-style mansions perch improbably on California sand, and shiny concrete slabs have replaced weathered shingles deep in the New England woods.

But, as with all trends, we’ve begun to circle back. Many experts are now seeing that buyers are rediscovering the charm of craftsmanship and are looking to prioritize authentic vintage features that just can't be faked. Ahead, we spoke with several pros about the special home details their clients are falling for—and what it takes to score your own home ripe with vintage appeal in a competitive market.

A renewed appreciation for period details—think original windows, layered molding, and aged floors—is something Peter Michael, founder of Piere Michel Brokerage, is witnessing firsthand.

“People are drawn to homes that feel lived in and loved,” he says. “Original craftsmanship is what this new wave of buyers is really looking for. It starts with the arches in the hallways to the exposed brick fireplaces—the list can go on and on.”

After years of streamlined interiors and minimalist façades, buyers are once again searching for texture, depth, and evidence of the human hand. “Original hardwood floors, high ceilings, stained glass, exposed brick, and vintage light fixtures are usually the things that pop out,” Michael adds. “They make a space feel rooted, not manufactured.”

That nostalgia isn’t confined to historic properties. Jackie Mosher, cofounder and COO of Dzinly, says even new builds are embracing time-worn charm through exterior details that nod to the past.

“I’m seeing a growing number of new homes incorporating decorative eaves and exposed rafters,” she says. “These details, once hallmarks of early Craftsman homes, are being reinterpreted today on Modern and Transitional houses. They add depth and visible craftsmanship to otherwise simple designs—bridging the gap between timeless tradition and contemporary simplicity.”

Mosher notes that stucco, once reserved for homes in warmer climates, is reemerging in cooler regions. “It’s fascinating to see homeowners in the Midwest and Northeast choosing stucco—it requires more effort to install because of the temperature fluctuations, but people are willing to make that investment for the aesthetic,” she explains. And while the material palette may draw from the past, the execution is crisp: thinner window frames, smooth finishes, and clean horizontal lines inspired by midcentury design.

The goal, Mosher says, isn’t replication—it’s reinterpretation. “People aren’t trying to recreate the past, but to blend nostalgic details with modern forms,” she explains. “That’s what makes a home feel truly timeless.”

Of course, today’s buyers want the best of both worlds. “They want the vintage look, but they can’t live without modern technology,” Michael says. “A smart thermostat is almost a must, along with stainless steel appliances. It’s a blend of things.”

That blend extends to floor plans as well. While classic Tudors, Craftsman bungalows, and brownstones are perpetually in demand, interior layouts are evolving to suit modern lifestyles. “Popcorn ceilings and small, isolated kitchens are becoming a no-no,” Michael says. “Buyers love a good open layout and want a clean-cut look with a mixture of craftsmanship.”

It’s that mixture—the contrast of the hand-hewn with the high-tech—that defines the moment.

Mosher believes the secret to timeless appeal lies in restraint. “For a home to feel enduring, the design shouldn’t lean too heavily on trends or overly bold choices,” she says. “Muted, nature-based tones—warm whites, taupes, and sages—create that sense of heritage. And contrasting the trim with the cladding helps define the home, grounding it in a way that feels classic.”

After decades of neutral sameness, it seems buyers are once again craving personality. They want arches instead of corners, warmth instead of gloss, and craftsmanship that invites you to look twice.

#6899. Why Everyone Is Wearing Suede This Fall—And the Chicest Ways to Style It

When the temperatures drop and fall dressing takes center stage, one fabric always finds its way back into our wardrobes: suede. Suede has been that fall fashion staple ever since it has been around. It brings that cozy, elevated feel to even the simplest outfits, making it the perfect texture to embrace when the seasons change.

While it’s common to see suede generally in the fall, lately it’s absolutely everywhere, and I’m here to remind you that there are so many ways you can wear this texture. From suede mini skirts paired with oversized sweaters, knee-high suede boots, and of course purses.

Suede is such a perfect fall trend because it brings a subtle touch of texture to any outfit. Shades like chocolate brown and deep burgundy make the material feel especially autumnal.

Consider this your sign to give your fall wardrobe a suede refresh. Whether you go all in with a statement skirt or keep things simple with a chic suede bag, this is one trend that makes every outfit feel a little more luxurious. And if you need some inspiration, I’ve got you covered with some trendy looks below.

There is something so effortlessly chic about a suede skirt. It’s a fun piece I’d wear to work, but styled like the above makes for such a cute day or night time look. The addition of the colored tights and shoes elevates the whole moment, so if you’re a maximalist this suede look is for you.

The simplest way to tap into the suede trend is with your footwear. There are so many popular suede shoes meant for the fall and winter season and these boots are the perfect ones.

Even though I am not a big hat girl, a suede hat like this is a fun way to test the waters. It’s easy and adds to any fall outfit you’re building.

Another staple way to tap into suede is with accessories, specifically a good purse. This dark brown suede shoulder bag is so stylish and paired with the all-white outfit is a texture combo I’m obsessing over.

Of course, no fall wardrobe is complete without a statement jacket. Suede blazers, like the one above, add instant personality to your everyday looks. They’re the kind of investment pieces that make getting dressed feel exciting, even on the busiest mornings.

#6898. Is Walton Goggins Leaving Fallout?

"Just took it off for the last time" the actor wrote in a cryptic social media post.

Walton Goggins may have just said goodbye to Fallout. Though Prime Video hasn’t made any announcements yet about wrapping production on the upcoming third season, Goggins posted a video to social media on Thursday, October 29, that alludes to the end of his time on the video game adaptation. At least, that’s what we think it means...

“Just took it off for the last time.. Season 3… Gatdamn this felt good,” Goggins captioned a video of himself driving through the California desert, lip-syncing to “Ramble On” by Led Zeppelin. “I wish this road or this song would never end,” he continued. “Gonna miss you California… til the next time. #Fallout.”

While it’s clear that Goggins is finished with season 3, his phrasing implies that he’s wrapped on Fallout entirely. “Just took it off for the last time” sounds like a permanent goodbye for The Ghoul—his gun-slinging character from the Fallout series who is transformed into a post-apocalyptic mutant. His caption could also imply that Fallout season 3 marks the end of the series, though that would be a bit of a stretch considering the show’s sustained popularity and acclaim. Prime Video has yet to comment.

Whatever Goggins means by “the last time,” at least we know now that Fallout season 3 is closer than ever. It’s been a long year since season 2 premiered on Prime Video, with the finale releasing in January earlier this year.

Fans are dying to see what will become of Lucy (Ella Purnell), Maximus (Aaron Moten), and everyone’s favorite noseless bounty hunter. In the season 2 finale, Lucy’s father, Hank (Kyle MacLachlan), triggered a switch that made him lose all memory of himself and his time at Vault-Tec. That couldn’t have come at a worse time, as Caesar’s imposing Legion approaches the ruins of New Vegas. As for Goggins’ Cooper Howard, he’s on his way to Colorado, chasing the slim chance his family may still be alive.

In January, Goggins told Esquire that Cooper is in the pursuit of hope, odds be damned. “What happens [to the Ghoul] is what happens to all of us, which is we hope," he said. "Even before we had language, [we had hope] of finding warmth or shelter or what's on the other side of that rock. That's just who we are as people.”

Let’s hope that Goggins’ time on Fallout isn’t the end. But if it is, he better get the greatest send-off we’ve ever seen on television.

#6896. Zendaya and Law Roach Win Halloween With a Familiar Yellow Lanvin Look Worn by Halle Berry

Zendaya and Law Roach know a thing or two about a fashion reference. Whether diving into tennis motifs for Challengers, finding archival spiderweb gowns for Spider-Man: Brand New Day, or leaning into bridal whites for The Drama, the duo has approached every press tour with an attention to detail and a love for the art of method dressing. But this time, they put their expertise to work not for a premiere, but for Halloween.

While most people take a DIY approach to the holiday, channeling their inner witch or favorite pop star with a mix of thrifted items, hand-sewn projects, and pieces they have in their closets, Zendaya and Roach turned to their industry connections for a familiar yellow set that helped them be as authentic as possible to their reference: Halle Berry at the July 2000 New York City premiere of X-Men.

Photos from the Y2K event saw Berry in a geometric Lanvin set from the Fall 2000 collection, composed of a yellow patterned bikini top and matching low-rise trousers with extra-long legs. In the years since, the look has remained one of the most recognizable looks from the era, and of course, Roach managed to get his hands on it.

Zendaya wore the ensemble with sunglasses and diamond earrings, just like the actor before her did, though left behind her predecessor’s semi-sheer brown button-down shirt and long crystal waist chain. Her tousled pixie cut certainly helped with the resemblance, and she finished things off with simple pointed-toe nude heels and red nails.

#6895. 13 Things You Should Toss ASAP If You’ve Lived in Your Home for More Than 5 Years

You'll feel a wave of relief once you do it.

It may seem like you just moved into your new home or apartment yesterday. But in a flash, five years have passed and you’re out of storage and feeling overwhelmed. Most of us know it doesn’t take long for our belongings to pile up.

“By year five, the ‘endless storage’ honeymoon is over,” says Ann Lightfoot, cofounder, DDH Home Organizing & Move Management. “Your stuff has multiplied, your square footage hasn’t, and you now know how you actually live in your space.”

But now is the ideal time for a thoughtful edit. “Taking stock after five years gives us a chance to see what's actually taking up space, what we're still using, and what we've simply gotten used to storing,” says Laura Kinsella, founder of Urban OrgaNYze. “Our careers, families, routines, and interests can change dramatically, but the things we've accumulated don't automatically change with us.”

Decluttering also enhances good design. “To me, a beautifully designed home isn't about how much you can fit into it. It's about being intentional with what you choose to keep,” says Kerrie Kelly, NKBA, FASID, CEO and creative director of Kerrie Kelly Studio, and NKBA 2026 National Board Chair. “Editing isn't about deprivation; it's about making room for what you actually love and use.”

If you don’t know where to begin, start small. “Edit by category rather than trying to tackle the entire house in a weekend,” says Kelly. “Start with one drawer, one cabinet, or one closet. Take everything out, clean the space, and put back only what deserves to be there. It's amazing how quickly those small decisions transform the way a home feels. Empty space is a luxury, too!”

Ahead, here’s what design and organizational experts say you should get rid of if you’ve been in your home five years or more.

“Five years of conferences, events, fundraisers, and corporate gifting can leave you with an impressive collection of tote bags, reusable water bottles, and branded merchandise you never actually set out to own,” says Kinsella. Keep what you genuinely like and use, and pass along the extras.

Most manuals live online now, and that tangle of chargers likely powers nothing in your house. Recycle them responsibly, says Lightfoot.

“Threadbare towels, mismatched sheets, and extra bedding you never reach for take up valuable storage space,” says Kelly. “Keep a thoughtfully curated collection of beautiful, functional linens and recycle the worn-out ones where possible.”

“If your spin shoes haven't been touched since lockdown and the exercise bike has become ‘the chair’ in your bedroom, you may be dedicating valuable space to a workout routine that's no longer part of your life,” says Kinsella. Sell or donate equipment you no longer use so you have room for the activities you truly enjoy now.

Craft kits, furniture you planned to refinish, the bread maker and so on: “If someday hasn’t arrived in five years, give yourself permission to let it go, guilt-free,” says Lightfoot.

“Those little objects that have been sitting on a shelf for years can become visual clutter,” says Kelly. “I love a meaningful collection, but every piece should earn its place. Sometimes removing three things makes the one beautiful object remaining look even better.”

“That rug or wall art you swore would work ‘somewhere’ has spent five years leaning against a wall. It’s time to release it to a home where it fits,” says Lightfoot.

Hang on to a small labeled paint sample for touch-ups—and maybe one tile. But the rest, including the dried-up products or the five half-empty cans in the garage or basement, can go to your town’s hazardous waste drop-off, says Lightfoot.

This is a no-judgment zone: “If the avocado slicer, specialty pan, or oversized appliance hasn't made an appearance in years, it probably doesn't deserve prime real estate in your kitchen,” says Kelly. Ditto for duplicate kitchen tools.

“Whether it's a juicer, red light mask, or massage tool, we've all had that new wellness routine we're completely committed to for a few weeks before life gets busy and the habit quietly falls away,” says Kinsella. “The problem is that the gadget doesn't disappear when the enthusiasm does, and five years later, you may have an entire cabinet devoted to routines that lasted a month.” It’s fine to try something new to learn it wasn't for you, but you don't have to keep storing the equipment forever.

Maybe it's an extra accent chair that collects clothes or a side table that's always in the way. Just because something fits physically doesn't mean it belongs. Give your rooms space to breathe and your furniture room to shine, says Kelly.

Purge the old sunscreen, crusty nail polish, expired makeup, shampoo you don’t like, and half-used cleaners that have piled up in your bathroom. Toss them safely and enjoy the instant breathing room, says Lightfoot.

“If you've lived in your home for five years and still have boxes you never unpacked, it's a pretty good indication that you haven't needed most of what's inside on a regular basis,” says Kinsella. “Open the box, decide what's worth keeping, and find those belongings a proper home so you're not continuing to live around a move that’s already happened.”

#6891. Winter Date Ideas to Heat Up Your Love Life This Season

Time to turn those dark, cold nights into hot date nights.

I’m afraid the rumors are true: Winter is, in fact, upon us. But while the colder weather may find you ready to cuddle up with takeout and TV ‘til spring, let this be your reminder that winter does not mean you have to put your dating life on hold. While this may not be the sexiest time of year, it is the coziest—which means that the cooler months ahead are filled with romantic date-night potential. To help you make the most of that potential, allow me to present this list of the best winter date ideas for you and whoever you’re cuddling up to this season.

Whether you’re already happily cuffed and committed, single and seeking, or feeling things out with a new crush, there’s something on this list for every kind of winter date vibe. Prepare to fill your cold-weather calendar with these 18 winter date ideas.

What better way to heat up your winter nights than an evening of laughs with the one you love? And by the way, you don’t have to shell out for a Netflix Special taping (although if you can swing it, by all means). Tons of venues in cities across the country regularly host affordable standup shows where you can grab some food or a drink and enjoy a chill night filled with plenty of laughs.

To be clear, you can support the arts any time. But there’s just something a little fancy-yet-cozy about winter ballet.

In a similar if much sexier vein, nothing beats a burlesque date night. If you’re New York–based (or have an NYC trip coming up this holiday season) I literally can’t recommend Company XIV’s Nutcracker Rouge highly enough. Get tickets here!

Can you technically see a play any time of year? Yes, of course. But you can’t tell me there’s not something about “going to the theater” that just seems to ooze a certain wintery sophistication. Whether it’s a Broadway show or a more intimate performance at your local theater, catching some live entertainment is a perfect way to spend a winter night out on the town.

If you’re down to class things up a bit this winter, nothing will make you feel more sexy and sophisticated than a jazz club date night—just you and your date, sipping cocktails and enjoying some live tunes.

If you and your person are already seasoned slope-hitters, then a ski trip is a given. But if you don’t know your way around the slopes yet, why not take a lesson together? It’s a great opportunity to learn something new, get outside, and make the most of the cold weather—and it comes with a built-in excuse to warm up with a steaming mug of hot chocolate (or your après-ski beverage of choice) afterwards.

Sometimes an intimate evening with a home-cooked meal you prepare together is actually more romantic than dining out. Pick a recipe you both want to try, open a bottle of wine, and channel your inner chef.

Plenty of companies now offer virtual wine tastings you can do from the comfort of your own home. They send the wine to you, and you and your date can follow along as an expert guides you through the various selections in a Zoom-style seminar. What better way to spend a night in than exploring new wines together and refining your sommelier sensibilities?

Alexa, play “Christmas Tree Farm” by Taylor Swift. Whether or not you actually want to perform the manual labor of cutting down a live tree, lugging it home, and setting it up when you could just order a fake from Amazon, spending a cozy day at a Christmas tree farm is an iconic holi-date. It’s outdoorsy without being too physically demanding, it’s brimming with cute selfie potential, and you can basically pretend you’re in the cheesy-yet-addictive holiday rom-com of your dreams. What more could you ask for?

Get into the holiday spirit and cross some seasonal chores off your to-do list by turning holiday decorating into a cute date night. Best of all: You get to stay inside and keep your comfy clothes on.

This one may sound a little less fun, but hear me out. Un-decorating is sad and boring and work—but turning it into date night can make an otherwise tedious chore actually kind of fun! Grab some wine, throw on a movie or set the mood with some music, and un-deck the halls.

While some of your summertime haunts may be closed for the season, plenty of typically summery destinations are actually open year-round. Not to mention, a lot of them are probably less expensive and less crowded during the off-season. Plan a trip to your favorite beach town or seaside locale and see what it’s like in the winter. Chances are it’s probably a lot more peaceful.

Spending a night together in a hotel is a great way to get out of the house and treat yourselves to a romantic mini-getaway. Some hotels even offer special holiday/winter packages for the season.

Nothing says cuffing season like meeting the parents! But even if you’re not ready to take that step, a little trip to your hometown to show your crush all the old haunts is a great way to spend a winter weekend and indulge in a little nostalgia.

Sometimes it can be tough to work up the motivation to actually get up and go out in the winter. It’s dark, it’s cold, your seasonal depression is acting up, etc. But that’s exactly why you deserve to treat yourselves to a luxe night out. You know that fancy restaurant you’ve been saving for a special occasion? Screw the special occasion. Go now, for no reason other than you can.

What better way to unwind and beat the winter blues than a spa date? Treat yourselves to a couple’s massage, a relaxing sauna steam, and/or some beauty treatments that your dry winter skin will definitely thank you for.

Lots of bars offer mixology classes where you and your date can not only enjoy some delicious seasonal cocktails but also learn to make them yourselves. If you’d rather not leave the house, there are also virtual options you can attend online from the comfort of your own home.

Going to the movies is a lost art. Catch a new release (Wuthering Heights, anyone?) and enjoy your date’s company while snacking on popcorn, candy, and slurpees

#6889. It’s Not Too Late to Plant These 5 Perennials for a Gorgeous Fall Garden

Add cheer to gray days with these late-season planting picks.

Consistent wind and rain might not exactly be “gardening weather,” but you can still add color to your garden this autumn. If you have a bare patch to fill, some established perennials can be planted now—as long as the ground isn't waterlogged or frozen.

As autumn deepens and the last of the summer-flowering plants begin to fade, our gardens can start to look a little tired. Add the persistent drizzle and gray skies, and it's easy to feel that the color has gone for the year.

The good news is that there's still time to brighten things up. Established autumn-flowering perennials are a quick and reliable way to bring color and seasonal interest to your garden, and a mild, wet spell can help newly planted roots settle in.

The key is to check the conditions first: avoid planting directly into waterlogged soil, which can suffocate plants, or when the ground is frozen. If the soil is too wet to work, choose pots with good drainage and keep them somewhere sheltered from strong winds.

As Charlotte McGrattan, hardy nursery stock buyer at Hillier Garden Centers, adds, newly planted perennials “require watering well while they establish.” Rain can help with that, but check the soil rather than assuming your plants have had enough: containers, in particular, may still need watering.

Here, McGrattan shares five autumn-flowering perennials you can plant now.

Heleniums are tough, hardy, and easy to grow. If your soil is very wet or compacted, it's best to plant them in a pot positioned in a sheltered spot away from the wind.

“Heleniums add an abundance of color, bursting from mid-summer through into autumn. There are hundreds of varieties to choose from, from hot yellows and reds to vibrant oranges,” McGrattan says.

In these mild temperatures, it's also not too late to plant heliopsis, also known as “false sunflower.” “They not only make impressive, colorful features in borders and beds, attracting an array of pollinators but can also make for lovely cut flowers,” says McGrattan.

“They grow in clumps with branching stems that give a bushy habit, and the flowering period is between six and eight weeks. To encourage buds to form, deadhead any spent flowers.”

While spring is generally the best time to plant penstemons—which are available in shades of purple, pink, white, and blue—you can still plant them in pots now.

“With some varieties being bi-colors combining two shades, they bring welcome color to the garden,” says McGrattan. “Penstemon thrives in well-drained soil and is very tolerant to drought conditions.”

Crocosmias are a great way to bring both height and vibrant color to your autumn borders, as long as you plant established plants with roots instead of growing them from corms (bulbs). “Originating from South Africa, they require fertile, moist, but well-drained soil,” McGrattan adds.

You can also still plant potted Japanese anemones in late autumn, provided the ground is neither frozen nor waterlogged. “Japanese anemones boast cup-shaped pink or white flowers on the top of tall stems and can spread rapidly, so can be a great means of filling borders,” says McGrattan.

#6888. All the Best Celebrity Halloween Costumes of 2026

From Sabrina Carpenter as Hairspray’s Penny to Kendall Jenner’s Paris, Texas moment

Another Halloween means another round of celebrity costumes. Every year, actors, artists, and public figures go all out for the holiday, sparing no expense and taking no shortcuts to see their vision through.
